# Master Hua’s <term>Classic of the Central Treasury<note>original: "華氏中藏經" (Huáshì Zhōngzàng Jīng). This work is a foundational text in traditional Chinese medicine. While attributed to the legendary 2nd-century physician Hua Tuo—known for his pioneering work in surgery and anesthesia—most historians believe it was compiled by later scholars to preserve his medical theories.</note></term>

<summary>This is the title page of a classic Chinese medical treatise attributed to the legendary physician Hua Tuo. The text focuses on internal medicine and the diagnostic importance of the human "treasury," or the internal organ systems.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, Zhongzang Jing, traditional Chinese medicine, internal medicine, medical classics</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal preface to the work, establishing the legendary origins of the medical knowledge contained within. It begins with the lineage and character of the famed physician Hua Tuo.</meta>

# Preface to Master Hua’s <term>Classic of the Central Treasury<note>original: "華氏中藏經" (Huáshì Zhōngzàng Jīng). This text is traditionally attributed to the Han dynasty physician Hua Tuo, though scholars suggest it was compiled later. The "Central Treasury" refers to the internal organs of the human body.</note></term>

->Written by Deng Chuzhong of Mount Shaoshi, the <term>Perfected One<note>A title for a Daoist adept or immortal who has achieved a high state of spiritual realization.</note></term> of Penetrating Subtleties and Master of Yingling Grotto<-

Master Hua, whose taboo name was Tuo and courtesy name was Yuanhua, was by nature tranquil and detached from worldly ambitions. He delighted in the study of <term>medical recipes<note>original: "方書" (fāngshū). Specifically, books containing medicinal formulas and occult or technical methods.</note></term> and frequently wandered through famous mountains and secluded caves, where he often experienced remarkable encounters.

One day, while resting after drinking wine in front of an ancient grotto on Mount Gongyi, he suddenly heard voices discussing methods for curing diseases. Surprised by this strange occurrence, the Master crept closer to the cave to listen in secret. After a short while, someone inside said, “Master Hua is close by; these arts could be entrusted to him.” 

But another responded, “Tuo is greedy by nature and has no pity for living creatures. How could we possibly grant them to him?” 

Hearing this, the Master was even more shocked. He leaped into the cave and saw two elderly men dressed in bark with crowns made of grass. The Master bowed deeply before them and said, “I happened to hear you wise ones discussing the medical arts, and so I forgot to return home. Moreover, the path of saving others is what I have always cherished; my only regret is that I have not yet encountered <insert>the true way</insert>.”

<summary>This preface recounts a legendary encounter where the physician Hua Tuo eavesdrops on two mysterious elders in a mountain cave discussing medical secrets. The story serves to frame the medical text as a gift of divine or hidden wisdom, emphasizing the moral requirements for a physician.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, Deng Chuzhong, medical ethics, Daoism, Mount Gongyi, medical origins, ancient medicine</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the legendary account of Hua Tuo’s encounter with two mysterious elders in a mountain cave, detailing the moral conditions they set for granting him their secret medical knowledge and the dramatic conclusion of their meeting.</meta>

“...vations are profound, yet even a single method could be put to the test. I know my own abilities are insufficient, but I pray you wise ones might briefly consider my humble sincerity. I beg for your guidance and enlightenment; I shall never betray this debt of gratitude as long as I live.”

The first-seated elder replied, “We do not begrudge you these arts, but we fear that in days to come they may bring you <term>entanglements<note>original: "累" (lèi). This refers to worldly troubles, burdens, or legal consequences that might arise from possessing such powerful knowledge.</note></term>. If you can treat others without regard for high or low status, without distinguishing between the poor and the rich or the noble and the base; if you do not strive for wealth and bribes, nor fear labor and hardship; and if you make the care of the elderly and compassion for the young your most urgent priority—then, and only then, can you escape calamity.”

The Master <note>Referring to Hua Tuo.</note> bowed twice in thanks and said, “I dare not forget a single word of your sagely advice; I am capable of following it all.”

The two elders laughed and pointed toward the eastern part of the grotto, saying, “On the stone bed, there is a book container. Take it yourself. Leave our dwelling quickly and do not show these works to the <term>common stream<note>original: "俗流" (súliú). Refers to ordinary people or those with worldly, non-spiritual motivations.</note></term>; you must keep them secret.”

As soon as the Master obtained the books and turned his head, the old men had already vanished. Apprehensive and fearful, the Master fled the cave. Suddenly, the sky turned dark as clouds raced and rain poured down, and the stone cave collapsed into ruins. 

Once he examined the formulas and theories within the books, he found many of them to be strange and marvelous. From then on, whenever he applied or tested these methods, their effects were always <term>divinely inspired<note>original: "存神" (cúnshén). A phrase indicating that the results were so perfect they seemed to involve supernatural or divine efficacy.</note></term>. 

Yet, before the Master reached the age of sixty, he was indeed <term>executed by Wei<note>original: "為魏所戮" (wèi wèi suǒ lù). This refers to the historical execution of Hua Tuo by the warlord Cao Cao, who was the King of Wei during the Three Kingdoms period.</note></term>. The old...

<summary>Hua Tuo receives a secret medical text from two mysterious elders after promising to practice medicine ethically and without greed. The encounter ends with the cave collapsing and a forewarning of Hua Tuo's eventual execution by the ruler of Wei.</summary>

<keywords>Hua Tuo, medical ethics, divine transmission, hidden scrolls, Cao Cao, Kingdom of Wei, miraculous healing</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the preface to the *Zhongzang Jing* (Classic of the Central Treasury). While the previous page recounted the legendary story of the physician Hua Tuo receiving secret books from two elders in a mountain cave, this page shifts to a later narrator—identifying as the Master’s descendant—who describes "rediscovering" the manuscript in a hidden casket after a prophetic dream.</meta>

# Preface to the Classic of the Central Treasury

The words people had spoken previously were thus proven true. I am the Master’s <term>maternal grandson<note>original: "外孫" (wàisūn). While literally meaning maternal grandson, this identifies the narrator as a direct family descendant authorized to handle the Master’s legacy.</note></term>. While I was mourning in the Master’s private chamber, I dreamed that the Master led me to a seat and spoke to me, saying: "The *Classic of the Central Treasury* is a true <term>method for saving lives<note>original: "活人法" (huórén fǎ). In traditional Chinese medicine, this refers to a set of clinical principles or formulas so powerful they are said to restore life to the dying.</note></term>. You may take it, but do not pass it on to those who are unworthy."

I awoke feeling startled and unsettled. I then searched through the Master’s old belongings and discovered a stone casket. Upon opening it, I found a bundle of books; it was indeed the *Classic of the Central Treasury*. Because my own nature is clumsy in practicing these arts, I have passed them on to my successor <note>original: "次予思" (cì yǔ sī). This refers to the next person in the line of transmission; the narrator acknowledges their own limitations and justifies why the book is being shared or published now.</note> and have recorded these facts here to attest to its authenticity.

->Written as a preface in the ninth month of autumn, during the <term>Jiayin year<note>original: "甲寅" (jiǎyín). A year in the traditional sixty-year sexagenary cycle. Based on the history of this text, it likely refers to 1134 or 1194 CE during the Song Dynasty.</note></term>.<--

<summary>This page describes how a descendant of Hua Tuo rediscovered the hidden "Classic of the Central Treasury" inside a stone casket following a dream visitation. It establishes the "miraculous" lineage of the text and marks its transition from a family secret to a shared medical resource.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, Zhongzang Jing, lineage, medical transmission, dream vision, stone casket, Jiayin year</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded the preface, where the narrator (identified as Hua Tuo's descendant) explained the miraculous discovery of this manuscript in a stone casket. # Master Hua’s Classic of the Central Treasury, Volume One

->Written by Hua Tuo, courtesy name Yuanhua, of Qiao County during the Han Dynasty<-

## First Discourse: On Humanity Patterning itself after Heaven and Earth

Humans are beings who receive their mandate from Heaven above and are sustained by Earth below. <term>Yang<note>The active, masculine, solar, or expansive principle in Chinese philosophy and medicine.</note></term> assists them, and <term>Yin<note>The receptive, feminine, lunar, or contractive principle in Chinese philosophy and medicine.</note></term> supports them. When Heaven and Earth are in harmony, the human <term>Qi<note>original: "氣" (qì). Vital life force, energy, or breath that flows through the body and the universe.</note></term> is tranquil; when Heaven and Earth are in discord, the human Qi is obstructed. 

For this reason, Heaven and Earth possess the four seasons and the <term>Five Elements<note>original: "五行" (wǔxíng). The fundamental phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water that govern all natural phenomena.</note></term>, cold and warmth, and movement and stillness. Their transformations manifest in the environment: joy becomes rain, anger becomes wind, condensation becomes frost, and expansion becomes the rainbow. This is the constant order of Heaven and Earth. 

Similarly, humans possess four limbs and <term>five viscera<note>original: "五臟" (wǔzàng). These are the Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys.</note></term>, the acts of breathing and exhaling, waking and sleeping, and the flow and dispersal of <term>Essence and Qi<note>original: "精氣" (jīngqì). "Essence" (Jing) refers to the physical foundation of life, while "Qi" refers to functional energy.</note></term>. Physical action manifests as <term>Nutritive Qi<note>original: "榮" (róng). Also known as Ying Qi, it is the energy that flows through the vessels to nourish the blood and organs.</note></term>, expansion manifests as <term>Protective Qi<note>original: "氣" (qì). Specifically referring here to the energy that circulates on the body's surface to defend against illness.</note></term>, and utterance manifests as sound. This is the constant order of humanity. 

The Yang principle is bestowed upon our physical form, while the Yin principle is carefully preserved within our Essence. In this, humans and the universe are identical. If a person fails to maintain this stewardship, then internal "steaming" causes heat to erupt, or stagnation causes cold to arise, the mind becomes clouded, and the body wastes away.

<summary>This page establishes the foundational theory of the text, arguing that the human body is a microcosm of the universe (Heaven and Earth) and that health depends on maintaining a balance between Yin and Yang.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, Zhongzang Jing, Microcosm, Yin and Yang, Five Elements, Qi, Five Viscera, Essence</keywords>

While the previous page established the general harmony between the human body and the cosmos, this section shifts to a specific analogy: comparing human pathology (illnesses and physical symptoms) to astronomical and geological disturbances in the natural world. Although attributed to Hua Tuo in this manuscript, the text is historically associated with the "Profound Mechanism of the Plain Questions" by Liu Wansu.</meta>

<margin>
Profound Mechanism of the Plain Questions <note>original: "素問玄機" (Sùwèn Xuánjī). This refers to a seminal commentary on the *Huangdi Neijing* (Yellow Emperor's Inner Classic) focusing on the "Plain Questions" section.</note>
Volume One

Tumors and obstructions become deep-seated abscesses and ulcers <term>Yongju<note>original: "癰疽" (yōngjū). Severe inflammatory skin lesions; "Yong" are superficial abscesses, while "Ju" are deep-seated, more dangerous ulcers.</note></term>. When these conditions are flourishing, they cause wheezing and gasping; when they are diminishing, they cause the body to wither and dry up. These states are clearly displayed upon the facial features and manifest throughout the physical form. 

The openings and blockages of Heaven and Earth operate in exactly this same way. Therefore, the waxing and waning of the <term>Five Planets<note>original: "五緯" (wǔwěi). Literally "The Five Warp-threads," referring to Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note></term>, the deviations of the stars, the eclipses of the sun and moon, and the flight of comets and "bushy" stars <note>original: "彗孛" (huì bèi). Ancient Chinese terms for different types of comets; "Hui" refers to those with clear tails, and "Bei" to those with a hazy, radiating "bushy" appearance.</note> are the disasters and anomalies of Heaven and Earth. 

When cold and warmth do not arrive at their proper times, this is the stagnant heat and congestion of Heaven and Earth. When the earth heaves and stones stand upright <note>A reference to earthquakes or geological upheavals.</note>, these are the abscesses and ulcers of Heaven and Earth. Violent winds and rapid rains are the shortness of breath and exhaustion of Heaven and Earth. When the great rivers and streams are depleted and consumed, this is the withered parching of Heaven and Earth. 

A discerning physician resolves these states with medicine, aids the body with the <term>Acupuncture Needle<note>original: "鍼" (zhēn). The traditional tool used to prick specific points on the body to regulate the flow of vital energy.</note></term>, transforms the condition through the <term>Way<note>original: "道" (Dào). The fundamental principle of balance and natural order.</note></term>, and assists through practical management. Therefore, the physical body has illnesses that can be saved, just as Heaven and Earth have disasters that can be averted. 

The perils, fortunes, life, and death of a human being are endowed by the principles of Heaven and Earth. Illnesses of <term>Yin<note>The receptive, cold, and slow principle.</note></term> arrive slowly and depart slowly. Illnesses of <term>Yang<note>The active, hot, and rapid principle.</note></term> arrive quickly and depart quickly. Yang is born from heat, and heat leads to relaxation and expansion; Yin is born from cold, and cold leads to contraction and urgency. When cold evils... <note>The text breaks off here at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page uses a macrocosm-microcosm analogy to explain that human diseases like abscesses and wheezing are equivalent to cosmic "illnesses" like eclipses, earthquakes, and storms. It introduces the diagnostic concept that Yang-type illnesses progress rapidly while Yin-type illnesses are slow and lingering.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory comparison between cosmic disturbances and human illness, transitioning into the second treatise which defines the roles of Yin and Yang as the fundamental forces of life and death. It maintains the theme that the human body is a microcosm of the universe.</meta>

...[pathogens of cold and dampness] strike the lower body, <term>heat pathogens<note>original: "熱邪" (rèxié). In traditional Chinese medicine, these are external influences like fever or inflammation that disrupt the body's balance.</note></term> strike the upper body, and pathogens from food and drink strike the center. A person's movement and stillness are fundamentally rooted in the patterns of Heaven and Earth. 

One who understands humanity will find verification in the Heavens; one who understands the Heavens will surely find verification in humanity. Heaven aligns with man, and man follows the laws of Heaven. By observing whether the movements of Heaven and Earth are in harmony or in opposition, one can know the decline and prosperity of human health. 

Humans suffer from a hundred illnesses; these illnesses present a hundred signs, and those signs undergo a hundred transformations. All of these arise from whether the <term>Yin and Yang<note>original: "陰陽" (yīnyáng). The two opposing yet complementary forces that govern the universe: light and dark, heat and cold, masculine and feminine.</note></term> of Heaven and Earth are in harmony or conflict. If one can exhaustively investigate these principles, their insight will be like that of the gods.

## Second Treatise: The Great Essentials of Yin and Yang and the Regulation of the Spirit

Heaven is the ancestor of Yang; Earth is the domain of Yin. Yang is the root of life; Yin is the foundation of death.

Between Heaven and Earth, it is the human being who is supported by the assistance of both Yin and Yang. To obtain Yang is to live; to be consumed by Yin is to die. The "Yang within Yang" is the <term>High Transcendent<note>original: "高真" (gāozhēn). A Daoist term referring to a perfected being or a celestial deity who has achieved immortality through the cultivation of pure Yang energy.</note></term>; the "Yin within Yin" is the dark ghost. Therefore, those who are imbued with Yang...

<summary>This page explains that human illness is a reflection of cosmic imbalance and introduces the second treatise, which identifies Yang as the source of life and Yin as the foundation of death.</summary>
<keywords>Yin and Yang, Heat pathogens, Macrocosm-Microcosm, High Transcendent, Regulation of Spirit, Hua Tuo, Chinese Medical Theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Second Treatise, detailing the physical characteristics and temporal cycles of Yin and Yang, while citing the Golden Casket to emphasize the importance of living in harmony with seasonal shifts.</meta>

...nsitory. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the "High Transformation" or nature of Yang.</note>

The body of Yang is long, while the body of Yin is short. An abundance of heat is the master of Yang, whereas an abundance of cold is the root of Yin. Yang strives toward the heights; Yin strives toward the depths. The movement of Yang is swift; the movement of Yin is slow. The substance of Yang is light; the substance of Yin is heavy. 

When Yin and Yang are balanced, Heaven and Earth are in harmony and the human <term>vital energy<note>original: "氣" (qì). The fundamental life force or breath that flows through all living things and the universe.</note></term> is tranquil. When Yin and Yang are in opposition, Heaven and Earth are obstructed and the human vital energy collapses. Therefore, when Heaven and Earth obtain Yang, there is blazing heat; when they obtain Yin, there is piercing cold. 

Yang begins before the <term>Hour of the Rat<note>original: "子前" (zǐqián). The period leading up to and including midnight (11 PM – 1 AM), marking the birth of Yang energy.</note></term> and ends after the <term>Hour of the Horse<note>original: "午後" (wǔhòu). The period during and following noon (11 AM – 1 PM), when Yang begins to decline.</note></term>. Yin begins after the Hour of the Horse and ends before the Hour of the Rat. The waxing and waning of Yin and Yang occur each in their proper time, beginning and ending in an alternating cycle without rest. If a person can follow this cycle, they possess true wisdom. 

The <term>Golden Casket<note>original: "金匱" (Jīnguì). This refers to the *Essential Prescriptions from the Golden Casket*, a foundational clinical manual of Chinese medicine written around 200 CE.</note></term> says: "At the start of autumn, nourish Yang; at the start of spring, nourish Yin. Do not let Yang be closed off from the outside, and do not let Yin invade from the outside." 

Fire emerges from Wood, and Water is born from Metal. <note>This describes the "Generating Cycle" of the Five Elements: Wood provides the fuel for Fire, and Metal (associated with the cool of autumn and condensation) generates Water.</note> When Water and Fire provide mutual assistance, they seek one another out across the upper and lower regions of the body. One who can follow this path will never be submerged in ruin; this is the meaning of the principle. 

Alas! How can the common and the foolish understand this logic? Their actions and conduct lack propriety, and they spontaneously...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental principles of Yin and Yang, specifically focusing on the internal and external causes of physical decline and the diagnostic signs found in the pulse, speech, and temporal cycles. <margin>Treatise on Cold Damage, Part One</margin>

One suffers affliction from external sources such as Wind, Cold, Summer-heat, and Dampness <note>The "Four Evils" or pathogenic factors in traditional medicine that invade the body from the environment.</note>; or from internal sources such as hunger, overeating, physical labor, and exhaustion. These factors lead to ruin, deceiving and damaging the vital body until the governing spirit perishes. The body becomes bound, and the judgment of life or death is delivered. Yet, many do not realize that the <term>pulse<note>original: "脉" (mài). The diagnostic technique of feeling the arterial throb to determine the state of internal organs and vital energy.</note></term> has five signs of death, while the <term>vital energy<note>original: "氣" (qì). The life force or breath that animates the body.</note></term> has five signs of birth.

Those of a Yin nature have a heavy pulse; those of a Yang nature have a light pulse. If a person has a Yang disease but a Yin pulse, they shall not endure. If they have a Yin disease but a Yang pulse, the recovery will not be completed. In Yang conditions, the patient talks much, while in Yin syndromes, they are silent. Those who speak much are easily aided; those who are silent find it difficult to thrive.

In a Yang disease, the patient is quiet at dawn; in a Yin disease, they are peaceful at night. The movements of Yin and Yang follow their proper seasons and hours. If Yang is deficient, there is turmoil at dusk; if Yin is deficient, there is conflict at daybreak. When morning and evening are thus confused, the vital energy is exhausted and gasping. 

The principles of life and death are clearly revealed within the understanding of Yin and Yang. When the Yin energy descends but does not ascend, it is called <term>Severed Networks<note>original: "斷絡" (duànluò). A pathological state where the secondary vessels lose their connection, preventing energy from returning upward.</note></term>. When the Yang energy ascends but does not descend, it is called <term>Extinguished Channels<note>original: "絶經" (juéjīng). A critical condition where the primary pathways of energy are blocked from circulating back down, leading to a separation of the body's forces.</note></term>. 

A pathogen within the Yin is called "Turbid"; a pathogen within the Yang is called "Clear." Only when Fire reaches the <term>Gate of Water<note>original: "坎戶" (kǎnhù). A reference to the Kan (Water) trigram; in healthy circulation, the "Fire" of the Heart must descend to warm the "Water" of the Kidneys.</note></term> and Water reaches the <term>Station of Fire<note>original: "離局" (líjú). A reference to the Li (Fire) trigram; health requires the "Water" of the Kidneys to ascend and cool the "Fire" of the Heart.</note></term> do Yin and Yang respond to one another in harmony. If Yin is insufficient, one must supplement it...

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical discussion on the internal cultivation of Yang energy to achieve longevity, then transitions into Chapter 3, which outlines the cosmological foundations of human existence through the interplay of Yin, Yang, and the Five Elements.</meta>

...hausted. If Yang is insufficient, one must assist it with <term>fire-essence<note>original: "火精" (huǒ jīng). In the context of internal alchemy, this refers to the concentrated, heating energy of Yang, sometimes associated with medicinal sulfur.</note></term> to balance the <term>water-mother<note>original: "水母" (shuǐ mǔ). This refers to the cooling essence of Yin, often associated with the kidneys or alchemical mercury.</note></term>. When Yin and Yang are properly harmonized, each follows its own ascending order. 

The path reaching three inches upward is called the "Divine Path of Yang," and the course reaching three inches downward is called the "Ghostly Route of Yin." <note>These "three inches" likely refer to distances from the navel or the Dantian (the body's "cinnabar field" or energy center), where upward movement signifies vitality and downward movement signifies physical decline.</note> It is always proper for Yin to be diminished and for Yang to be kept in a state of fullness. For those who dwell in the center, Yin and Yang are perfectly balanced and stable. 

Therefore, the "Yang within Yang" is granted the title of <term>Heavenly Immortal<note>original: "天仙" (tiān xiān). A Daoist term for a transcendent being who has refined their vital energy to the highest degree.</note></term>, while the "Yin within Yin" carries the name of a "Lower Ghost." Those who follow the nature of Yin mostly face extinction, while those who follow the nature of Yang mostly achieve longevity. One who understands this profound and subtle principle will find that there is nothing they cannot master.

## Chapter Three: On Generation and Formation

Yin and Yang are the <term>pivot and mechanism<note>original: "樞機" (shū jī). A metaphor for the central axis or fundamental workings of the universe.</note></term> of Heaven and Earth; the <term>Five Elements<note>original: "五行" (wǔ xíng). The five phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water that govern all natural cycles.</note></term> are the beginning and the end of Yin and Yang. Without Yin and Yang, Heaven and Earth could not be formed; without the Five Elements, Yin and Yang could not operate. 

Thus, humanity is brought into being by Heaven and Earth, but meets its downfall through the discord of Yin and Yang. Life arises from the flow or the opposition of the Five Elements. Heaven and Earth possess Yin, Yang, and the Five...

<summary>This page concludes a discourse on the vital necessity of prioritizing Yang energy over Yin to achieve immortality and introduces the "Treatise on Generation," which defines Yin, Yang, and the Five Elements as the fundamental building blocks of the universe and the human body.</summary>
<keywords>Yin and Yang, Fire-essence, Water-mother, Heavenly Immortal, Five Elements, Generation and Formation, Longevity, Cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter Three, moving from the abstract principles of Yin and Yang to the specific physiological application of the Five Elements (Wu Xing) within the human body. It establishes the generative cycle of the internal organs and cites a classic medical text to link these organs to specific tissues like blood, bone, and tendon.</meta>

<margin>Central Treasury, Page One</margin>
<margin>The Five Elements Prevail</margin>

Every human being possesses blood vessels and the <term>Five Viscera<note>original: "五臓" (wǔ zàng). In traditional Chinese medicine, these are the "solid" organs—Lungs, Liver, Heart, Kidneys, and Spleen—thought to store vital essence rather than just processing waste.</note></term>, which correspond to the <term>Five Elements<note>original: "五行" (wǔ xíng). Also known as the Five Phases: Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth. They represent the fundamental forces and cycles of the universe.</note></term>. The Five Viscera are the Lungs, Liver, Heart, Kidneys, and Spleen. 

Metal generates Water; Water generates Wood; Wood generates Fire; Fire generates Earth; and Earth generates Metal. Thus, the Way of generation and formation circulates in an infinite loop. 

The Lungs generate the Kidneys; the Kidneys generate the Liver; the Liver generates the Heart; the Heart generates the Spleen; and the Spleen generates the Lungs. They provide <term>nourishment<note>original: "榮養" (róng yǎng). This refers to the continuous flow of nutrient-rich energy and blood that sustains the body's tissues.</note></term> throughout the upper and lower regions of the body without ever resting. Therefore, the *Essential Discussion on the Golden Chamber's Ultimate Truth* <note>original: "金匱至真要論" (jīn kuì zhì zhēn yào lùn). A reference to a classical medical authority, likely a lost or variant section of the "Golden Chamber" medical tradition.</note> states:

> The Heart generates the blood, and the blood is the mother of the flesh.
> The Spleen generates the flesh, and the flesh is the dwelling place of the blood.
> The Lungs belong to the <term>Qi<note>original: "氣" (qì). The vital life force or "breath" that animates the body and governs its movement.</note></term>, and Qi is the foundation of the bones.
> The Kidneys correspond to the bones, and the bones are the root of the tendons.
> The Liver is connected to the tendons, and the tendons are the source of the blood.

The Five Viscera and the Five Elements complete and generate one another, circulating day and night without beginning or end. To follow this natural order brings good fortune; to go against it brings disaster. 

The Way of Heaven, Earth, Yin, Yang, and the Five Elements is contained within every human being. One who grasps this principle can transcend the <term>calculations of Yin and Yang<note>original: "陰陽之數" (yīn yáng zhī shù). This refers to the predetermined span of life or destiny dictated by natural cycles.</note></term>, seize the hidden mechanisms of Heaven and Earth, and delight in the essentials of the Five Elements, existing without beginning and without end.

<summary>This page explains the interconnected cycle of the Five Elements and the Five Viscera, detailing how organs like the Heart and Lungs generate bodily components like blood and bone. It emphasizes that human health depends on harmonizing with these eternal, circulating natural laws.</summary>

<keywords>Five Elements, Five Viscera, Hua Tuo, Blood and Qi, Generation Cycle, Jinkui Zhizhen Yaolun, Longevity, Traditional Chinese Medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the generative cycle of the Five Viscera to the pathological state of "Yang Reversal." ...the immortals shall never die.

## Chapter Four: Treatise on Yang Reversal

<note>original: "陽厥" (yáng jué). In traditional medicine, "Jue" refers to a reversal or inversion of the natural flow of Qi. "Yang Reversal" specifically denotes a state of extreme, pathological heat or excess Yang energy that has reached a breaking point, causing severe physical and mental distress.</note>

Sudden winds and violent heat; clouds and objects scattering in the air; dull mornings followed by clear evenings; hot nights and cold days; cold when it should be warm, and rain failing to fall when it is due; the exhaustion of water and the parching of the soil; years of great drought where grass and trees wither in distress and the rivers and streams dry up—this is the **Yang Reversal** of Heaven and Earth. 

In the human body, this manifests as sudden blockages and obstructions, followed by abrupt <term>shortness of breath<note>original: "喘促" (chuǎn cù). Specifically, a rapid, labored breathing often associated with excess heat in the lungs or chest.</note></term>. The four limbs cannot be drawn in, and the <term>two bowels<note>original: "二腑" (èr fǔ). Here referring to the primary hollow organs responsible for elimination: the large intestine and the bladder.</note></term> do not function smoothly. The ears become deaf and the eyes go blind; the throat dries and the mouth becomes parched; sores erupt upon the tongue and clear fluid flows from the nose. The cheeks turn crimson and the heart is filled with irritability; the head feels dizzy and the brain feels heavy. 

Both eyes burn like fire and the entire body feels as if it is being scorched. Those who were previously incapable of certain feats suddenly find themselves capable; those who previously had no desires suddenly find themselves overcome by them. They climb to high places to sing and laugh; they discard their clothing and run about wildly. Their speech is <term>manic and incoherent<note>original: "狂言妄語" (kuáng yán wàng yǔ). A classic description of heat-induced delirium or mania in historical medical texts.</note></term>, and they can no longer distinguish between relatives and strangers. They are seized by boundless restlessness and drink water without ceasing. The chest and diaphragm swell and expand, while the abdomen and flanks feel full and oppressed. The flesh of the back...

<note>The vocabulary provided in the source identifies key terms used in this passage: Yang Reversal (陽厥), Treatise on Yang Reversal (陽厥論), Shortness of Breath (喘促), Wild and Delirious Speech (狂言妄語), The Drying of Rivers (江河之涸), and the Hollow Organs (腑).</note>

<summary>This page describes "Yang Reversal," a state of extreme heat imbalance, comparing environmental disasters like drought to severe human symptoms including respiratory distress, sensory loss, and manic behavior.</summary>
<keywords>Yang Reversal, Yang Jue, mania, drought, internal heat, delirium, respiratory distress, traditional Chinese medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of pathological "reversal" (Jue) and transitions into Chapter Eight, which defines the fundamental diagnostic categories of Deficiency and Excess. It follows the previous description of the symptoms of Yang Reversal.</meta>

...[leads to] death. To treat cold with more cold leads to death; yet to treat heat with more heat leads to life. <note>original: "寒之又寒...熱之又熱". This refers to the advanced medical concept of "Counter-treatment." In cases of "True Cold with False Heat," a doctor might use hot herbs to treat a patient who appears to have a fever, a paradoxical method that "follows" the false symptom to reach the true cause.</note> The *Great Treatise of the Golden Casket* <note>original: "金匱大要論" (Jīn Guì Dà Yào Lùn). An influential medical text, likely referring to early versions of the "Golden Chamber" traditions attributed to Zhang Zhongjing.</note> states: "Chills occurring at night follow the natural order; fever occurring at night runs counter to it. Heat occurring during the day follows the natural order; chills occurring during the day run counter to it." The signs of natural progression or adverse reversal also depend upon clear and careful examination.

## Chapter Eight: Great Treatise on Deficiency and Excess

<note>In traditional medicine, <term>Deficiency (Xu)<note>A state of depletion, where the body lacks sufficient vital energy (Qi), blood, or fluids.</note></term> and <term>Excess (Shi)<note>A state of overabundance, usually referring to the presence of a pathogen (like wind or heat) or a stagnation of energy.</note></term> are the two "poles" used to categorize all illnesses.</note>

Illnesses include deficiency of the **viscera** and excess of the **viscera**, deficiency of the **bowels** and excess of the **bowels**, deficiency and excess in the upper body, and deficiency and excess in the lower body. <note>"Viscera" (Zang) refers to the solid organs like the heart and liver; "Bowels" (Fu) refers to hollow organs like the stomach and intestines.</note> Their manifestations are each different and must be investigated deeply. 

When the intestines growl and the <term>Qi wanders<note>The sensation of gas or energy moving uncontrollably through the torso.</note></term>; when the feet are chilled and the hands are cold; when food will not enter the stomach and vomiting occurs at any time; when the skin and hair appear withered and the flesh is shriveled; when the ears are blocked and the eyes are clouded; when the voice is broken and scattered; when walking leads to <term>labored breathing<note>original: "喘促" (chuǎn cù). Shortness of breath or panting upon slight exertion.</note></term> and the spirit cannot be gathered—these are the signs of **Deficiency of the Five Viscera**. 

In diagnosing this through the <term>pulse<note>The primary diagnostic tool, where the physician feels the radial artery at different depths and positions.</note></term>, the pulse feels lively when the finger is lifted, but becomes faint when pressed down. One must observe which specific position the pulse occupies to determine which specific organ is affected. Furthermore, upon pressing, the pulse may feel **deep, small, weak, faint, and short**.

<summary>This page concludes the signs of favorable and unfavorable disease progression before introducing the diagnostic concepts of Deficiency (Xu) and Excess (Shi), specifically detailing the physical symptoms and pulse qualities of visceral deficiency.</summary>

...low organs like the stomach and intestines.

Weak, soft, and soggy pulses all indicate **visceral deficiency**. <note>original: "臟虛" (zàng xū). A state where the internal solid organs—the heart, liver, spleen, lungs, or kidneys—lack sufficient vital energy or substance.</note> When deficiency is found, the standard course of treatment is to supplement and nourish the body. 

In cases where there is excessive intake of food and drink, difficulty with urination or bowel movements, a feeling of fullness and oppression in the stomach and diaphragm, or pain in the joints; when the body feels heavy, the head is dizzy, and vision is blurred; if the lips are swollen, the throat is constricted, there is gas and tension in the intestines, or the skin and flesh feel numb; if there is sudden shortness of breath and fatigue, occasional bouts of chills and fever, or the simultaneous outbreak of sores and ulcers; if emotions of grief and joy fluctuate, or if the body becomes spontaneously weak or abnormally rigid; and if the energy (Qi) does not flow smoothly and the blood becomes stagnant—these are all signs of **visceral excess**. <note>original: "臟之實" (zàng zhī shí). Unlike deficiency, "excess" here refers to a pathological overabundance, often caused by the accumulation of heat, toxins, or stagnant energy within the core organs.</note> 

When diagnosing the pulse, if it feels vigorous and strong both when touched lightly and when pressed firmly, it indicates an **excess condition**. Furthermore, pulses described as long, floating, rapid, racing, flooding, tight, wiry, or large are all categorized as "excess." One must observe which meridian is affected to determine which specific viscus is in a state of excess.

When there is headache, redness of the eyes, heat in the skin but cold in the bones, sluggishness in the limbs, and blockage of the blood and Qi; when red tumors or growths appear, and the throat is swollen and painful; if the pain increases with a light touch but feels relieved by firm pressure, and yet the patient’s eating and drinking habits remain normal—this is called **bowel excess**. <note>original: "腑實" (fǔ shí). This specifically refers to "excess" in the hollow organs, such as the stomach, gallbladder, or bladder, often manifesting as physical obstructions or acute inflammation.</note> 

In such cases, the pulse will be floating, solid, and large. Symptoms also include itching of the skin and...

When the flesh is distended and the abdomen feels full, food and drink remain undigested, and bowel movements are loose and uncontrollable, one must examine the pulse. If the pulse feels **slippery** <note>original: "滑" (huá). A pulse described as feeling like pearls rolling in a basin, often associated with dampness or phlegm.</note> under a light touch, but feels **even** <note>original: "平" (píng). A balanced, normal pulse indicating the underlying energy is not yet exhausted.</note> when pressed firmly, this indicates **bowel deficiency**. <term>Bowel deficiency (fǔ xū): A state of weakness or lack of function in the "six hollow organs," which include the stomach and intestines, responsible for transport and digestion.</term> One must observe which meridian is affected to provide treatment according to the proper season.

When there is a sensation of blockage and fullness in the stomach and diaphragm, splitting pain in the head and eyes, and an inability to swallow food; when the head and neck feel heavy and dizzy, the throat is obstructed, and nasal mucus or saliva is thick and sticky—examine the pulse. If the **Cunkou** <note>original: "寸口" (cùn kǒu). The pulse position on the wrist closest to the hand, often used to diagnose the upper body and lungs.</note> on both the left and right sides feels **deep, knotted, solid, and large**, this is a case of **upper excess**. <note>original: "上實" (shàng shí). A condition where pathological factors like heat or phlegm accumulate in the chest or head.</note>

When there is irritability and redness, a feeling of instability in the heart, trembling or shivering during movement, and a hoarse or raspy voice; when the lips are scorched, the mouth is dry, and there is wheezing, fatigue, and a total lack of strength; when the face lacks healthy color and the **jaws and chin** <note>original: "頤頜" (yí hé).</note> appear swollen and full—examine the pulse. If the **Cun** pulses on both sides are **weak and minute**, this is a case of **upper deficiency**.

When there is difficulty with urination and bowel movements, yet the appetite for food and drink remains as usual; when the lower back and legs feel heavy and there is pain around the navel and abdomen—examine the pulse. If the **Chizhong** <note>original: "尺中" (chǐ zhōng). The "cubit" pulse position located furthest from the hand, corresponding to the kidneys and lower body.</note> on both hands feels **hidden and hesitant**, this is a case of **lower excess**. <note>original: "下實" (xià shí). An accumulation or blockage in the lower abdomen or pelvic region.</note>

When urination and bowel movements are difficult, appetite fluctuates, and the lower back and legs feel so heavy it is as if one were sitting in water; when walking is laborious, the **internal energy rushes upward** <note>original: "氣上奔衝" (qì shàng bēn chōng). A sensation of energy surging violently from the abdomen toward the throat, often linked to kidney or liver imbalance.</note>, and sleep is disturbed by dreams...

## On the Restlessness of the Upper and Lower [Burners], Chapter Nine

In cases of **Spleen disease**, there is "restlessness in the upper and lower." What does this mean? Above the Spleen is the Heart, which is its **mother**; below the Spleen is the Lung, which is its **son**. <note>This refers to the "Generating" cycle of the Five Elements (Wu Xing). Fire (Heart) generates Earth (Spleen), and Earth (Spleen) generates Metal (Lungs).</note> 

The Heart governs the **blood** and belongs to **Yin**; the Lung governs the **Qi** <note>Vital energy or breath.</note> and belongs to **Yang**. When the Spleen is diseased, the "mother" above (the Heart) becomes restless. If the mother is restless, this results in **insufficient Yin**. When Yin is insufficient, **fever** develops. 

Furthermore, when the Spleen is diseased, the "son" below (the Lung) becomes restless. If the son is restless, this results in **insufficient Yang**. When Yang is insufficient, **chills** develop. 

When the Spleen is diseased, both blood and Qi become restless. When blood and Qi are restless, chills and fever alternate without rest; therefore, Spleen disease resembles **malaria**. <note>original: "瘧" (nüè). A traditional term for disorders characterized by alternating fits of shivering cold and intense heat.</note> This is because the Spleen is **Earth**, the Heart is **Fire**, and the Lung is **Metal**. Fire generates Earth...

<summary>This page describes the pulse characteristics of "lower deficiency" and introduces Chapter Nine, which explains how Spleen illness disrupts the Heart and Lungs through the Five Elements "Mother-Son" relationship.</summary>
<keywords>Spleen disease, Heart and Lungs, Five Elements theory, Mother-Son relationship, Yin and Yang, pulse diagnosis, malaria, lower deficiency</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter Nine regarding the Spleen's central role and begins Chapter Ten, which establishes the fundamental principles of pulse diagnosis. Since Earth generates Metal, it is said that above there is the **Heart-Mother** and below there is the **Lung-Son**. The Spleen dwells in the middle; when it is diseased, it manifests in this way. The other organs likewise follow this principle of "upper and lower" relationship. <note>In the Five Elements theory, the Spleen (Earth) is "generated" by the Heart (Fire) and "generates" the Lungs (Metal). This makes the Heart its "mother" and the Lungs its "son."</note>

# Treatise on the Essentials of the Pulse, Chapter Ten

The **pulse** is the precursor of the **Qi** and **blood**. <term>Qi (qì): Often translated as "vital energy," it is the motive force that circulates through the body alongside the blood.</term> When the Qi and blood are robust, the pulse is robust; when the Qi and blood decline, the pulse declines. When the Qi and blood are hot, the pulse is **rapid**; when the Qi and blood are cold, the pulse is **slow**. When the Qi and blood are minute, the pulse is **weak**; when the Qi and blood are balanced, the pulse is **moderate**.

Furthermore, a tall person’s pulse should be long, and a short person’s pulse should be short. Those with an impulsive nature should have an **urgent** pulse, while those with a calm nature should have a **relaxed** pulse. When the pulse contradicts these physical and temperamental traits, it is called **discordant** (against the natural flow); when it matches them, it is called **accordant** (following the natural flow). <note>The "discordant" (ni) and "accordant" (shun) relationship is a key diagnostic tool; a pulse that doesn't "fit" the patient's body type often indicates a more serious underlying illness.</note>

Moreover, all **rapid** pulses indicate heat; all **slow** pulses indicate cold; all **tight** pulses indicate pain; all **floating** pulses indicate **Wind** <note>In Chinese medicine, "Wind" (feng) refers to external pathogens that enter the body, often causing sudden onset symptoms like chills or tremors.</note>; all **slippery** pulses indicate **deficiency** <note>original: "虛" (xū). A state of insufficiency in the body's vital resources.</note>; all **hidden** pulses indicate **accumulation**; all **long** pulses indicate **excess**; and all **short** pulses indicate **deficiency**.

Furthermore, pulses that are **short, rough, deep, slow, or hidden** all belong to **Yin**. Pulses that are **rapid, slippery, long, floating, or tight** all [belong to Yang...] <note>The text cuts off here and likely concludes the classification into Yang pulses on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page explains how the Spleen acts as a central pivot between the Heart and Lungs and introduces pulse diagnosis as a reflection of the body's internal Qi, blood, and temperament.</summary>
<keywords>Spleen, Five Elements, Pulse Diagnosis, Qi and Blood, Yin and Yang, Diagnostic Accordance</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between physical traits and pulse readings from Chapter Ten, specifically addressing how Yin and Yang manifestations dictate health or disease. It then transitions into Chapter Eleven, which correlates facial color with specific pulse failures to predict mortality.</meta>

...belong to Yang or Yin. When Yin traits obtain Yin conditions, it follows the natural order; when Yang traits obtain Yang conditions, it is **accordant**. To contradict this is **discordant**. The waxing and waning of **Yin** and **Yang** <note>Yin and Yang represent the dualistic forces of the universe—cool/passive and warm/active—which must remain in balance within the body.</note> should be treated according to the diagnostic classics. 

For instance, if a <term>rapid pulse</term> is found on the left hand: if it is <term>floating</term>, heat has entered the **Small Intestine**; if it is <term>deep</term>, heat has entered the **Heart**. All other cases follow this same principle.

<note>
- **Rapid pulse (shù):** A pulse that beats faster than the standard six beats per respiratory cycle, usually indicating heat.
- **Floating pulse (fú):** A pulse that is easily felt with light pressure but grows weak with heavy pressure, often indicating an illness on the "surface" of the body.
- **Deep pulse (shén):** A pulse felt only with heavy pressure near the bone, indicating an internal or "sinking" condition.
</note>

# Treatise on the Five Colors and the Severance of the Pulse, Chapter Eleven <gloss>One version replaces the word "Severance" with "Pulse"</gloss>

A blue complexion without a pulse at the right <term>Guan</term> position indicates that the **Spleen** has reached its end. A red complexion without a pulse at the right <term>Cun</term> position indicates that the **Lungs** have reached their end. A white complexion without a pulse at the left <term>Guan</term> position indicates that the **Liver** has reached its end. A yellow complexion without a pulse at the left <term>Chi</term> position indicates that the **Kidneys** have reached their end. A black complexion without a pulse at the left <term>Cun</term> position indicates that the **Heart** has reached its end. 

<note>
Ancient Chinese pulse diagnosis uses three positions on the wrist:
- **Cun (Inch):** The position closest to the hand.
- **Guan (Gate):** The middle position.
- **Chi (Foot):** The position furthest from the hand.
Each position corresponds to different internal organs.
</note>

These five "severances" <note>original: "jué" (絕), meaning a total exhaustion or cutting off of vital energy.</note> result in death. If the severance occurs during the organ's corresponding season, death is immediate; if it occurs outside its season, the patient will die within half a year. However, if these five colors appear on the face but the pulses do not show such signs of exhaustion, then the person is not actually ill.

# Chapter Twelve: Treatise on the Determination of Internal and External Symptoms of Pulse Illnesses

For a person suffering from **Wind illness** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, "Wind" (fēng) is a primary cause of disease, often associated with sudden onset, paralysis, or tremors, similar to the modern concept of a stroke.</note>, if the pulse at the kidney position is rapid and fluctuates between floating and deep, and if they have incessant sweating and noisy, labored breathing, they will die. Otherwise, they shall live. For a person suffering from **Qi illness** <note>Referring to disorders of the body's vital energy, often manifesting here as severe fluid retention or edema.</note>, if the entire body is swollen, the four limbs cannot be moved, wheezing occurs at all times, and there is **Cold Reversal** <term>jué nì: a condition where the extremities become icy cold due to the failure of Yang energy to circulate</term>, they will die. Otherwise, they shall live.

<note>The following is written in small characters:</note>
Regarding the signs of **Dampness** in the pulse: if it is deep and small, the patient will die; if it is floating and large, they shall live. 

For a person suffering from **Consumption** <note>Consumptive disease (láo) refers to a chronic wasting illness, often involving the exhaustion of the body's vital essences, historically associated with conditions like tuberculosis.</note>, if they experience a prolapsed rectum, the wasting away of flesh from the bone, a scattered or weak voice, the vomiting of blood, uncontrollable sexual impulses, a state where dreams and reality seem to collide, disconnected breathing, and a pattern of being cool during the day but feverish at night, they will die. Those who spit up pus and blood will also die. However, if their pulse is not rapid and possesses **roots** <note>A pulse with "roots" (gēn dì) is one that remains perceptible at the deepest level of pressure, indicating that the patient's fundamental "Kidney Qi" is still intact.</note>, and if their cheeks are not flushed red, they shall live.

For a person suffering from **Dysentery** <note>The original term is "Cháng pì," referring to chronic diarrhea or blood and pus in the stool.</note> who is passing pus and blood: if the pulse is urgent, the skin is hot to the touch, food cannot be kept down, the abdomen is distended, and the eyes are fixed in a stare, they will die. Or, if the entire body is cold and the pulse is deep and fine without vitality, they will also die. If they can eat as they normally do, and the pulse is either deep or floating but remains strong and continuous, they shall live. 

For a person suffering from **Heat illness**, if the four limbs are cold and the pulse is weak—

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the prognostic signs of various illnesses from the previous page, focusing on the balance of symptoms and the pulse as indicators of life and death, following the discussion on Wind, Qi, and Consumption.</meta>

If a patient does not wish to see people, cannot take in food, and suffers from incessant **diarrhea** <note>original: "利下" (lì xià), referring to the downward leaking of fluids and vital essence</note>, they will die. If they are able to eat, their four limbs are warm, their pulse is large, and even if they speak frantically and cannot sleep, they shall live.

For a patient suffering from a **Cold illness** <note>A category of disease characterized by a lack of Yang energy, often manifesting as chills and stagnation.</note>, if they speak frantically, do not sleep, the body is cold, the **pulse is rapid** <term>shù mài: a pulse that beats more than five times per breath, usually indicating heat or extreme agitation of the heart-fire</term>, their breathing is labored, and their eyes are fixed and staring straight ahead, they will die. If the pulse is forceful and they do not wheeze, they shall live.

For a patient with a **Yang illness** <note>A disease characterized by excess heat, inflammation, or over-activity.</note>, if their spirit is **disordered** <note>original: "顚倒" (diāndǎo), meaning inverted or topsy-turvy</note>, they sleep without waking, and their speech is incoherent, yet the pulse—whether floating or deep—is forceful, they shall live. If the pulse is weak, food cannot be taken in, the stomach is unstable, and there is incessant diarrhea, they will die.

For a long-term patient, if the pulse is large but the body is emaciated, food does not nourish the intestines, and their speech sounds as if they are not ill, but they are exhausted whether sitting or lying down, they will die. If their intake of food fluctuates, the pulse is small but forceful, their speech is clear and distinct, there is no dark aura on the forehead, and the bowels are **constipated** <note>In this context, constipation suggests the body still retains some "substance" or "holding power," whereas incessant diarrhea suggests the total collapse of the system's ability to contain energy.</note>, they shall live.

Generally, when a **Yang illness presents with Yin symptoms**, or a **Yin illness presents with Yang symptoms**; when an emaciated person has a large pulse, or a robust person has a weak pulse; when the upper and lower body functions are inverted, and **Yin and Yang are disordered**; or when Cold and Heat overcome one another—these are all inauspicious signs. Those whose symptoms follow the natural order shall live; those whose symptoms go against it shall die. The methods of treatment must be deeply and carefully considered.

<summary>This page outlines clinical indicators for prognosis, emphasizing that the "forcefulness" of the pulse and the ability to take in food are vital signs of life, even when behavior is frantic. It highlights the danger of "inverted" symptoms where the physical body and the pulse do not match.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from specific prognostic signs of existing illnesses to a discussion of "sudden death" in individuals who appear healthy, concluding the thirteenth discourse and beginning the fourteenth regarding calamitous or strange medical conditions.</meta>

# Essential Discourse on Life and Death, Number Thirteen

In all cases where a person is not ill, but their **Five Elements** <note>The five phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water that govern the body's internal systems and organ functions.</note> are exhausted, they will die. If they are not ill but their **temperament changes** <note>original: "性變" (xìng biàn), referring to a sudden, radical shift in personality or behavior without an obvious external cause.</note>, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly speak deliriously, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly fall silent, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly experience **rapid, labored breathing** <note>original: "喘促" (chuǎn cù), a sign of the lungs failing to grasp Qi.</note>, they will die.

If they are not ill but suddenly suffer a **rigid collapse** <term>qiáng jué: a condition of sudden fainting or loss of consciousness accompanied by physical stiffness</term> <gloss>one version says "a stroke"</gloss>, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly go blind, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly go deaf, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly suffer **atrophy or limpness** <note>original: "痿緩" (wěi huǎn), the sudden loss of use or strength in the limbs.</note>, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly become swollen and bloated, they will die.

If they are not ill but suddenly suffer a blockage of the stool and urine, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly have no pulse, they will die. If they are not ill but suddenly become confused and dazed as if drunk, they will die. All of these cases occur because the **Internal Qi** <term>nèi qì: the vital energy stored within the organs that sustains the body’s core functions</term> has been exhausted <gloss>one version says "severed"</gloss> beforehand.

If the flow of Qi is contrary, they will die immediately; if the flow is favorable, they may last for two years, but none shall survive.

# Discourse on Illnesses involving Calamities and Wonders, Number Fourteen

<summary>This section lists various "sudden" symptoms in seemingly healthy people that signify the internal exhaustion of vital energy, serving as omens of inevitable death. It also introduces the next chapter on unusual or catastrophic diseases.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on prognosis, concluding the fourteenth discourse on "Calamitous Anomalies"—paradoxical symptoms that signal a breakdown of internal harmony—and beginning the fifteenth discourse regarding the "Methods of Water" and illnesses originating in the Yang-aligned Six Bowels.</meta>

What is meant by the term **calamitous anomalies** <note>original: "災恠" (zāi guài), referring to bizarre, paradoxical, or "monstrous" clinical presentations that defy the expected patterns of a disease.</note>? It refers to situations where a patient should be cold but is instead hot; should be hot but is instead cold; should vomit but does not; should have diarrhea but does not; should sweat but does not; should speak but remains silent; should sleep but stays awake; or should desire water but refuses it. These are all categorized as calamitous anomalies. This occurs because the **Qi** <note>The vital life force or energy that flows through the body.</note> of the **Five Viscera** <term>wǔ zàng: the five core "solid" organs—Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys—which store essential essence.</term> no longer follow or support one another. Cases of **Fourfold Reversal** <term>sì nì: a critical condition where the hands and feet turn icy cold, signifying a total collapse of Yang energy</term> cannot be cured. "Fourfold Reversal" indicates that the **Host and Guest Cyclical Qi** <note>original: "主客運氣" (zhǔ kè yùn qì). This refers to the "Five Movements and Six Qi" theory, where the "Host" is the fixed seasonal energy and the "Guest" is the shifting annual energy. If neither aligns with the patient's needs, the timing for recovery is lost.</note> have both failed to find their proper timing.

### Six Discourses on the Methods of Water, Number Fifteen

Diseases that arise in the **Six Bowels** <term>liù fǔ: the "hollow" organs—Gallbladder, Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Bladder, and the Triple Burner—responsible for processing and transporting waste.</term> belong to the **Yang** system. When Yang energy erupts, it may move upward or downward, inward or outward, or it may accumulate and stagnate in the center. When this movement reaches its extreme, it manifests in the patient's behavior: some may sing and laugh; some may wail and weep; some may run about wildly; some may groan and moan; some may act submissively or dejected; while others may act as if they are lofty and wise <note>This describes a spectrum of psychiatric symptoms known in traditional medicine as "Yang Madness" (kuáng), where excessive heat or agitation in the hollow organs affects the spirit.</note>.

<summary>This page defines "calamitous anomalies" as paradoxical symptoms indicating a fatal lack of harmony between the organs and seasonal energies, then begins a new discourse on how imbalances in the Six Bowels produce erratic, manic behaviors.</summary>

There are those who are awake but cannot sleep; there are those who sleep but cannot wake; there are those who can eat but cannot pass waste <note>original: "便利" (biàn lì), referring to the smooth excretion of urine and stool.</note> comfortably.
There are those who cannot eat but have spontaneous diarrhea; there are those who can speak and their voice is clear; there are those who cannot speak and their voice is muffled. Although these symptoms are all different, they all originate in the **Six Bowels** <term>liù fǔ: the "hollow" organs—Gallbladder, Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Bladder, and the Triple Burner—responsible for processing and transporting nutrients and waste.</term>. If the patient desires passage <note>Refers to the clearing of the bowels or pathways of the body.</note>, then facilitate that passage; if they desire blockage, then facilitate that blockage; if they desire water, provide water to assist them; if they desire ice, use ice to help them. 
Take care never to go against the patient’s inclinations, nor should one forcibly suppress them. By following and complying with these desires in this manner, ten out of ten will live, and a hundred out of a hundred will live—no illness will fail to be cured.

## Discourse Sixteen: The Five Great Methods

Illnesses that arise from the **Five Viscera** <term>wǔ zàng: the five core "solid" organs—Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys—which store essential essence.</term> all belong to the category of **Yin** <note>The cooling, internal, and structural aspect of the body’s energy, contrasted with the active, warming Yang.</note>. When they manifest, they may appear as **hemiplegia** <term>piān kū: paralysis or wasting of one side of the body, often associated with a stroke in modern medicine.</term> or **atrophy and lameness** <term>wěi bì: a condition characterized by weakness, limpness, or wasting of the limbs, making it difficult to walk.</term>; 
or as external cold with internal heat; or external heat with internal cold; or as distention of the heart and abdomen; or [afflictions of] the hands and feet...

...clenching of the hands and contraction of the limbs; or deviation of the mouth and eyes <note>A classic description of facial paralysis or the aftermath of a stroke.</note>; or numbness and insensitivity of the skin; or difficulty in walking; or rigidity and stiffness of the body; or incessant vomiting and diarrhea; or restless, agonizing pain; or sudden loss of speech; or a long-term loss of the voice. In such states, the patient is weak and silent, appearing like a dead person. 

Symptoms such as these all arise from **Yin** <note>The internal, cooling, and structural energy of the body.</note>. When Yin is in excess, **Yang** <note>The external, warming, and functional energy of the body.</note> must be deficient. When Yang is in excess, Yin must not be full. Therefore, as the previous discourse states: “If Yang is insufficient, assist it with **Fire Essence** <term>huǒ jīng: a term for the primordial, essential heat or Yang energy required to sustain life.</term>; if Yin is insufficient, aid it with **Water Mother** <term>shuǐ mǔ: the primordial source of fluid and cooling essence, representing the root of Yin.</term>.” 

For this reason, if a patient desires to sweat, induce sweating; if they desire warmth, provide warmth; if they desire heat, apply heat; if they desire fire <note>Likely referring to moxibustion or direct cautery.</note>, use fire; if they desire decoctions <note>original: "湯" (tāng), which can refer to medicinal soups or hot medicinal baths.</note>, provide decoctions. Warmth, heat, decoctions, and fire must be applied according to what is appropriate for the individual; be careful never to force these treatments upon them. 

By proceeding in this manner, one will achieve a perfect success rate—ten thousand out of ten thousand will be saved. These methods of Water and Fire are the true application of Yin and Yang. The way of healing and salvation should now be understood in detail and with clarity.

# Discourse Seventeen: On the Five States of Life and Death within Wind

"Wind <term>fēng: in traditional medicine, 'Wind' is a primary cause of disease that appears suddenly, changes rapidly, and causes symptoms like tremors, dizziness, or paralysis.</term> within the five" refers to the five internal organs: the Liver, Heart, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys. Among these Five Viscera <term>wǔ zàng: the core functional systems of the body in traditional Chinese medicine.</term>, the signs indicating whether a patient will live or die are different for each.

The signs <gloss>alternatively: clinical symptoms</gloss> of **Heart Wind** are spontaneous sweating and a preference for lying flat on the back; the patient is unable to turn over. Their speech becomes wild and delusional. If the lips are a healthy, bright red, the patient will live. In such cases, it is appropriate to apply moxibustion <term>jiǔ: the practice of burning dried mugwort near the skin to stimulate healing through heat.</term> at the **Xinshu** <note>Also known as the "Heart-Shu" point (BL15), located on the back near the spine; it is believed to directly influence the Heart's energy.</note> point. However, if the lips and face appear blue-green, yellow, white, or black—shifting instability between these colors—and the eyes twitch or flutter without stopping, the "Heart energy" is exhausted. Such a patient cannot be saved; they will die within five or six days.

The signs of **Liver Wind** involve a blue-green <note>original: "青" (qīng), a color that can describe the shades of a bruise, the sky, or lush plants; in medicine, it often indicates stagnation or cold.</note> color surrounding the eyes and extending up to the forehead. If the patient can only sit and is unable to crouch or bend over, the condition can be treated. However, if they are panting with their eyes staring straight ahead, and both the lips and face are blue-green, it is a sign of death. For Liver Wind, it is appropriate to apply moxibustion at the **Ganshu** <note>The "Liver-Shu" point (BL18) on the back, used to regulate the Liver.</note> point.

The signs of **Spleen Wind** include the entire body turning yellow, and an abdomen that is large and feels full. The patient has no appetite, and the four limbs cannot be controlled or held steady. If the tips of the hands and feet appear blue-green while the face remains yellow...

<summary>This page details the diagnostic signs (prognosis) for "Wind" diseases affecting the Heart, Liver, and Spleen, distinguishing between treatable symptoms and signs of impending death.</summary>
<keywords>Heart Wind, Liver Wind, Spleen Wind, moxibustion, Xinshu, Ganshu, Five Viscera, prognosis, traditional Chinese medicine</keywords>

...can be treated; otherwise, the patient will die. For **Spleen Wind** <term>pí fēng: a condition where "Wind" enters the Spleen system, often affecting digestion and muscle tone.</term>, it is appropriate to apply moxibustion <term>jiǔ: the practice of burning dried mugwort near the skin to stimulate healing through heat.</term> at the **Pishu** <note>Also known as the "Spleen-Shu" point (BL20), located on the back; it is used to regulate the energy of the Spleen and stomach.</note> point.

The signs of **Kidney Wind** involve the patient being unable to do anything but sit in a crouched position <note>original: "踞坐" (jù zuò), a squatting or huddled sitting posture often adopted to protect the lower back.</note>, as the waist and legs feel heavy and painful. If you examine the area under the ribs and find that yellow spots have not yet appeared, the condition can be treated. Otherwise, the patient will die. For Kidney Wind, one should apply moxibustion to the **Shenshu** <note>Also known as the "Kidney-Shu" point (BL23), used to strengthen the lower back and Kidney essence.</note> point.

The signs of **Lung Wind** are a feeling of fullness in the stomach, a state of being dazed or dimmed in vision <note>original: "冒昧" (mào mèi), which in this context refers to a clouded consciousness or darkened sight.</note>, and spontaneous sweating. The nose cannot distinguish between fragrances and foul odors, and the patient pants so heavily they cannot lie down. These cases can be treated. However, if there is a loss of blood <note>referring to hemoptysis or coughing up blood.</note> and the patient engages in wild, delusional speech, the condition cannot be treated, and they will die within seven or eight days. For Lung Wind, it is appropriate to apply moxibustion at the **Feishu** <note>Also known as the "Lung-Shu" point (BL13), used to clear the lungs and regulate breathing.</note> point.

In all cases of diagnosis, a **pulse** <term>mài: the feeling of the blood flow at the wrist, used to diagnose the state of internal organs.</term> that is **Slippery** <note>smooth and flowing, like pearls rolling in a basin.</note> and **Scattered** <note>lacking a root, indicating the dispersal of vital energy.</note> indicates Wind. Pulses that are **Slow** but **Large**, **Floating** and **Tight** <gloss>one version says "Hollow"</gloss>, or **Soft** and **Weak** all belong to the category of Wind disorders.

In the disease of **Wind-strike** <term>zhòng fēng: a sudden 'attack' of Wind, historically corresponding to what we now call a stroke.</term>, if the area beneath the nose appears both red and black, and the patient spits out foam while the body remains rigid and straight, they will die within seven days. Furthermore, in cases of Wind-strike, if the mouth is locked shut and the sinews are cramped, but the pulse remains **Slow**, the patient will live. If the pulse is **Urgent** and **Rapid**, they will die.

Additionally, if both the Heart and Spleen are struck by Wind, the tongue will become stiff and the patient will be unable to speak. If both the Liver and Kidney are struck by Wind...

**Sudden collapse** is acquired from over-exertion and labor. 
**Unresponsiveness of the hands and feet** and **hesitant or slurred speech** are acquired from overindulgence in sexual intercourse <note>original: "房中" (fáng zhōng), literally "inside the room," a standard medical euphemism for sexual activity.</note>. 
**Rashes and hives** are acquired from "Bi" <term>bì: a "blockage" or "impediment" syndrome usually caused by wind, cold, or dampness.</term> <gloss>also written as "Dampness"</gloss>. 
**Severe joint pain** is acquired by engaging in sexual intercourse while intoxicated. 
**Deafness, blindness, sores, and lesions** are acquired from violating dietary prohibitions regarding the **Five Flavors** <note>The five flavors are sour, bitter, sweet, pungent, and salty. Traditional Chinese medicine teaches that an excess or improper balance of these flavors can damage specific internal organs.</note>.

## Treatise Eighteen: On Accumulations, Gatherings, Stagnant Masses, Conglomerations, and Various Vermin

Accumulations, gatherings, stagnant masses, conglomerations, and various vermin <note>original: "雜蟲" (zá chóng), referring to various internal parasites, worms, or even complex growths that were historically perceived as living entities.</note> all arise when the True Qi <term>zhēn qì: the vital, original energy that sustains life and protects the body from illness.</term> of the Five Zang and Six Fu organs is lost, allowing Evil Qi <term>xié qì: external or internal pathogenic factors like cold, heat, or stagnant energy that cause disease.</term> to take hold and combine. 

If these conditions are left untreated for a long time, they develop into accumulations, gatherings, stagnant masses, or conglomerations, or they may even transform into vermin. Their forms are each distinct: some can cause great harm to a person, while others do not; some cause the illness to progress slowly, while others cause it to advance rapidly. 

There are those that cause pain and those that cause itching; some even grow heads and feet, while others resemble cups or hard lumps. Their behaviors and categories differ. Generally, they are formed because of the resonance between internal states and external influences, the conflict between the True and the Evil, and the boiling and clumping <note>original: "熏摶" (xūn tuán), a vivid description of energy and blood being "smoked" or "steamed" until they bind together into a solid mass.</note> of the Qi and Blood. 

**Accumulations** <term>jī: fixed, immovable masses associated with the solid Zang organs, usually related to the blood.</term> are linked to the Zang organs. 
**Gatherings** <term>jù: movable, fluctuating masses associated with the hollow Fu organs, usually related to the Qi.</term> are linked to the Fu organs. 
**Stagnant masses** <note>original: "癥" (zhèng).</note> are linked to...

Vermin <note>original: "蟲" (chóng). In this context, it refers to parasites, worms, or pathological growths believed to have their own "life" within the body.</note> are formed when Blood, Qi, and food interact and transform one another.

Therefore, there are five types of Accumulations, six types of Gatherings, twelve types of Stagnant Masses, eight types of Conglomerations <note>The OCR repeats the character for "stagnant mass" (瘕), but the context and subsequent list of eight types typically refer to *gǔ* (蠱), which are toxic or parasitic conglomerations.</note>, and nine types of Vermin. The twelve Stagnant Masses are named: Overexertion, Qi, Cold, Heat, Deficiency, Excess, Wind, Dampness, Food, Medicine, Worry, and Grief. 
The eight Conglomerations are named: Green, Yellow, Dry, Blood, Fat, Fox, Snake, and Turtle <note>These descriptive names refer to the physical appearance of the masses or the specific symptoms they manifest, which were thought to mimic the behaviors of these animals or substances.</note>. 
The nine types of Vermin are: the Hidden, the Snake, the White, the Flesh, the Lung, the Stomach, the Red, the Weak, and the Threadworm.

The explanations for how these diseases manifest are found in various treatises, and the specific methods for treating them are all detailed in the following sections.

## Treatise Nineteen: On Overexertion and Injury

"Overexertion" <term>láo: a state of exhaustion or "consumption" caused by the depletion of vital energy through mental or physical strain.</term> refers to exhausting the Spirit and Qi. "Injury" <term>shāng: physical damage or trauma to the body's structure and systems.</term> refers to harming the physical form and appearance <note>original: "形容" (xíng róng), meaning the external body and its healthy luster.</note>. If hunger and satiety are without moderation, then injury occurs.

### Twenty-One: The Classic of Life Cycles

When the Spleen experiences excessive worry and contemplation, it harms the Heart. Excessive sexual desire harms the Kidneys. Irregularities in one's daily routine of waking and resting harm the Liver. Excessive joy, anger, sadness, or grief harm the Lungs. Furthermore, the external elements of wind, cold, summer heat, and dampness cause injury from the outside, while hunger, over-satiation, and taxing physical labor cause failure from within.

If a pathogen is contracted during the day, the illness affects the Nutritive Qi <term>Yíng: the refined essence of food and water that circulates within the blood vessels to nourish the body.</term>. If it is contracted at night, the illness affects the Defensive Qi <term>Wèi: a fierce, protective energy that circulates outside the vessels to protect the body from external pathogens.</term>. The Nutritive and Defensive Qi flow through the meridians, circulating between the interior and exterior of the body, each following its own rhythm according to the day and the night.

When exhaustion begins in one organ, it gives rise to a second; the second transmits the illness to a third; the third connects to a fourth; the fourth interferes with a fifth; and the fifth returns to attack the first. Once these five pathogens are deeply hidden within, the Vital Qi <note>original: "眞氣" (zhēnqì). Also known as "True Qi," it is the total functional energy of the human body.</note> is lost of its own accord. This causes the person’s muscles to waste away, the spirit to weaken, the appetite to diminish, and walking to become difficult. When a person reaches such a state, even the Master of Fate <note>original: "司命" (Sīmìng). A deity in Chinese mythology believed to have the power to bestow or take away human lifespan.</note> cannot grant them life.

Therefore, the *Treatise on Regulating the Spirit and Qi* says: "Regulating the spirit and Qi, being cautious with alcohol and sex, maintaining discipline in daily routines, reducing worry and contemplation, and keeping one's diet light in flavor—these are the primary principles of longevity."

In diagnosis, the pulse will be extremely rapid <gloss>some versions read: rapid and extreme</gloss>, extremely tight, extremely thin, and extremely weak. The remaining cases follow this pattern.

<summary>This page explains how emotional excess, environmental factors, and poor lifestyle habits damage the internal organs in a cascading cycle that eventually exhausts the body's Vital Qi. It emphasizes moderation in all things as the foundation for a long life.</summary>
<keywords>Spleen, Heart, Kidney, Liver, Lung, Nutritive Qi (Ying), Defensive Qi (Wei), Vital Qi (Zhen Qi), Siming (Master of Fate), Longevity, Pulse Diagnosis</keywords>
