Pulses that are extremely faint, extremely rough, extremely slippery, extremely short, extremely long, extremely floating, extremely sunken, extremely tight, extremely bowstring-like, extremely surging, or extremely full—all of these conditions arise from exhaustion and injury <note>original: "láoshāng" (勞傷). This refers to physical or mental overexertion that damages the internal systems.</note>.

## Twenty: Discourse on Corpse Transmission

Corpse Transmission <note>original: "Chuánshī" (傳尸). A historical term for a group of chronic, wasting diseases similar to tuberculosis, believed to be "transmitted" from the deceased or by spectral pathogens.</note> is not only caused by members of the same household infecting one another. When a person’s Blood and Qi <term>Xuè-Qì: the dual vitalities of the body; Blood provides nourishment while Qi provides the functional energy.</term> are weakened and their internal organs <term>Zàng-Fǔ: the collective name for the solid yin organs and hollow yang organs.</term> are depleted and frail, they may be struck by ghostly qi <note>original: "guǐqì" (鬼氣). In the medical context of 800 AD, this referred to invisible, malevolent environmental pathogens that preyed on the physically vulnerable.</note>. Because they are susceptible to this evil influence, the disease then takes hold.

The signs of this condition include:
Incessant coughing;
Oppression and discomfort in the stomach and diaphragm;
Aching and pain in the limbs;
Wasting away of the flesh and skin;
Inability to consume food or drink;
Unpredictable vomiting and diarrhea;
Spitting up pus and blood;
A craving for water and syrups;
A strange fondness for singing or a sudden love for sorrow and grief;
Madness and mania <gloss>one version says: breaking out into song</gloss>;
Difficulty with urination and defecation.

The illness may be encountered through food and drink, or arrive during wind and rain. It may be contracted while visiting the sick or attending a funeral, or met while wandering about at dawn or dusk.

<keywords>pulse diagnosis, exhaustion (laoshang), Corpse Transmission (chuanshi), ghostly qi (guiqi), internal organs (zang-fu), consumption, diagnostic signs</keywords>

It then introduces Chapter Twenty, which establishes the fundamental principles for diagnosing and treating the internal organs.</meta>

...madness and mania <gloss>shīkuáng: a state of severe mental derangement often associated with spectral influence in ancient medicine</gloss>.

Or the condition may arise because of the stagnation of Qi or the movement of Blood; or from sleeping exposed in the open fields, or by chance encounters in gardens and groves. In such places, one encounters and concentrates the qi of disease and death <note>original: "bìngsǐ zhī qì" (病死之氣). This refers to pathogenic environmental factors or "miasmas" believed to linger in places where death or decay had occurred.</note>, becoming infected and falling ill; thus, it is called "Corpse Transmission." ## Twenty: Discourse on the Methods of Depletion, Repletion, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, Reverse, and Smooth Conditions of the Five Zang and Six Fu Organs

->1<-

Generally, in humans, the conditions of the five zang and six fu <term>Zàng-Fǔ: the collective name for the internal organs. The five Zang (solid) are the Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys; the six Fu (hollow) are the Stomach, Gallbladder, Large Intestine, Small Intestine, Bladder, and Sanjiao (Triple Burner).</term>—whether they are in a state of depletion <term>Xū: a state of deficiency or weakness in the body's vital substances or organ function.</term> or repletion <term>Shí: a state of excess, often characterized by the presence of a pathogen or the stagnation of Qi and Blood.</term>, cold or heat, life or death, or whether the flow of Qi is reverse or smooth—are all manifested in physical symptoms and the pulse-qi.

If one does not perform a careful diagnosis and examination, there is no way to recognize these states. If there is depletion, one must tonify it <note>original: "bǔ" (補). To nourish or strengthen what is weak.</note>; if there is repletion, one must drain it <note>original: "xiè" (瀉). To purge or clear an excess.</note>; if there is cold, one must warm it; if there is heat, one must cool it. If the condition is neither depletion nor repletion, one should use the meridians <note>original: "jīng" (經). The pathways through which Qi and Blood circulate throughout the body.</note> to regulate the body. This is the fundamental method of a skilled physician. The specific details regarding the pulses and symptoms are recorded at the end of the chapter.

# Twenty: Discourse on the Methods of Determining the Depletion, Repletion, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, Reverse, and Smooth Conditions of the Liver via Pulse and Symptoms

The Liver is paired with the Gallbladder as its exterior-interior counterpart <term>Biǎolǐ: A fundamental pairing in Chinese medicine where a solid (zang) organ and a hollow (fu) organ are energetically linked.</term>; the Foot Jueyin <term>Juéyīn: "Terminal Yin," the name of the acupuncture channel associated with the Liver.</term> and the Foot Shaoyang <term>Shàoyáng: "Lesser Yang," the name of the channel associated with the Gallbladder.</term> are their respective meridians.

The Liver flourishes during the Spring. Spring is the season when the myriad things begin to take life; its Qi is tender and soft, vacuous and expansive. For this reason, the pulse of the Liver is naturally "wiry" <term>Xián: a pulse that feels tight and straight, like a tuned string of a musical instrument; it is the healthy standard for the Liver during springtime.</term> yet soft. In such a state, one must not induce sweating; if the pulse is weak, one must not use purgatives <note>original: "xià" (下). This refers to "downward-draining" treatments, such as laxatives, used to clear internal heat or stagnation.</note>. A pulse that is both wiry and long is called "balanced" (healthy); any pulse that contradicts this indicates disease.

A pulse that is vacuous and wiry is called "Excessive" <note>original: "tàiguò" (太過). In this diagnostic context, it refers to the organ's energy over-extending or being hyperactive.</note>; in such cases, the disease resides on the Exterior. This excess causes a person to be forgetful, absent-minded, and to experience dizziness. A pulse that is replete yet faint is called "Deficient" <note>original: "bùjí" (不及). Literally "not reaching," meaning the pulse energy is insufficient or fails to meet the healthy standard.</note>; in this case, the disease resides in the Interior. This deficiency causes chest pain that pulls at both flanks and leads to a sensation of abdominal distention and fullness.

Generally, when the Liver is in a state of repletion <term>Shí: a state of excess or "fullness," often caused by a localized blockage or an invading pathogen.</term>, it causes pain beneath the flanks that pulls toward the lower abdomen, making one <gloss>one version lacks these five words</gloss> prone to fits of anger. When the Liver is in a state of depletion <term>Xū: a state of "emptiness" or deficiency in the organ's vital energy.</term>, the person feels fearful, as if someone is about to hunt them down or arrest them. When the Liver's Qi flows in reverse <note>original: "qì nì" (氣逆). This describes a pathological upward surge of energy, contrary to the organ's natural downward or smoothing flow.</note>, it causes headaches, deafness, and redness of the cheeks <gloss>one version says "swelling"</gloss>.

When the pulse is pressed deeply, it is urgent; when felt at the surface, it is the same. This governs pain in the flanks and ribs <gloss>one version says "fullness"</gloss>, difficulty urinating, headache, and dizziness. If the pulse is extremely urgent, the patient finds speech distasteful. If it is only slightly urgent, the Qi <term>Qi: The vital energy or life force that flows through the body's meridians.</term> is lodged in the stomach and beneath the flanks.

If the pulse is extremely relaxed, there is vomiting and reflux; if it is slightly relaxed, it indicates water-numbness <term>Shuǐbì: A condition where fluid accumulation leads to localized numbness, swelling, or heaviness.</term>. If the pulse is large and urgent, it indicates internal abscesses and the vomiting of blood; if it is slightly large, it indicates sinew-numbness <term>Jīnbì: A condition where the tendons and ligaments are affected, causing stiffness or restricted movement.</term>.

If the pulse is extremely small, the patient drinks excessively. If it is slightly large <gloss>one edition says "wasting"</gloss>, it indicates numbness <gloss>one edition says "numbness"</gloss> <note>The variant readings suggest this refers to Xiāobì (Wasting Numbness), a condition of depletion and sensory loss.</note>. If the pulse is extremely slippery, it indicates urinary blockage and hernias; if it is slightly slippery, it indicates urinary incontinence. If the pulse is extremely choppy, it indicates "flowing fluids" <note>original: "liúyǐn" (流飲). This refers to a condition where fluids stay in the limbs rather than being processed by the organs.</note>; if it is slightly choppy, it indicates abdominal masses and contractures <gloss>these last two words are missing in one version</gloss>.

Furthermore, if the accumulated Qi of the Liver remains in the flanks for a long time without dispersing, it causes cough with reflux or intermittent malarial fevers. When the Liver is in a state of depletion <note>original: "xū" (虚). Also translated as "vacuity," meaning the organ's energy is insufficient.</note>, one dreams of fine, downy flowers and grasses. When the Liver is in a state of repletion <note>original: "shí" (實). Also translated as "excess," meaning the organ's energy is hyperactive or stagnant.</note>, one dreams of lush mountain forests.

Regarding Liver disease: in the morning, the patient is cheerful <gloss>one version says "lucid"</gloss>; in the evening, the symptoms worsen; and at night, the patient is quiet. Liver disease results in headache and flank pain <gloss>these two words are missing in one version</gloss>, dizziness, fullness in the limbs, retraction of the scrotum <term>Nángsuō: A critical symptom in Chinese medicine where the genitals contract inward, signaling the exhaustion of Liver Qi.</term>, and the blockage of urine <gloss>one version says "smoothly passing"</gloss>. In such cases, death occurs within ten days.

Furthermore, if the body is hot with chills and the four limbs cannot be lifted, the pulse should normally be wiry, long, and urgent. If it is instead short and choppy, this represents "Metal overcoming Wood" <term>Jīn kè mù: In Five Phases theory, the Metal element (associated with the Lungs) suppresses the Wood element (associated with the Liver). A "Metal" pulse appearing during a "Wood" disease is a "reverse" or life-threatening sign.</term>. Ten...

In cases of death, the condition cannot be treated. Furthermore, if there is Cold within the Liver, then both arms will be painful and cannot be raised; the root of the tongue will be dry, and the patient will sigh frequently. There is pain within the stomach such that the patient cannot turn onto their side. This is the condition when the pulse at the left *guan* <term>Guan: The middle position of the pulse at the wrist; the left side specifically reflects the state of the Liver and Gallbladder.</term> is slow and choppy. 

If there is Heat within the Liver, there is panting and a sense of fullness, accompanied by frequent anger, eye pain, and abdominal distension. The patient has no appetite, their behavior is erratic, and they suffer from fright and palpitations during sleep; their vision is also blurred. This is the condition when the pulse at the left *guan* is *yin-shi* <term>Yin-Shi: A "hidden excess" pulse that feels deep and forceful, indicating internal accumulation or blockage.</term>.

Chills and fever occur, resembling the symptoms of malaria, and there is no desire to eat. In women, the menses do not arrive and the breath is rapid. This is the condition when the pulse at the left *guan* is deep and weak.

## Section 23: On the Methods for Pulse Signs of Vacuity, Excess, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, Adverse, and Favorable Conditions of the Gallbladder

The Gallbladder is the "Official of Neutrality and Uprightness" <note>This title suggests the Gallbladder's role in maintaining balance and unbiased judgment.</note>; it is titled the "General," for decisiveness and judgment stem from it. This means it governs the capacity for joy and anger, as well as hardness and softness. It stands in an interior-exterior relationship with the Liver, and the Foot Lesser Yang <term>Foot Lesser Yang: The primary acupuncture meridian associated with the Gallbladder.</term> is its meridian. If the Gallbladder is Vacuous, it is susceptible to "Cold Damage" <note>original: "shānghán" (傷寒). A category of febrile diseases caused by external cold pathogens.</note>; if there is Cold, then—

Fear and dizziness occur, and the patient cannot sleep alone. In this context, it refers to one's mental clarity, vitality, and the ability of the heart/mind to remain calm and focused.</note> will not be guarded.

The patient is restless whether lying down or rising. Furthermore, when "Original Water" <note>An archaic term for systemic edema or fluid accumulation.</note> breaks out, its root lies in the Gallbladder; the swelling begins first in the head and face and spreads down to the feet. Additionally, if a Liver-cough <term>Liver-cough: A type of cough associated with flank pain, often resulting from the "transmission" of illness between the Zang (solid) and Fu (hollow) organs.</term> persists for a long time without ceasing, the pathogenic influence is transmitted into the Gallbladder, causing the patient to vomit clear, bitter fluid.

In Gallbladder disease, the patient is prone to frequent sighing, experiences a bitter taste in the mouth, and vomits clear fluid <gloss>one version says "fluid from the night before"</gloss>. There is a feeling of agitation in the heart and a sense of fear, as if one were about to be captured. There is a sensation of a foreign object in the throat and frequent spitting. Furthermore, in cases of Gallbladder distension, there is pain beneath the tongue <gloss>another version says "beneath the flanks"</gloss>, a bitter mouth, and frequent sighing.

When pathogenic qi takes up residence in the Gallbladder, the patient dreams of fighting and lawsuits. The pulse diagnosis is found on the left hand at the *guan* position <term>Guan: The middle pulse position on the wrist, associated in this system with the Liver and Gallbladder.</term>; if the pulse is felt as floating <note>A "floating" pulse is felt with light pressure on the skin, often indicating an "exterior" condition or the state of a "Fu" (hollow) organ like the Gallbladder.</note> at this spot, that is its specific region.

If the Gallbladder has Excess Heat, the spirit-mind will not be stable. Furthermore, Heat in the Gallbladder leads to excessive sleep, while Cold in the Gallbladder leads to a lack of sleep. Again, at the left *guan* pulse, if the *yang* <note>Referring to the superficial level of the pulse reading, which corresponds to the Gallbladder (the hollow Fu organ) as opposed to the deeper Liver reading.</note> is faint, it indicates Gallbladder Vacuity. If the *yang* is rapid, it indicates Gallbladder Excess. If the *yang* is vacuous, it indicates the exhaustion of the Gallbladder.

# Chapter 20: Methods for Determining Excess, Deficiency, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, Adverse, and Favorable Conditions of the Heart through Pulse and Symptoms

->The Heart is the most honorable of the five viscera <term>Viscera (Zang): The five solid organs (Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, Kidneys) that store vital essence.</term>, holding the title of "Emperor."<- It stands in an interior-exterior relationship with the Small Intestine and is the dwelling place of the Spirit <note>original: "Shen" (神). In Chinese medicine, the Heart does not just pump blood but houses the consciousness and mental vitality.</note>. Furthermore, it governs the blood, corresponds to the element of Fire, and flourishes during the summer. The Hand Shaoyin <note>The "Lesser Yin" meridian of the hand.</note> is its primary meridian.

In general, the summer pulse is "hooked" <term>Hooked Pulse (Gou): A pulse that feels forceful and full as it arrives but fades as it departs, like the curve of a hook; this is the healthy pulse of the Heart during its peak season.</term>: it arrives forcefully and departs weakly. Therefore it is called "hooked." If the pulse is the opposite of this, it indicates illness. If it arrives forcefully and departs just as forcefully, this is a state of "Excess," and the illness resides in the exterior <note>The surface of the body, such as the skin and muscles.</note>. If it arrives weakly and departs forcefully, this is a state of "Insufficiency," and the illness resides in the interior <note>The internal organs.</note>.

A state of Excess causes the patient’s body to become hot, with aching bones, mouth sores, and a scorched, dry tongue that leads them to drink excessive water. Insufficiency causes the patient to feel irritable and restless <gloss>one version says "agitation of the Heart"</gloss>; in the upper body, this manifests as coughing and spitting, while in the lower body, it manifests as the leakage of qi <note>Likely referring to flatulence or a loss of "downward" energetic control.</note>.

When the pulse arrives continuously, like a string of pearls or like stroking fine jade <term>Lang-gan: A mythical or precious stone resembling a pearl; used here to describe a smooth, rounded, and healthy pulse quality.</term>, it is called a "Peaceful" pulse. When the pulse arrives continuously <gloss>one version omits these four words</gloss>, but is hurried <gloss>one version says "turbulent"</gloss> and panting, yet the beats remain connected with a slight curvature...

When a disease manifests and the pulse is curved in the front and rigid in the back, as if one were grasping a hard belt hook <note>This "hook" pulse (dai gou) refers to a rigid, inelastic sensation, which is a "dead" pulse lacking the softness of vital energy.</note>, it indicates certain death. 

Furthermore, excessive pondering and worry lead to apprehension <term>Apprehension (Chu Ti): A state of being easily startled or fearful, reflecting a disturbance in the Heart's role as the seat of consciousness.</term>. Apprehension injures the Heart. When the Heart is injured, the Spirit <note>original: "Shen" (神). In Chinese medicine, the Spirit is housed in the Heart; its loss implies a total loss of mental and physical coordination.</note> is lost; when the Spirit is lost, the patient experiences deep terror. 

Additionally, in cases of True Heart Pain <term>True Heart Pain (Zhen Xin Tong): A severe, often fatal chest pain accompanied by cold extremities, traditionally corresponding to what modern medicine calls a myocardial infarction or heart attack.</term>, if the coldness in the hands and feet extends more than five inches past the wrist and ankle joints, a patient who falls ill in the morning will die by evening, and one who falls ill in the evening will die by morning. 

Furthermore, when the Heart is afflicted by Water-Qi <note>An accumulation of pathological fluid affecting the chest, often manifesting as symptoms similar to congestive heart failure.</note>, there is blockage, stagnant energy, and the body swells. The patient cannot lie down and feels irritable and restless; the genitals will also swell. 

When "Wind" strikes the Heart, there is a fluttering <gloss>one version says: drawing in</gloss> fever. The patient cannot walk or stand. They feel hunger in their heart but cannot eat; if they do eat, they will vomit. 

When the Heart is flourishing <note>In its peak season of summer.</note>, the pulse at the Inch Opening <note>The radial pulse point on the left wrist.</note> of the left hand should be flooding, floating, large, and scattered. This is considered a "Balanced" or normal state. If the pulse is the opposite of this, it indicates illness. 

If the pulse is instead deep and slippery, it signifies that "Water is coming to overcome Fire" <note>In the Five Elements cycle, Water (associated with the Kidneys) restricts or "extinguishes" Fire (associated with the Heart). A pulse reflecting Water during a Heart-dominant period is a sign of total system failure.</note>; ten out of ten such cases result in death and are beyond treatment. 

If the pulse is wiry and long, it signifies "Wood coming to visit its Child" <note>Wood (Liver) is the "mother" of Fire (Heart) in the Five Elements generating cycle. This indicates the mother organ is supporting its child, which is a favorable sign for recovery.</note>; the illness will resolve on its own. 

If the pulse is relaxed and large, it signifies "Earth entering Fire." This is merely a minor pathogen interfering and causes no harm <note>Because Fire (Heart) is the mother of Earth (Spleen), the interaction is considered "insulting" but not fatal.</note>.

<margin>Additionally, when the Heart is diseased, there is pain in the stomach, fullness and distension in the four limbs <gloss>one version says: the flanks</gloss>, and pain in the shoulders, back, and arms.</margin>

When the Heart is deficient, there are frequent palpitations and a state of fearful apprehension, leading to sleeplessness. Pain radiates through the chest, abdomen, waist, and back. The patient is prone to <gloss>one version says: frequently</gloss> feeling sorrowful and experiences occasional dizzy spells and fainting.

When the Heart is in a state of excess, there is incessant laughter. One dreams of fire breaking out. When the Heart's energy is flourishing, one dreams of laughter or of great fear and terror. When pathogenic energy takes up residence in the Heart, one dreams of hills, mounds, smoke, and fire.

When the Heart is distended, there is irritability and shortness of breath. Sleep at night is restless. There is pain in the Heart and abdomen, a feeling of stifling distress <term>Stifling distress (Ao Nong): A sensation of oppressive heat and discomfort in the chest, often described as a "burning annoyance" that makes one restless.</term>, and swelling energy that moves up and down. The pain stops and starts at intervals.

When there is heat within the Heart and abdomen, a craving for water, and the discharge of saliva, this indicates "roundworm agitation" <note>original: "Hui Jiao" (蚘蛟). In ancient medicine, this referred to parasitic infestations causing severe abdominal pain that could radiate toward the Heart.</note> affecting the Heart.

In Heart disease, the mind is clear at midday, the condition worsens at midnight, and it is quiet at dawn. <note>This follows the "Five Elements" and "Yin-Yang" cycles, where the Heart—the Fire organ—reaches its peak clarity during the peak of the Sun (midday).</note>

Furthermore, if the pulse at the "Inch Opening" <term>Inch Opening (Cun Kou): The primary position on the wrist used for pulse diagnosis in Chinese medicine.</term> of the left hand is excessively large, there will be heat in the palms and redness <gloss>one version says: in the upper arms</gloss> and armpits. If the pulse is extreme, the stomach feels full and irritable; there is persistent sweating <note>original: "Ji Ji" (濈濈), describing the appearance of continuous, oozing sweat.</note>, a red face, and yellow eyes.

Furthermore, in Heart disease, there is first pain in the Heart and incessant coughing. If the "Gate and Barrier" <term>Gate and Barrier (Guan Ge): A severe medical condition where the digestive and urinary paths are blocked, preventing anything from entering or leaving the body.</term> <gloss>one version says: obstruction</gloss> is blocked and the body feels continuously heavy, the patient will die within three days.

When the Heart is deficient, the patient is fearful of people.

When the eyes are closed in a desire to sleep, if the essence and spirit <note>original: "Jing Shen" (精神). In ancient Chinese medicine, these are the fundamental vital forces and mental faculties housed in the Heart.</note> are unsupported and the ethereal and corporeal souls <term>Ethereal and Corporeal Souls (Hun Po): The "Hun" (ethereal soul) is associated with mental activity and sleep, while the "Po" (corporeal soul) relates to physical instincts. Their disorder causes vivid, disturbing dreams or restlessness.</term> are chaotic and disordered, the Heart pulse will be deep, small, and tight. If the pulse is floating, it primarily indicates shortness of breath. 

If energy below the Heart is firm and solid and does not descend, the patient will prone to a dry throat, heat in the palms of the hands, a feeling of irritability and fullness, frequent forgetfulness, and deep sighing. This condition is caused by excessive rumination and worry.

Specific variations in the Heart pulse indicate the following conditions:
*   If the pulse is **extremely urgent**, the patient will suffer from madness and uncontrollable laughter.
*   If it is **slightly slow**, the patient will vomit blood.
*   If it is **extremely large**, the throat will become obstructed <gloss>one version says: numb</gloss>.
*   If it is **slightly large**, Heart pain will radiate to the back, and the patient will be prone to weeping.
*   If it is **extremely small**, the patient will suffer from retching and hiccups.
*   If it is **slightly small**, it indicates wasting and thirsting <gloss>one version says: numbness</gloss> <note>original: "Xiao Dan" (消癉), a condition similar to diabetes involving excessive thirst and weight loss.</note>.
*   If it is **extremely slippery**, it results in intense thirst.
*   If it is **slightly slippery**, it indicates a Heart hernia <note>original: "Xin Shan" (心疝). This refers to severe, cramp-like pain in the chest or upper abdomen that may feel like a physical mass.</note> that pulls toward the navel and abdomen <gloss>one version says: causes bowel rumbling</gloss>.
*   If it is **extremely choppy**, the patient will be mute and unable to speak.
*   If it is **slightly choppy**, blood will overflow from the vessels, the hands and feet will be cold, there will be ringing in the ears, and the patient may suffer from "epileptic-like" madness.

Furthermore, if the Heart pulse is beating forcefully, firm, and long, it indicates that the tongue is stiff and the patient cannot speak <gloss>one version says: cannot talk</gloss>. If the pulse is soft and scattered, the patient will be apprehensive and fearful and will refuse to eat.

Additionally, if the pulse is **extremely urgent**, it indicates a Heart hernia where a pathological shape can be felt below the navel; the patient feels irritable and distressed, lacks sufficient breath, and suffers from intense heat that "scortches" the upper body. In cases of Heart disease characterized by raving speech, sweat that stands out on the skin like pearls, and a cold, collapsing body, the condition is critical.

<meta>This page continues the diagnostic criteria for Heart ailments, specifically focusing on the relationship between the Heart pulse and physical manifestations, the elemental theory of "Water overcoming Fire," and the prognostic signs of "Walking Corpses" and imminent death as recorded by the physician Bian Que.</meta>

### Central Treasury Medical Classic, Volume 1

In cases of "Cold," the pulse should be floating and large. If it is instead deep, soggy, and extremely slippery, and if the complexion should be red but is now conversely black, this indicates that **Water overcomes Fire** <term>The Five Phases (Wu Xing): A system of cyclic relationships where elements control one another. Here, the Water element (associated with the Kidneys and the color black) has extinguished the Fire element (associated with the Heart and the color red). This is a fatal sign of systemic collapse.</term>. In such instances, ten out of ten will die; it cannot be treated.

Furthermore, regarding an **Accumulation of the Heart** <note>original: "Xin Zhi Ji" (心之積). In traditional pathology, this refers to a physical or energetic mass in the chest or upper abdomen, often associated with stagnant blood or congealed Qi.</note>: if you press deeply to find it, it feels hollow and empty. At times it moves up and down, coming and going with no fixed location. This illness causes a sense of fullness and palpitations in the chest, heat within the waist and abdomen, red cheeks <gloss>one version says: face</gloss>, a dry throat, irritability of the heart, and heat in the palms of the hands. In severe cases, the patient will vomit blood. This condition improves in the Summer <gloss>one version says: becomes better</gloss> but grows severe in the Winter. It should be treated urgently, for life may end within ten days.

Additionally, if a reddish-black color appears and enters the area of the mouth, the patient will surely die. "When the Heart's vitality is severed, death occurs within one day."

Within one year, disaster will surely strike them.

...this is its channel. The legendary physician Bian Que <note>Bian Que (扁鵲) is a semi-mythical figure from the 5th century BCE, often called the "God of Medicine" for his mastery of pulse diagnosis.</note> said: When the **Spleen** <term>Spleen (Pi 脾): In traditional medicine, the Spleen is the central organ of the "Earth" element, responsible for digestion, the movement of fluids, and the production of blood.</term> is diseased, the facial complexion becomes withered and yellow <note>Yellow is the color associated with the Earth element; a sallow or "withered" yellow indicates the Spleen's inability to transform and transport nutrients.</note>.

In a state of **Repletion** <note>original: "Shi" (實). A state of excess or "fullness," often involving inflammation or a blockage of energy.</note>, the tongue becomes rigid and straight, one has no appetite, suffers from vomiting and reflux, and the four limbs feel slack. 

In a state of **Vacuity** <note>original: "Xu" (虛). A state of deficiency or depletion of the body's vital resources.</note>, the **Essence** <term>Essence (Jing 精): The fundamental life-substance that supports growth, development, and reproduction.</term> cannot overcome the ailment, the **Original Qi** <term>Original Qi (Yuan Qi 元氣): The foundational energy that serves as the root of all life processes.</term> is exhausted, and one loses control over urination.

If the pulse arrives like the steady flow of water, it is termed **Excessive** <note>original: "Taiguo" (太過).</note>, indicating the illness is on the exterior of the body. If the pulse arrives like the sharp scratch of a bird’s claw <note>original: "Niao zhi ju" (鳥之距). This describes a pulse that feels sharp, abrupt, and lacking in "root" or softness.</note>, it is termed **Insufficient** <note>original: "Buji" (不及).</note>, indicating the illness is in the interior. 

When the pulse is Excessive, it causes the four limbs to feel heavy and speech to become halting and difficult. When it is Insufficient, it causes a sense of fullness in the center <note>the abdomen and epigastrium</note>, an inability to eat, a lack of physical strength, and limbs that are weak, sluggish, and unresponsive, with drool being drawn from the mouth <gloss>one version says "exiting"</gloss>. This state also leads to swelling of the limbs and loose stools <gloss>one version says "leakage"</gloss>, and the patient frequently dreams of eating and drinking.

When the Spleen pulse arrives harmoniously and softly, and leaves like a chicken’s spur gently stepping on the ground, it is called a **Balanced Pulse** <note>original: "Ping Mai" (平脉). A state of healthy, rhythmic equilibrium.</note>. If the pulse arrives full and replete, slightly rapid like a chicken lifting its foot, it is called a **Diseased Pulse**. Furthermore, if the pulse is like a bird <gloss>one version says "sparrow"</gloss> pecking, or like a bird’s sharp claw, or like water leaking through a roof <note>These are traditional "Deadly Pulses" (Si Mai), characterized by erratic or "broken" rhythms that indicate the total exhaustion of the organ's vital energy.</note>, it signifies **Death**.

In cases of **Wind-Stroke** <term>Wind-Stroke (Zhongfeng 中風): A sudden onset of illness characterized by paralysis, numbness, or loss of speech, traditionally attributed to internal or external "wind" invading the body.</term>, there is a fluttering fever, and the patient appears as if intoxicated. The abdomen feels restless and full, the skin and flesh twitch, and the breath is short...

### Pulse Manifestations and Prognosis

If the pulse is extremely **Urgent**, it leads to **Convulsions** <note>original: "Chi zong" (瘈瘲). A pattern of muscular contractions and relaxations, similar to a seizure.</note>. If it is only slightly Urgent, there is discomfort in the stomach and diaphragm; food is swallowed only to be vomited back out. 

If the pulse is **Slow and Flourishing**, it leads to **Wilting-Reversal** <term>Wilting-Reversal (Wei Jue 痿厥): A condition where the limbs become weak, useless, or atrophied, often accompanied by coldness.</term>. If it is slightly Slow, it leads to **Wind-Wilting**, where the four limbs cannot be controlled. 

If the pulse is extremely **Large**, the patient may suddenly collapse as if struck down. If it is slightly Large, it indicates **Numbness-Hernia** <note>original: "Bi Shan" (痺疝). A condition involving localized pain, numbness, and swelling, often in the lower abdomen or groin.</note>; in this case, the **Qi** is "wrapped" and enlarged, and there is pus and blood outside the stomach and intestines. 

If the pulse is extremely **Small**, chills and fever will alternate. If it is slightly Small, it leads to **Wasting-Thirst** <term>Wasting-Thirst (Xiao Dan 消癉): A traditional category of disease characterized by excessive thirst, hunger, and urination, closely corresponding to modern diabetes.</term>. 

If the pulse is extremely **Slippery**, it leads to **Drooping Hernia** <note>original: "Tui Shan" (頹疝). Typically refers to a swollen scrotum or a descending inguinal hernia.</note>. If it is slightly Slippery, it indicates **Parasite Poisoning** and rumbling sounds within the intestines.

In a medical context, it describes a specific dark, dull, greenish-yellow hue that signifies a terminal lack of vitality.</term>, death will occur within one <gloss>alternatively: "a full"</gloss> month. Auspiciousness, misfortune, prosperity, or failure <gloss>alternatively: "calamity"</gloss> are all visible in the colors that appear on their designated facial regions.

Furthermore, if the jaw is locked, the lips are black, and the four limbs feel heavy as mountains—to the point that the patient cannot lift or control them—and if urination and defecation continue without rest, and food and drink cannot be ingested, death will occur in seven days.

However, even if the lips are withered and yellow, if the voice still has modulation and resonance, the condition can be treated. If Spleen disease and **Malarial Qi** <note>original: "Nue qi" (瘧氣). Pathogenic energy responsible for symptoms like intermittent chills, fever, and abdominal distress.</note> persist for a long time without departing, causing pain and rumbling in the abdomen, and a slow, warm sweat emerges, the person’s original...

...red, it can be treated. However, if a bluish-black color enters the mouth, death will occur.

If a person's temperament is usually easygoing and patient, but they suddenly become abnormal and prone to anger, or if they snort with laughter <note>original: "bi xiao" (鼻笑). This suggests an involuntary or derisive nasal sound during serious conversation.</note> while being spoken to seriously and are unable to answer others, misfortune <note>In this context, "misfortune" refers to impending death.</note> will inevitably arrive within no more than one month.

Furthermore, when there is Cold or Heat within the **Spleen**, both conditions cause pain in the abdomen and an inability to swallow food. In cases of Spleen disease, the tongue becomes stiff and speech becomes hesitant or slurred; the patient may suffer from muscle cramps and testicular retraction <note>original: "luan suo" (卵縮). This refers to the pulling upward or shrinking of the scrotum, often associated with terminal "Liver" or "Spleen" exhaustion in ancient texts.</note> which pulls at the inner thighs and leads to pain in the hips. The body feels heavy, there is no appetite, and if abdominal distension <gloss>alternatively: "ascites" or "drum-bloat"</gloss> develops into watery diarrhea and the patient is unable to lie down, it is a fatal sign and cannot be cured.

When the Spleen is affected by true **Heat**, the face turns yellow, the eyes become red, and the lower flanks feel painful and full. When affected by **Cold**, the patient vomits saliva and froth and refuses food; the four limbs are painful, and watery diarrhea continues without ceasing. If the cold in the hands and feet is severe, the patient will tremble and shiver as if suffering from malaria.

When attending to a disease, the essential task is to clarify the symptoms and examine the pulse in detail; only then should one administer decoctions or pills to seek recovery from the damage.

## Chapter 27: Methods for Pulse and Symptoms of Stomach Deficiency, Excess, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, and Prognosis

The **Stomach** is a **Fu-organ** <term>Fu-organ (Fu 腑): In traditional medicine, these are the "hollow" organs (such as the stomach and intestines) responsible for receiving, digesting, and transmitting food and waste.</term>. It is also named the **Sea of Water and Grain** <term>Sea of Water and Grain (Shui Gu Zhi Hai 水穀之海): A metaphor for the Stomach as the primary reservoir for all nourishment consumed by the body.</term>. It stands in an **interior-exterior relationship** <term>Interior-Exterior (Biao Li 表裏): A conceptual pairing where a "hollow" Fu-organ (Stomach) is paired with a "solid" Zang-organ (Spleen), showing they share a functional and pathological connection.</term> with the Spleen. The Stomach is the root of a human being.

<summary>This page concludes the diagnostic signs of terminal Spleen disease, including behavioral changes and physical symptoms like testicular retraction. It introduces Chapter 27, defining the Stomach as the "Sea of Water and Grain" and the root of human life.</summary>
<keywords>Spleen disease, behavioral diagnostics, testicular retraction, Heat and Cold, Stomach, Sea of Water and Grain, Fu-organ, prognosis</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the diagnostic discussion of the internal organs, moving from the Spleen (the focus of the previous section) to the Stomach, which the text identifies as the "root" of the body's vitality.</meta>

...is the root. When **Stomach Qi** is robust, then all the five viscera <note>The Zang (solid) organs: Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys.</note> and six bowels <note>The Fu (hollow) organs: Gallbladder, Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Bladder, and Triple Burner.</note> are robust. The **Foot Yangming** <note>The primary acupuncture meridian or channel associated with the Stomach.</note> is its corresponding channel. When Stomach Qi is cut off, death occurs within five days.

When there is **Excess** <note>original: "shi" (實). In Chinese medicine, this refers to a state of pathological overabundance, such as a blockage or a high fever.</note>, there is distension in the middle, difficulty with bowel movements, pain in the joints of the limbs, an inability to swallow food, and incessant vomiting.

When there is **Deficiency** <note>original: "xu" (虛). A state of weakness, depletion, or lack of essential energy.</note>, there is rumbling in the intestines, abdominal fullness, a constant craving for water, and "slippery" diarrhea <gloss>involuntary or urgent watery stools</gloss>.

When there is **Cold**, there is pain within the abdomen and an inability to eat cold foods.

When there is **Heat**, the face is red like that of a drunkard, the four limbs cannot be controlled or held steady, the patient cannot sleep peacefully, the speech is manic, the eyes are wandering or disordered, and the stools are hard.

If the disease becomes severe, the abdomen and flanks feel distended and full, and there is upward vomiting such that food cannot enter. There is pain at the heart <note>This refers to the epigastric region (the "pit" of the stomach) rather than the heart organ itself.</note>, and the upper and lower paths do not communicate <gloss>meaning the digestive system is completely blocked</gloss>. The patient loathes the smell of food, dislikes the sound of people talking, shivers with cold, and has a tendency to stretch and yawn.

If there is **Heat within the Stomach**, the lips turn black. If the heat is extreme, the patient will climb to high places and sing, or strip off their clothes and run about <note>These "manic" behaviors were classic diagnostic markers for extreme heat in the Yangming (Stomach) channel in ancient clinical texts.</note>. They are unstable and delirious; sweat breaks out on the forehead, and nosebleeds <term>nü (衄): bleeding from the nose or mucus membranes without external injury</term> do not stop.

If **Deficiency is extreme**, the four limbs become swollen and full, there is shortness of breath within the stomach, and grains <note>referring to all food and nourishment</note> are not digested; this is known as **Middle Wasting** <note>original: "zhong xiao" (中消). A wasting disease characterized by rapid hunger and weight loss, similar to modern descriptions of diabetes.</note>.

If **Wind** enters the Stomach, there is incessant watery diarrhea. If the Stomach is **insufficient**, there is frequent hunger, but...

For a patient with wasting and thirst <note>Often referring to "Xiao Ke" or "Wasting and Thirsting" disorder, similar to modern diabetes, or general digestive depletion.</note>, if the area below the nose is flat and smooth, the stomach is diseased. If the patient is thirsty, the condition is incurable. <gloss>One version lacks the thirteen characters above and instead reads:</gloss> If there is slight dryness and thirst, the condition can be treated. <gloss>If the Stomach pulse is striking, hard, and long, and the complexion is yellowish-red, the patient will suffer from a "broken waist" <gloss>one version says "thigh"</gloss>.</gloss>

If the pulse is soft and scattered, the disease is "Food Blockage" <term>Shi Bi: a condition where food does not digest properly, leading to obstruction and pain</term>. If the pulse at the **Left Guan** position <note>The middle pulse position on the wrist. While usually associated with the Liver on the left hand, traditional texts occasionally vary in their associations.</note> is floating and large, it indicates **Deficiency**.

If it is floating, short, and rough, it indicates **Excess**. If it is floating and slightly slippery, it also indicates **Deficiency**. If it is floating and slow, it indicates **Cold**. If it is floating and rapid, it indicates **Excess**. If the methods for discerning Deficiency, Excess, Cold, Heat, life, and death are observed with extreme care and sincerity, one’s diagnostic skill will become divinely subtle.

## Section 28: On the methods of Pulse Diagnosis and Symptoms for Deficiency, Excess, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, and Favorable or Adverse conditions of the Lung Viscus

The **Lungs** are the dwelling place of the **Po** <note>The "corporeal soul" or "physical soul" in Chinese thought, which governs bodily instincts and physical form, in contrast to the "Hun" or spiritual soul.</note> and the source of the "Engendering Qi" <note>The vital breath that creates and sustains life.</note>. They are known as the **Supreme General** and serve as the **Flowery Canopy** <note>The "Hua Gai," a metaphor comparing the Lungs to a ceremonial umbrella or canopy that sits at the top of the body's internal organs to protect them.</note> of the five viscera.

<summary>This page concludes the pulse-based diagnosis for the Stomach and begins a new section on the Lungs, describing them as the physical soul's home and the body's protective canopy.</summary>
<keywords>Pulse Classic, Stomach pulse, Food Blockage, Lung Viscus, Po soul, Flowery Canopy, Deficiency and Excess, diagnostic methods</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Section 28 regarding the Lungs, specifically detailing the physiological roles of the Lung viscus, its diagnostic manifestations in the nose and skin, and the specific pulse qualities that indicate health or disease during the autumn season.</meta>

It externally nourishes the skin and hair, and internally enriches the intestines and stomach. It stands in an "exterior-interior" relationship with the Large Intestine <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, "Interior-Exterior" (Biao-Li) relationships link a Yin organ with a Yang organ; the Lungs (Yin) and Large Intestine (Yang) are paired.</note>. The **Hand Taiyin** <term>Hand Taiyin: The Lung Meridian, one of the twelve primary channels through which Qi flows.</term> is its primary channel. 

The **Lung Qi** communicates through the nose; when in harmony, one can distinguish between fragrant and foul odors. If there is **Cold**, one is prone to coughing <gloss>One version says "If there is illness"</gloss>; if diseased, one will frequently cough. If the condition is one of **Excess**, the nose will run with clear mucus. 

Generally, whether the condition is **Deficiency**, **Excess**, **Cold**, or **Heat**, all will cause a person to suffer from wheezing and coughing. In cases of **Excess**, the patient may dream of weapons and warfare <note>Dreams were considered diagnostic indicators; dreams of metal weapons relate to the "Metal" element associated with the Lungs.</note>, experience fear, breathe with labored shoulders <term>Shoulder breathing: a sign of respiratory distress where the shoulders rise and fall significantly with each breath</term>, and feel a fullness in the chest. 

If there is **Deficiency**, Cold arises <gloss>One version says "Heat," another says "Cough"</gloss>, accompanied by shortness of breath, loose stools <note>The original "li xia" suggests a downward leak, often linking Lung deficiency to a lack of control over the bowels.</note>, a lack of physical strength, and a tendency toward sadness and grief <note>Sadness is the emotion traditionally associated with the Lungs.</note>. 

The Lung governs during the **Autumn**. When its pulse is floating and "feather-soft" <term>Mao: a "hair-like" or "downy" pulse characteristic of the autumn season</term>, it is called **Balanced** (Normal). Furthermore, a pulse that is floating, short, and rough is characteristic of the Lung. 

If the pulse arrives feather-soft but is firm in the center while the ends <gloss>One version says "sides"</gloss> are weak, this is called **Excessive**; the disease is located in the **Exterior**. If the pulse arrives feather-soft but is faint, this is called **Insufficient**; the disease is located in the **Interior**. 

**Excess** causes the Qi to move upward against its proper flow <note>Rebellious Qi: Lung Qi should normally descend; when it "rebels" upward, it causes coughing or chest pressure.</note>, resulting in a full sensation in the chest and pain in the back. **Insufficiency** causes the patient to wheeze and cough <gloss>One version says "dry cough"</gloss>; there is upward-rushing Qi, the presence of blood <note>This likely refers to hemoptysis, or coughing up blood.</note>, and labored sounds can be heard from the chest. Furthermore, the Lung pulse may feel "faint and fluttering" <term>Yan yan nie nie: an onomatopoeic description of a pulse that is soft, rhythmic, and light</term>, resembling the falling of elm leaves.

<summary>This page describes the diagnostic signs of the Lungs, including how its health manifests in the sense of smell and the skin, and details how "Excess" and "Deficiency" lead to symptoms like coughing, grief, and specific pulse patterns.</summary>
<keywords>Lung Qi, Hand Taiyin, pulse diagnosis, respiratory symptoms, Five Phases (Metal), Autumn pulse, seasonal medicine</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific diagnostic details for the Lungs (Section 28), shifting from general physiology to the specific "Lung Pulse" and the prognostic signs of life and death through the lens of the Five Elements.</meta>

It is said that a healthy pulse arrives without moving up or down, feeling like one is stroking chicken feathers. A diseased pulse is said to feel like an object floating, or like the wind blowing the feathers on a bird’s back; this signifies death. 

The **True Lung Pulse** <term>True visceral pulse: A pulse that lacks "Stomach Qi," indicating that the organ's energy is exhausted and death is imminent.</term> is extremely large and empty; it also feels like touching a person's skin with feathers. If the patient's complexion is red and their hair is brittle or breaking, it signifies death. 

A pulse that is slightly "hair-like" <note>original: 毛 "mao." A floating, light pulse quality associated with the Lungs and the season of Autumn.</note> is considered healthy. If the hair-like quality is excessive, it indicates disease. If the pulse is both hair-like and wiry <term>Wiry pulse: A pulse that feels taut, like a musical string; usually associated with the Liver.</term>, it indicates a disease originating in the Spring. If the wiriness is extreme, the illness is immediate. 

Furthermore, in Lung diseases where there is vomiting of blood or nosebleeds, feverish skin, a rapid pulse, and red cheeks, the patient will die. 

In cases of chronic coughing accompanied by blood, body fever, and shortness of breath, the pulse should naturally be choppy <term>Choppy pulse: A rough, hesitant pulse likened to a knife scraping bamboo; it indicates blood deficiency or stagnant Qi.</term>. If it is instead floating and large—and if the complexion, which should be white <note>White is the color associated with the Lungs and the Metal element.</note>, is instead red—this is a case of **Fire overcoming Metal** <note>In the Five Elements cycle, Fire (Heart/Red) acts upon and destroys Metal (Lungs/White). This "overwhelming" or "insulting" sequence is a grave diagnostic sign.</note>. In ten out of ten such cases, the patient will die and cannot be treated.

When a Lung disease presents with wheezing and coughing, but the body is only cold without fever, and the pulse is slow and faint, the condition is treatable. 

During Autumn, the Lungs are at their peak <note>original: 王 "wang," meaning to rule or flourish.</note>. The pulse should naturally be floating, choppy, and short; this is considered healthy. However, if the pulse is instead surging, large, and long, this is "Fire punishing Metal," and it is also incurable. 

If the pulse is found to be soft and slippery, this indicates the "Kidney coming to support the Lungs" <gloss>One version says "Kidney Qi riding the Lungs"</gloss>; the condition will heal on its own without treatment. If, on the other hand, the pulse is floating and large...

### The Pulse Classic, Part One

If the pulse is slow, this indicates that the Spleen is "generating" the Lungs <note>In the Five Elements cycle, the Spleen (Earth) is the "mother" of the Lungs (Metal). This is a nourishing, harmonious relationship.</note>; in such cases, the patient will recover without specific treatment. Conversely, if the pulse is wiry and long, it indicates the Lungs are being "followed" by the Liver <note>original: 肝從 "gan cong." This refers to the Liver (Wood) exerting a mild, inverse influence on the Lungs (Metal). Usually, Metal controls Wood; this reversal is considered a "tiny evil" or a minor disharmony.</note>; while the patient is ill, it is not life-threatening. 

When the Lungs are deficient, the patient cannot catch their breath, the ears feel heavy, the throat is dry, and there is panting, coughing, and "ascending Qi" <term>Ascending Qi: A condition where the body's energy flows upward abnormally, causing chest fullness, coughing, or dizziness.</term>. There is also pain in the back. If there is an accumulation of stagnant energy, the area below the ribs will feel bloated and full. 

If the patient is "struck by wind" <note>A traditional category of illness caused by external environmental factors (Wind-strike), often presenting like a sudden flu or a stroke.</note>, the mouth will be dry, they will pant, the body will feel heavy or dizzy, they will sweat, and the stomach will feel oppressed. If the pulse, when pressed, is weak and hollow—like an onion leaf with no root beneath it—it signifies death.

In cases of "internal heat," the patient will spit blood. If the pulse is fine, tight, floating, rapid, hollow, or slippery, these are all signs of blood-loss disease. This condition is caused by dryness, agitation, anger, or exhaustion, which leads to the congestion and "knotting" of the body's Qi.

In **Lung Distention** <note>original: 肺脹 "fei zhang." A condition similar to modern emphysema or chronic bronchitis where the lungs feel over-full.</note>, the person pants and coughs, and their eyes look as if they are bulging out; a pulse that is floating and large confirms this. 

Furthermore, in **Lung Atrophy** <term>Lung Atrophy (Fei Wei): A chronic, wasting lung disease characterized by the loss of fluids, leading to the "wilting" of lung tissue.</term>, the patient spits out saliva and foam and has a dry throat. If the patient desires to drink, they will recover; if they do not wish to drink, they have not yet healed. 

Also, if a patient coughs and experiences involuntary urination, it is because the "upper" is too weak to control the "lower" <note>The Lungs are the "Upper Source of Water" in Chinese medicine; if they are weak, they cannot regulate the bladder correctly.</note>. If the pulse is deep and turbid, the disease is located deep within the body; if the pulse is floating and clear, the disease is on the exterior.

<keywords>Lung Pulse, Spleen-Lung relationship, Lung Atrophy, Lung Distention, Wind-strike, Five Elements, Blood-loss, Prognosis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the clinical assessment of Lung conditions from the previous page, shifting from pulse diagnosis to the physical signs of terminal "Lung death," environmental triggers like wind and alcohol, and the fatal progression of Heat within the Lungs.</meta>

### The Pulse Classic, Part One

When the Lungs are in a state of "death" <note>In this context, "death" (si) refers to the total cessation of the organ's functional energy (Qi), representing the terminal stage of a disease.</note>, the nostrils flare and appear blackened and withered; the patient pants for breath, and their eyes are fixed in a straight stare. Furthermore, when the Lung energy is utterly exhausted, death will occur within twelve days. The symptoms include swelling of the feet and involuntary diarrhea or dysentery that occurs without the patient's awareness. If the face is white while the eyes appear blue-green, this is called "disordered meridians" <note>White is the color associated with the Lungs (Metal), while blue-green is associated with the Liver (Wood). This specific combination suggests a chaotic internal struggle between the organs.</note>. Even if this is considered the "mandate of Heaven" <note>original: 天命 (tianming). This implies a natural end to one’s lifespan that cannot be altered by human intervention.</note>, it cannot be treated.

If a person drinks alcohol and is then exposed to the wind, the "wind-evil" can strike the Lungs. This causes coughing, wheezing, and a feeling of oppression in the chest. If blood is seen (in the phlegm), it cannot be treated; if no blood is present, it is treatable. If the face is yellowish and the eyes are white, the condition is treatable; however, if a patient with Lung disease has bright red cheeks, it signifies death <note>Red is the color of Fire. In the Five Elements cycle, Fire "melts" Metal (the Lungs), so red cheeks in a Lung patient indicate that Heart-fire is destroying the Lungs.</note>. 

Furthermore, if the voice is rapid and wheezing, the breath is short, and the patient is prone to spitting <gloss>one version says "sleeping"</gloss>, these are signs of "True Ghost Harm" <note>original: 真鬼相害 (zhen gui xiang hai). A traditional term for a terminal condition where the body’s vital essence is so depleted that it appears as if malevolent forces are taking over.</note>. In such cases, ten out of ten—or one hundred out of one hundred—will die. This is an omen of "Great Rebellion" <term>Great Rebellion: A severe pathological reversal where the body’s energy flows in the opposite direction of health, leading to certain death.</term>.

When the *Yang Qi* <term>Yang Qi: The active, warming, and upward-moving energy of the body.</term> ascends but fails to descend, it "burns" the Lungs. The Lungs then become knotted with "evil energy," leading to bloating, fullness, and rapid panting. The patient may speak deliriously, keep their eyes closed, and say things that are entirely out of character. The mouth and nostrils will remain open, the abdomen and forehead will be distended, and the patient will drink water without limit. This is caused by Heat injuring the Lungs, causing the Lung tissue to "turn into blood." This cannot be treated, and the patient will die within half a year. 

Additionally, "Lung Malaria" <note>A traditional category of illness characterized by periodic chills and fever where the primary symptoms—specifically intense cold—manifest in the Lungs.</note> causes the person’s heart to feel cold, and this cold can become extreme...

<summary>This page details the terminal physical signs of Lung failure, including facial discoloration and respiratory distress, while explaining how Heat and "Wind-strike" after drinking can lead to fatal outcomes.</summary>
<keywords>Lung Death, terminal symptoms, Five Elements colors, Wind-strike, Heat injury, Lung Malaria, Hua Tuo, Chinese medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the clinical assessment of Lung conditions from the previous page, specifically detailing the symptoms of "Lung malaria" and the differentiation between "Excess" and "Deficiency" states in Lung disease, before transitioning to the Large Intestine.</meta>

...[then] there will be fever, or alternating chills and fever that occur at irregular intervals. This is accompanied by frequent fright, coughing, wheezing, and hallucinations <note>Original: 如有所見者 (ru you suo jian zhe), literally "as if something is seen." This suggests the patient is seeing visions or delirious due to the severity of the illness.</note>. This condition is Lung Malaria <term>Fei Nue: A specific classification of "malaria-like" disorders in traditional medicine where the pathogenic factors have settled in the Lung system, causing respiratory distress alongside the typical alternating fevers.</term>. Its pulse may be floating and tight, or slippery and rapid, or slow, choppy, and small; these are all characteristic pulses of Lung Malaria.

Furthermore, if a person whose voice is normally clear and resonant suddenly loses its resonance, and they must labor and strain their breath to speak, making words difficult to produce, while their eyes stare fixedly without moving—even if they do not yet seem "sick," that person will not live long. 

In Lung disease:
**Excess** <term>Shi: A state where pathogenic energy is overabundant, often presenting with acute, "heavy," or forceful symptoms.</term> is characterized by "surging Qi" <note>Qi moving upward against its natural downward flow, which should be toward the Kidneys.</note>, urgent wheezing, coughing, a feverish body, and a large pulse.
**Deficiency** <term>Xu: A state of exhaustion, weakness, or insufficiency of the body's vital energy (Qi) or fluids.</term> is characterized by a lack of physical strength, gasping for breath, a feeling of fullness or distension in the right flank, and shortness of breath <gloss>one version says "urgent breath"</gloss> when speaking.
Additionally, the alternating sensation of cold and heat, a blocked nose, a reddened forehead, and a pale white face are all diagnostic signs of Lung disease.

# Chapter 20: Methods for Assessing Large Intestine Pulses and Signs: Excess and Deficiency, Cold and Heat, Life and Death, and Favorable or Adverse Progressions

<summary>This page concludes the diagnostic criteria for Lung disorders, distinguishing between "Lung malaria" and the physical signs of respiratory Excess and Deficiency, then introduces the section on the Large Intestine.</summary>
<keywords>Lung Malaria (Fei Nue), Excess and Deficiency (Shi and Xu), pulse diagnosis, Large Intestine, respiratory failure, Hua Tuo</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the pathology of the Lungs to its paired "hollow" organ, the Large Intestine. It maintains the diagnostic framework of Excess and Deficiency established on the previous page to explain intestinal disorders and concludes the first volume of the treatise.</meta>

The Large Intestine is the *Fu* <term>Fu: In traditional Chinese medicine, these are the "hollow" organs (such as the stomach and intestines) that process and transport substances, paired with the "Zang" or solid, storage organs.</term> of the Lungs. It serves as the Office of Transmission and is known as the Official of Granary Oversight <note>Original: 監倉之官 (jian cang zhi guan). This title reflects the organ’s role in managing the "granary" of the body by overseeing the final passage and exit of waste.</note>. If a disease of the Lungs does not cease, it is then transmitted into the Large Intestine. Hand Yangming <note>Hand Yangming is the specific meridian or energetic pathway associated with the Large Intestine.</note> is its primary meridian. 

When there is Cold, there is diarrhea; when there is Heat, there is binding <note>Constipation or "knotted" stools.</note>. If the organ's energy is exhausted, there will be unceasing diarrhea; when the diarrhea finally stops <gloss>because the body’s vitality is spent</gloss>, the person dies. 

Extreme Heat leads to blood in the stool. Furthermore, if "Wind" <term>Wind: One of the "Six Evils" or external pathogens; in this context, it refers to a sudden, destabilizing influence that causes acute bleeding.</term> strikes the Large Intestine, there is likewise a discharge of blood from below. In cases of Excess Heat, there is distension and fullness, and the stool will not pass. In cases of Deficiency Cold, there is "slippery" diarrhea <note>Involuntary or extremely loose stools that cannot be "held" by the body's weakened Qi.</note> that is erratic. 

The state of the Large Intestine may suddenly shift from deficiency to excess, or the symptoms may suddenly appear and then vanish. Cold results in loose, watery stools. Heat results in foul, heavy discharge <note>Original: 垢重 (gou zhong), referring to stools that are thick, greasy, or particularly malodorous due to inflammatory heat.</note>. If there are accumulated materials <gloss>such as stagnant food or parasites</gloss>, there will be shivering and fever, appearing much like the symptoms of malaria. 

If accumulated Cold does not leave the body, there will be pain centered at the navel, and the patient will be unable to stand for long. When the pain finally subsides, it is followed by the excretion of white matter <note>Likely referring to mucus or undigested lipids, a sign of "Cold" and "Dampness" in the digestive system.</note>. If the organ is in a state of Deficiency, the patient will be prone to feelings of fullness, wheezing, and coughing, and there will be a sensation in the throat as if a pit <note>Original: 如核 (ru he). This is a classic description of "Plum-pit Qi," a sensation of a physical blockage in the throat that can neither be swallowed nor coughed up, often linked to emotional stagnation or Qi imbalance.</note> is causing a blockage.

# Master Hua’s Central Treasury Medical Classic: Middle Volume

## Written by Hua Tuo (Yuanhua) of Qiao County, Han Dynasty

### Section 30: On the Diagnostic Methods for the Pulse and Symptoms of the Kidney Organ regarding Deficiency, Excess, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, and Favorable or Adverse conditions.

The Kidneys are the residence of the Essence and Spirit <note>Original: 精神 (jingshen). In Chinese medicine, "Essence" (Jing) is the physical basis of life stored in the kidneys, while "Spirit" (Shen) refers to consciousness and vitality.</note> and the root of life itself. They open externally to the ears. In men, they are used for storage <gloss>one version says "Essence"</gloss>; in women, they contain the blood <note>This refers to the kidneys' role in governing the reproductive system and the "Uterus" (Bao), which is linked to the kidney's energetic function.</note>. They stand in an "interior-exterior" relationship with the Bladder. The Foot Shaoyin <term>Shaoyin: The "Lesser Yin" channel, which is the energetic pathway specifically associated with the Kidneys.</term> and Foot Taiyang <term>Taiyang: The "Great Yang" channel, associated with the Bladder; these two form a functional pair.</term> are their primary meridians. 

When the Kidney Qi is exhausted, a person cannot fulfill their natural lifespan and will die. The Kidneys flourish during the Winter. During this season, their pulse is "submerged and soft" <note>Original: 沉濡 (chen ru). A "submerged" pulse is felt only with deep pressure, and a "soft" pulse feels like silk floating on water; this is considered healthy and "even" during the winter months.</note>; this is called a balanced or "even" state. 

Anything contrary to this indicates illness. If the pulse feels like "pellets striking stone" <note>Original: 彈石 (danshi). This describes a pulse that is hard, tight, and lacks any springiness or "root," usually indicating a severe or terminal condition.</note>, it is called a state of Excess. In such cases, the illness is on the exterior. If the pulse leaves as if it were rapid <note>Suggesting it lacks substance or strength.</note>, it is a state of Deficiency or Insufficiency; in such cases, the illness is in the interior. 

A state of Excess causes a person to feel weary and dispirited <note>Original: 解㑊 (jiexie). A specific type of physical and mental lassitude where the limbs feel weak.</note>, with pain along the spinal vessels and shortness of breath. <gloss>One version says: "makes the person's body emaciated and..."</gloss> 

A state of Deficiency causes the person to feel as if their heart is suspended, similar to the sensation of hunger. There will be a feeling of coldness in the flanks and loins <note>Original: 眇中 (miaozhong), referring to the area of the small of the back or flanks.</note>, shortness of breath within the spine, and a lack of desire to speak.

<summary>This page introduces the Kidney organ as the "root of life," explaining its physiological connections to the ears, the Bladder, and the seasons. It defines the healthy "submerged and soft" pulse of winter versus the pathological pulses of Excess and Deficiency.</summary>
<keywords>Kidneys, Hua Tuo, Pulse Diagnosis, Winter, Shaoyin, Essence, Spirit, Deficiency and Excess</keywords>

...[if there is] pain, the lower abdomen is full, and urination is slippery <gloss>one version says "the heart feels as if it is suspended"</gloss>, and the urine turns a reddish-yellow color.

Furthermore, if the Kidney pulse comes "panting and panting" <note>Original: 喘喘 (chuan chuan). This describes a rapid, labored, or gasping pulse quality.</note>, continuous like a hook, and feels firm when pressed, this is called "balanced" <note>In this context, a firm, deep quality is considered normal for the Kidney organ, which is associated with the element of Water and the depth of Winter.</note>.

If the pulse comes like pulling on a creeping vine and becomes even firmer when pressed, it is called "diseased." If it comes like a twisting rope or sharp and hard like flicking a stone, it is called "death" <note>These are part of the "Seven Death Pulses," indicating that the organ's essential energy is scattered or exhausted.</note>.

Moreover, if the Kidney pulse is merely "stone-like" <note>A hard, deep pulse.</note> without any "Stomach Qi," the patient will also die. <term>Stomach Qi: In traditional pulse diagnosis, this refers to a gentle, harmonious, and resilient quality that shows the body still possesses the fundamental energy to process nutrients and heal.</term>

If the Kidneys contain excess "water" <note>Likely referring to edema or fluid retention caused by kidney failure.</note>, the abdomen becomes large, the navel swells, and the lower back feels heavy and painful. The patient is unable to urinate, the area beneath the genitals is damp, and sweat breaks out like the moisture on a bull's nose; this is known as "adverse cold." In such cases, defecation is difficult, yet the patient’s face, conversely, becomes thin and gaunt.

In Kidney disease, the hands and feet are "reversely cold" <note>A condition where the extremities are icy while the core may be distressed.</note>, the face appears red, the eyes are yellow, and urination is incontinent. The joints are vexingly painful, there is knotted pain in the lower abdomen, and Qi surges upward to strike the heart.

The pulse should naturally be submerged, thin, and slippery. If it is instead floating, large, and slow, it is a sign of trouble. The complexion should be black; if the color changes to something else, it is a sign that "Earth is overcoming Water." <term>Earth overcoming Water: According to the Five Phases theory, the Spleen (Earth) suppresses the Kidney (Water). If the Spleen's pathology dominates the Kidney's, it represents a "destructive cycle" that is highly dangerous.</term> This is a "great adverse" sign; ten out of ten such cases are untreatable and will result in death.

Furthermore, in Kidney disease where the facial color is black, the Qi is deficient and weak, the breathing is shallow and rapid, the two ears seem deaf, and the essence...

### Pulse Classic, Volume 4

...[the patient] naturally eats and drinks very little. The urine is clear, and the area below the knees is cold. If the pulse is deep, slippery, and slow, the condition is considered treatable.

Furthermore, in winter, a pulse that is deep, soft, and slippery is called "balanced" <note>In the system of the Five Elements, the Kidney is associated with Water and the season of Winter; therefore, a deep pulse mirroring the "hidden" nature of winter is considered healthy.</note>. Conversely, if the pulse is floating, rough, and short, it indicates the Lung is "overcoming" the Kidney; although the patient is ill, it will be easy to cure <note>original: 肺來乘腎 (fei lai cheng shen). In the Five Elements cycle, the Lung (Metal) is the "mother" of the Kidney (Water). A mother organ's influence on its child is generally seen as a supportive or manageable pathological shift.</note>. If the pulse is instead wiry, thin, and long, it indicates the Liver is "overcoming" the Kidney; this will heal on its own without treatment. If the pulse is floating, large, and flooding, it indicates the Heart is "overcoming" the Kidney; this does not cause harm.

In Kidney disease, the abdomen becomes large and the shins swell. There is panting and coughing, the body feels heavy, and night sweats occur. The patient has an aversion to wind. If there is a deficiency, there will be pain within the stomach, as well as pain in the upper and lower abdomen. One experiences <term>Cold Reversal: A condition where the limbs become icy cold because the body’s internal heat (Yang Qi) fails to reach the extremities.</term> and a general feeling of unhappiness.

When "Yin Evil" <note>Refers to pathogenic cold or dampness that invades the body's internal systems.</note> enters the Kidneys, it causes bone pain. A pulling sensation travels from the lower back up to the neck, and the spine and back ache. These conditions are all caused by lifting heavy weights with great effort, as well as excessive sexual activity, sweating and then being exposed to wind or bathing in cold water, or standing for too long; all of these actions damage the Kidneys.

Furthermore, if the Kidney pulse is extremely "urgent" <note>Tense or tight like a strained cord.</note>, it indicates "Kidney Wilting" <note>A condition involving weakness, wasting, or paralysis of the lower limbs.</note> or abdominal masses. If it is only slightly urgent, it indicates deep reversal and <term>Running Piglet: A subjective sensation of gas or energy rushing upward from the lower abdomen to the chest or throat, often accompanied by panic or heart palpitations.</term>, as well as an inability to control the movement of the feet.

If the pulse is extremely "relaxed" <note>Sluggish or loose.</note>, it feels as if the spine is breaking. If it is slightly relaxed, there is "through-leakage" <note>Severe diarrhea where food is passed completely undigested.</note>; food is not transformed, and what enters the throat is immediately vomited back out. If the pulse is extremely large, it indicates <term>Impotence: Known in the text as "Yin Wilting," the loss of sexual function or the inability to achieve an erection.</term> If it is slightly large...

### Central Treasury Medical Classic, Volume 4

<term>Stone Water: A severe form of edema where the swelling is firm and hard, like stone, and does not yield easily to pressure.</term> begins below the navel and extends to the lower abdomen. If the swelling appears heavy and drooping, and rises as far as the <term>Gastric Cavity: The region of the upper abdomen corresponding to the stomach.</term>, the condition is fatal and cannot be treated.

If the pulse is extremely small, there will be <term>Cave Diarrhea: Profuse, watery diarrhea that passes through the body as if through a hollow cave.</term>. If it is only slightly small, there will be <term>Wasting-Thirst: A condition characterized by intense thirst and the "wasting away" of the body, often compared to modern diabetes.</term>. 

If the pulse is extremely slippery, there will be urinary blockage or convulsions. If it is slightly slippery, there will be <term>Bone Wilting: A condition where the bones and joints become so weak and brittle that they cannot support the body.</term>; the patient will be unable to stand up from a sitting position, and their vision will be filled with "flowers" <note>This refers to visual disturbances such as spots, floaters, or blurred patterns caused by internal deficiency.</note>. 

If the pulse is extremely rough, there is a major internal obstruction. If it is only slightly rough, it results in the absence of menstruation or the development of hemorrhoids.

Furthermore, regarding the arrival of the pulse: if it feels hard and large in the upper position, it indicates the presence of <term>Accumulation Vapor: The energetic precursor to physical masses or tumors.</term> within the genitals and the abdomen. This is called <term>Kidney Bi: A "painful impediment" where cold or dampness obstructs the Kidney's energy, leading to pain or numbness.</term>. One contracts this condition by bathing in cold water and then immediately lying down to sleep.

If the pulse arrives deep, large, and hard, or floating and tight, and is accompanied by swelling in the hands, feet, or bones, then the <term>Faint Yin: Refers to the Liver and Kidney channels, which govern the lower body and reproductive health.</term> is wilted and cannot rise <note>This is a traditional reference to impotence or erectile dysfunction.</note>. There will be pain in the lower back and spine, swelling in the lower abdomen, and "water vapor" below the heart, causing periodic bloating and profuse diarrhea. These symptoms are all contracted by engaging in sexual intercourse after bathing, while the body is not yet dry.

When the Kidney is in a state of deficiency, one dreams of boats or of people drowning. If the dream occurs during the Kidney's dominant season <note>Winter, the season associated with the Water element and the Kidney.</note>, one dreams of being submerged in water and feeling great fear. 

If the Kidney is in a state of "excess" or "repletion," one dreams that the lower back and spine are breaking apart and no longer connected to each other. When <term>Reversal Evil: Pathogenic energy that flows in the wrong direction, disrupting the natural cycle of the body.</term> lodges in the Kidney, one dreams of standing on the edge of a deep abyss.

This describes a 'Deep' or 'Hidden' pulse that can only be felt by pressing firmly through the muscle to the bone, indicating the disease is located deep in the internal organs.</note>.

The Bladder is the <term>Storehouse of Body Fluids: The organ responsible for managing the transformation and excretion of liquids.</term> and shares an interior-exterior relationship with the Kidneys <note>In traditional medicine, the Kidney (Yin) and Bladder (Yang) form a functional pair associated with the Water element.</note>. It is titled the "Official of the Water Department" <note>original: "水曹掾" (Shuicao Yuan). Ancient medical texts often used bureaucratic titles to describe the "duties" of organs.</note> and is also known as the "Jade Sea." The <term>Foot Greater Yang: One of the twelve primary meridians, which runs from the eyes, over the head, and down the back to the feet.</term> is its associated meridian. 

It generally connects with all five of the <term>Bowels: The "Fu" or hollow organs, including the Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Gallbladder, and Bladder.</term>; therefore, when the five bowels are diseased, the symptoms reflect in the Bladder. Conversely, when the Bladder is diseased, the symptoms reflect in the <term>Uterine Enclosure: A term referring to the bladder sac or the womb region.</term>. When damaged by Heat, urination becomes difficult; [the presence of] Heat...

In cases of <term>Deficiency: A state of weakened vital energy or depleted substance within an organ.</term>, the energy is drawn upward and becomes trapped in the Lungs.

If this energy becomes "rebellious" <note>Referring to energy flowing in the wrong direction, such as upward when it should naturally descend.</note>, then all systems will fall into disorder. 

If the Bladder contains <term>Jueyin: Known as "Terminal Yin," the deepest level of the body's energetic layers; imbalances here often involve deep-seated cold or emotional distress.</term> energy, one's dreams will be unpleasant and fraught with a sense of restriction. When the Bladder is distended, urination will be obstructed, and there will be a heavy, oppressive sensation below the navel, or perhaps pain in the shoulders.

Approximately 3:00 AM to 5:00 AM, the time associated with the shift in the body's daily energy cycle.</note>.
