# **Section 30: On the Methods of Pulse and Symptoms for Deficiency, Excess, Cold, Heat, Life, Death, Reversal, and Smoothness of the Triple Burner**

The <term>Triple Burner: An invisible functional system in traditional medicine that coordinates the movement of water and energy through the upper, middle, and lower regions of the body.</term> is the <term>Qi: The vital life force or energy that flows through the body.</term> of the Three Primordials <note>original: "三元" (Sanyuan). Refers to the foundational energies of the heavens, earth, and humanity, here manifested within the body's three functional divisions.</note> in man. It is known as the **Viscus of Central Purity**. It presides over the five *Zang* <note>The solid organs: Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys.</note> and the six *Fu* <note>The hollow organs: Gallbladder, Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Bladder, and the Triple Burner itself.</note>, as well as the <term>Nutritive and Defensive Energies: "Ying" (Nutritive) flows within the vessels to nourish the body, while "Wei" (Defensive) flows outside the vessels to protect against illness.</term>, the <term>Meridians and Collaterals: The network of pathways through which Qi and blood circulate.</term>, and the *Qi* of the internal, external, left, right, upper, and lower regions of the body. 

When the Triple Burner is clear and unobstructed, then the internal, external, left, right, upper, and lower aspects of the body are all in communication. In terms of irrigating the entire frame, harmonizing the interior, regulating the exterior, nourishing the left, guiding the right, disseminating the upper, and draining the lower—nothing is greater than this. 

It is also known as the **Jade Sea** and the **Water Path**. Above, it is called the "Three Tubes"; in the middle, it is named "Sudden Turmoil" <note>original: "霍亂" (Huoluan). While this term often refers to the disease of sudden vomiting and diarrhea, here it is used as a functional name for the middle burner's role in the turbulent transformation of food and drink.</note>; below, it is called the "Path of Sustenance." Although there are three names, they return to a single unity. It is a system that possesses a name but has no physical form. 

It is also known as the **Solitary Viscus** <note>original: "孤獨之腑" (Gudu zhi fu). It is called "solitary" because, unlike the other hollow organs which are paired with a solid organ, the Triple Burner encompasses the entire torso and coordinates all the other organs.</note>. The Defensive energy emerges from the Upper Burner, while the Nutritive energy emerges from the Middle Burner. The Upper is the system of the collateral vessels; the Middle is the system of the main meridians; the Lower is the [origin of] water... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

...resulting in drum-like abdominal distension. If the **Lower Burner** suffers from **Excess Heat**, then urination is blocked and defecation is difficult, characterized by distressing heaviness and pain. If there is **Deficiency Cold**, then both the stool and urine will leak downward uncontrollably. When the Qi of the **Triple Burner** is in harmony, then the interior and exterior of the body are in harmony; if it is rebellious, then the interior and exterior will be in disorder. Thus it is said: "The Triple Burner is the source of a person's Triple Primal Qi." <note>original: "三元之氣" (Sanyuan zhi qi). This refers to the primary, foundational energy that sustains life, categorized here into three aspects linked to the Three Burners.</note> Therefore, one should diligently cultivate and maintain it.

## Chapter 33: On Bi-Impediment Syndrome

**Bi-Impediment** <term>Bi (痹): Often translated as "obstruction" or "rheumatism," it refers to conditions where the flow of Qi and blood is blocked by environmental pathogens, causing pain, numbness, or heaviness.</term> is caused when the energies of Wind, Cold, Summer-Heat, and Dampness strike a person's <term>Zang-Fu: The "Zang" (solid, yin) and "Fu" (hollow, yang) internal organs.</term>. When it enters the **Fu** (hollow) organs, the illness is shallow and easy to treat; when it enters the **Zang** (solid) organs, the illness is deep and difficult to treat. 

There are types known as **Wind Bi**, **Cold Bi**, **Damp Bi**, **Heat Bi**, and **Qi Bi**. Furthermore, there are the **Five Bi-Impediments** of the sinews, bones, blood, flesh, and Qi. Generally, when the pathogens of Wind, Cold, Summer-Heat, or Dampness enter the **Liver**, it is called **Sinew Bi**; when they enter the **Kidney**, it is called **Bone Bi**.

<summary>This page concludes the study of the Triple Burner's pathologies and introduces Bi-Impediment syndrome (rheumatism/obstruction), classifying it by environmental cause and the depth of the organ affected.</summary>
<keywords>Lower Burner, Triple Primal Qi, Bi-Impediment, Zang-Fu organs, Sinew Bi, Bone Bi, environmental pathogens</keywords>

## The Central Treasury Classic: Volume Two

...If the pathogen enters the **Heart**, it is called **Blood-Impediment**. If it enters the **Spleen**, it is called **Flesh-Impediment**. If it enters the **Lung**, it is called **Qi-Impediment**. <note>original: "入於心則名血痹入於脾則名肉痹入於肺則名氣痹". **Bi** means "closure" or "obstruction." <note>original: "痹者閉也" (Bi zhe, bi ye). This is a classical medical etymology, where the name of the disease is defined by its core characteristic: the blockage of the body's vital pathways.</note> When the **five Zang** and **six Fu** organs <term>Zang-Fu: The collective term for the solid (yin) and hollow (yang) internal organs in Chinese medicine.</term> are affected by **pathogenic factors**, the **True Qi** <term>True Qi (Zhen Qi): The primary vital energy that circulates through the body, maintaining health and protecting against illness.</term> becomes disordered. The pathways are closed off and sensation is lost; therefore, it is called **Bi-Impediment disease**.

The manifestations are diverse:
- There may be pain or itching.
- There may be <term>strangury: painful or difficult urination</term>, or a sudden sense of urgency.
- There may be flaccidity such that the body cannot be supported or held upright.
- Or the limbs may be clenched and unable to stretch or extend.
- There may be difficulty in walking or standing.
- Or speech may be faltering and difficult.
- There may be paralysis of one side of the body <note>original: "半身不遂" (banshen busui), literally "half the body does not follow [one's will]," referring to hemiplegia.</note>.
- Or the four limbs may be curled and contracted.
- Or the mouth and eyes may be deviated to one side.
- Or the hands and feet may be tilted and crooked.
- Some may be able to walk but be unable to speak.
- Others may be able to speak but be unable to walk.
- There may be wasting on the left side of the body, or stagnation and blockage on the right.
- The upper body may fail to communicate with the lower, or the lower may fail to communicate with the upper.
- Or the **Large Intestine** may be obstructed <gloss>one version states: "difficulty in urination"</gloss>.
- There may be pain in the left or right hands.
- In some cases, one may contract the illness and die immediately; in others, one may be struck by the pathogen and yet survive.
- There may be wheezing and a sense of fullness in the chest that prevents sleep.
- Or there may be dizziness and clouded consciousness...

## Chapter 34: On Qi-Impediment

**Qi-Impediment** <term>Qi-Impediment (Qi Bi): A type of blockage where the body’s vital energy (Qi) is obstructed. Unlike other forms of Bi caused by weather, this is primarily driven by internal emotional strain.</term> occurs when there is an excess of sorrow, worry, contemplation, joy, or anger. This causes the **Qi** to "knot" or bind in the upper part of the body. If this knotting remains for a long time without dissipating, it damages the **Lungs**. Once the Lungs are harmed, the **Life-Generating Qi** (*Sheng Qi*) <note>The vital energy responsible for growth and maintenance.</note> gradually weakens, allowing the **Pathogenic Qi** (*Xie Qi*) <term>Pathogenic Qi: Harmful energy that causes disease, here becoming stronger as the body’s defenses fail.</term> to become more dominant.

The effects of this blockage depend on where the stagnant energy settles:
- If it remains in the upper body, the stomach and abdomen become obstructed, and the person cannot eat.
- If it flows down to the lower body, the waist and legs feel heavy, and the person cannot walk.
- If it attacks the left side, the left side becomes paralyzed and unresponsive <note>original: "左不遂" (zuo bu sui). This term describes a limb that "will not follow" the instructions of the mind.</note>.
- If it rushes to the right side, the right side becomes numb and loses sensation <note>original: "右不仁" (you bu ren). In medical Chinese, "bu ren" (literally "not human" or "unfeeling") refers to a loss of tactile sensation or "dead" flesh.</note>.
- If it penetrates the tongue, the person cannot speak.
- If it is deposited within the intestines, the person cannot urinate.
- If the Qi congests and does not disperse, it causes pain. 
- If it flows but does not gather, it causes numbness.

The **True Classic** <note>original: "眞經" (Zhen Jing). This likely refers to the foundational medical texts, such as the *Yellow Emperor’s Inner Classic*, which this text seeks to interpret.</note> states...

This is the most excellent method of healing.

## Chapter 35: On Blood-Impediment

**Blood-Impediment** <term>Blood-Impediment (Xue Bi): A condition where the flow of blood is obstructed, leading to numbness, weakness, or the wasting away of limbs.</term> is caused by drinking an excess of wine or harboring too much internal heat. It may also occur when **Cold** strikes the **Meridians** <term>Meridians (Jingluo): The pathways in the body through which Qi and Blood circulate.</term> or **Dampness** invades the **Nutritive and Defensive levels** <term>Nutritive and Defensive (Ying Wei): "Ying" is the nourishing energy within the blood vessels, while "Wei" is the protective energy that circulates outside them to defend the body.</term>. Because of these factors, the blood "binds" <note>The term "tuan" (摶) suggests the blood congealing or clumping together rather than flowing smoothly.</note> and creates this affliction. 

Consequently, the blood cannot nourish the exterior of the body, and the **Qi** cannot sustain the interior. Once this balance between the internal and external is lost, the body gradually wastes and withers. 
- If the left side withers first, then the right side cannot be lifted. 
- If the right side withers first, then the left side cannot be extended. 
- If the upper body withers first, the upper cannot control the lower. 
- If the lower body withers first, the lower cannot support the upper. 
- If the center withers first, the "hundred symptoms" <note>A traditional way of saying "all possible illnesses."</note> cannot be cleared or resolved.

### Chapter 36: On Flesh-Impediment

**Flesh-Impediment** <term>Flesh-Impediment (Rou Bi): A condition of numbness, heaviness, or wasting in the muscles, traditionally associated with the dysfunction of the Spleen.</term> is caused by a lack of restraint in eating and drinking, and by the excessive consumption of **rich and fatty foods** <note>The original term "gao liang" (膏粱) refers to a diet of greasy meats and fine grains, typically associated with the wealthy and considered a primary cause of internal "heat" and dampness.</note>. The **Spleen** <term>Spleen (Pi): In traditional medicine, the Spleen is the organ responsible for digestion and the production of flesh and muscle throughout the body.</term> is the foundation of the flesh.

If the **Spleen-Qi** <term>Spleen-Qi: The vital energy of the Spleen that powers digestion and moves nutrients to the muscles.</term> is already depleted, the flesh will not flourish. If the flesh does not flourish, the skin and muscles will lose their smoothness and luster. When the muscles lack this healthy luster, the **striae** <term>Striae (Cou Li): The fine textures of the skin and the spaces between the muscles that serve as the body's defensive boundary against external illness.</term> become loose and porous. This allows the **Pathogenic Factors of Wind, Cold, Summer-Heat, and Dampness** <note>These are the "Four Evils" or environmental stressors that Chinese medicine believes invade the body when its defenses are weak.</note> to easily enter. Therefore, if the condition is left untreated for a long time, it develops into Flesh-Impediment.

The symptoms of Flesh-Impediment are as follows: initially, the patient is able to eat, yet the body does not feel nourished or satisfied. The four limbs become weak and limp, losing the strength to support the body or hold objects. This is confirmed when the pulse at the **right Guan position** <term>Right Guan (You Guan): The specific point on the right wrist pulse associated with the Spleen and Stomach.</term> lacks strength whether it is pressed lightly or firmly, and its movement feels "choppy" or sluggish. One should practice restraint in eating and drinking to harmonize the internal organs, and maintain a regular routine of rest and activity to stabilize the Spleen. However...

## Chapter 37: On Sinew-Impediment

**Sinew-Impediment** <term>Sinew-Impediment (Jin Bi): A condition characterized by stiffness, spasms, or pain in the tendons and ligaments. In traditional Chinese medicine, the "sinews" (Jin) include what we now call tendons, ligaments, and certain muscle groups.</term> is caused by untimely fits of anger and shouting, or by walking and running with excessive haste. These actions allow **perverse influences** <note>The term "yin xie" (淫邪) refers to environmental or internal pathogens that "overstep" or invade the body's defenses, often translated as "pathogenic factors."</note> to injure the **Liver** <note>In traditional medicine, the Liver is the organ "governor" of the sinews; emotional outbursts like anger are believed to directly damage its energetic function.</note>. When the Liver loses its **Qi** <term>Qi: The vital energy or life force that flows through the body's meridians.</term>, the pathogens of Cold and Heat take up residence. If they remain for a long time without being expelled, they flow into the **junctions of the sinews** <note>These are the areas where tendons and ligaments converge, typically near the joints.</note>. This causes the sinews to become constricted and tense, preventing the person from walking with a relaxed or easy gait.

Therefore, it is called Sinew-Impediment. To treat it, one should **invigorate the blood** to supplement the Liver, and warm the Qi to nourish the **Kidneys** <term>Kidneys (Shen): The Kidneys are considered the "mother" of the Liver in the Five Elements theory; thus, treating the Kidneys provides the foundation for the Liver to heal.</term>. Afterward, one should administer medicinal decoctions or pills. If the treatment is appropriate, the illness will be cured; otherwise, it will bring harm to the person.

The **pulse** for this condition is found at the **left Guan position** <term>Left Guan (Zuo Guan): The middle position of the pulse on the left wrist, which specifically reflects the health of the Liver and Gallbladder.</term>; it is **wiry, urgent, and rapid** <note>A "wiry" (xian) pulse feels like a tight guitar string under the fingers and is a classic diagnostic sign of Liver distress, tension, or pain.</note>, and feels forceful at both the superficial and deep levels.

When **Kidney-Qi** is consumed internally, it cannot open...

If **lockjaw** <term>Lockjaw (Jin): The inability to open the mouth, often occurring during acute seizures or stroke-like events.</term> occurs and cannot be released, then the **Middle and Upper Burners** <term>Triple Burner (San Jiao): A functional organ system in Chinese medicine; the "Upper" refers to the heart and lungs, the "Middle" to the spleen and stomach, and the "Lower" to the kidneys and bladder. It coordinates the movement of energy and fluids.</term> will both fall into disorder. If the Middle and Upper are in disorder, then the Qi of the Triple Burner becomes **blocked** <term>Blockage (Pi): A state of stagnation where energy cannot flow between the organ systems.</term> and cannot circulate. When the Triple Burner is blocked, food and drink are not transformed into waste <note>original: "zao po" (糟粕). This implies that the body can no longer properly digest food or extract nutrients, leaving the "dregs" to stagnate rather than being processed.</note>. 

If food and drink are not processed, then **Essential Qi** <term>Essential Qi (Jing Qi): The most refined and fundamental energy that sustains life and maintains the health of the organs.</term> will decline day by day. As the Essential Qi declines, **Pathogenic Qi** <term>Pathogenic Qi (Xie Qi): Environmental or internal "evils"—such as Wind, Cold, or Heat—that invade the body when its defenses are weakened.</term> will invade recklessly.

When Pathogenic Qi invades recklessly:
* If it rushes upward to the heart and tongue, it results in **aphasia** <term>Aphasia (Bu yu): The loss of the ability to speak, often following a stroke.</term>. 
* If it invades the **Spleen and Stomach** <note>The Spleen and Stomach are considered the root of post-natal energy; their failure leads to systemic wasting.</note> in the middle, it results in a lack of nourishment for the body. 
* If it flows downward to the waist and knees, it results in **paralysis** <term>Paralysis (Bu sui): Literally "not following" [one's will]; the loss of motor control in the lower body.</term>. 
* If it attacks the limbs laterally, it results in **numbness** <term>Numbness (Bu ren): Literally "insensitivity" or "lack of feeling"; a loss of sensation in the extremities.</term>.

If Cold is in the center, the pulse is **slow**. If Heat is in the center, the pulse is **rapid**. If Wind is in the center, the pulse is **floating** <note>A pulse felt easily with light pressure on the surface, typical of "Wind" conditions.</note>. If Dampness is in the center, the pulse is **soggy** <note>A soft, thin pulse that feels like a thread floating on water.</note>. If Deficiency is in the center, the pulse is **slippery** <note>Usually a sign of "fullness," here the author notes its appearance in specific states of internal weakness.</note>. 

These symptoms are not uniform; the essential task is to be detailed and clear in one's diagnosis. The treatment methods are listed in the following chapter.

<meta>This page transitions from the general physiological consequences of blockages discussed on the previous page to the specific clinical strategies required to resolve them. It concludes the diagnostic section on "stiffness and blockages" and introduces Chapter 40, which focuses on the etiology of deep-seated boils or carbuncles.</meta>

How should we treat **stiffness and blockages** <term>Stiffness and Blockages (Qian Se): A medical state where the body or limbs are rigid and the flow of energy and fluids is severely obstructed, often following a stroke or severe energetic imbalance.</term>? 

If the condition is in the **Upper** region, then induce vomiting. 
If it is in the **Middle**, then purge the bowels. 
If it is in the **Lower**, then supplement the deficiency. 
If it is on the **Exterior**, then release it. 
If it is in the **Interior**, then warm it. 
One should also use massage and hot compresses.

"Inducing vomiting" means to expel **excess phlegm** <note>original: "xian" (涎). In this context, it refers to the pathological fluids or thick saliva that can block the throat and chest during acute illness.</note>. 
"Purging" means to clear out obstructions in the digestive tract. 
"Supplementing" means to benefit areas where there is insufficiency or weakness. 
"Releasing" means to induce sweating to clear the body's surface. 
"Warming" means to drive out **Dampness** <term>Dampness (Shi): A pathological factor in Chinese medicine characterized by heaviness and stagnation; it is often treated with warming therapies to "evaporate" the fluid.</term>. 
"Massage" means to disperse stagnant Qi. 
"Hot compresses" <note>original: "yun" (熨). This refers to the ancient practice of applying heated stones, salts, or medicinal cloths to the body to soothe pain and move energy.</note> means to assist the **Yang energy** <term>Yang: The active, warming, and functional energy of the body that provides heat and movement.</term>.

Each treatment must be suited to the specific circumstances; how could one single method be applied to every case? The key lies in seeking the **root cause**. 

* If the **pulse is floating**, then release the exterior through sweating. 
* If the **pulse is slippery** <term>Slippery Pulse (Hua Mai): A pulse that feels like pearls rolling under the finger, often indicating the presence of phlegm or excess fluids.</term>, then induce vomiting. 
* If the **pulse is hidden and choppy**, then purge the bowels. 
* If the **pulse is tight**, then apply warming treatments. 
* If the **pulse is slow**, then use hot compresses. 
* If the **pulse is closed**, then use massage. 

It is essential to examine what is appropriate for the patient; therefore, one must never treat every case with a single, unvarying standard.

They are called "clove" sores because their hard centers resemble the shape of a clove.</term> are all caused by the internal upheaval of the emotions—joy, anger, worry, and pensiveness—as well as being struck by sudden cold or braving intense heat, indulging excessively in **strong wine**, and frequently craving sweets...

### Page 9

[These conditions] are caused by the consumption of **greasy toxins** <note>original: "fei du" (肥毒). This refers to the pathological effects of an overly rich, fatty diet.</note>, pickled fish, and fermented sauces, as well as by **excessive sexual desire** <note>original: "se yu" (色慾).</note>. When these toxic evils are harbored within, they soak into the **internal organs** <term>Zang-Fu (臟腑): The collective term for the solid (Zang) and hollow (Fu) organs in Chinese medicine, which govern the body's vital functions and the storage of essence.</term>. If they are not filtered or dispersed for a long time, they begin to transform into **Clove-Sores** <term>Ding (丁): Also known as "clove-sores," these are deep-seated, hard, and painful boils or carbuncles that resemble the head of a clove or a nail; they are considered highly dangerous if the toxins enter the bloodstream.</term>. 

There are five names for these: the first is the **White Clove-Sore**, the second is the **Red Clove-Sore**, the third is the **Yellow Clove-Sore**, the fourth is the **Black Clove-Sore**, and the fifth is the **Blue-Green Clove-Sore**.

The **White Clove-Sore** arises below the right nostril. At its onset, it looks like a grain of millet, with a red root and a white head. It may feel numb, or it may be painful and itchy. It causes the person to suffer from **chills** <note>original: "zeng han" (憎寒), a strong aversion to cold often accompanied by shivering.</note> and a heavy head, appearing much like a **Cold Damage** <term>Cold Damage (Shanghan): A broad category of external febrile diseases caused by environmental cold or wind-cold entering the body.</term> disorder. If the patient has no desire to eat, feels fullness and oppression in the **stomach and chest** <note>original: "wei ge" (胃膈). This refers to the region encompassing the upper digestive tract and the diaphragm.</note>, suffers from rapid breathing, or falls into a coma, they will die. If these symptoms have not yet appeared, the condition can be treated. In this illness, disaster will surely strike if more than five days pass; it must be treated with great urgency.

The **Red Clove-Sore** is located under the tongue. Both the root and the head of the sore are red, and it causes great pain. The base of the tongue becomes stiff, making the patient unable to speak. They will experience frequent fright, irritability, restlessness, and a **trance-like state** <note>original: "huang hu" (恍惚). In a medical context, this describes mental confusion, delirium, or a loss of clear consciousness.</note>. They will be intensely thirsty, drawing <gloss>or: drinking</gloss> water without ceasing. If urination becomes blocked or if the patient becomes manic, they will die. If these have not yet occurred, the condition can be treated. In this illness, disaster will surely strike if more than seven days pass, after which it cannot be cured. Both adults and children can be afflicted by this.

**Clove-Sore Classic: Section Ten**

The **Yellow Clove-Sore** <term>Huang Ding (黃疔): A variant of the clove-sore associated with the Spleen and Stomach elements, often manifesting with yellowish discharge.</term> arises near the lips, teeth, or gums. Its color is yellow, and it contains a yellow fluid. When it breaks out, it causes the patient to eat excessively <gloss>one version says: "to be able to eat"</gloss> but then <gloss>one version says: "repeatedly"</gloss> experience numbness in the hands and feet. Saliva flows without stopping, and the abdomen becomes bloated and irritable. If the patient is constantly drowsy yet unable to sleep, they will die; if they have not yet reached this state, the condition can be treated.

The **Black Clove-Sore** <term>Hei Ding (黑疔): A dangerous type of carbuncle associated with the exhaustion of Kidney essence.</term> arises in front of the ear. It looks like a scar or a dark blotch. Its color is black, and its size fluctuates inconsistently. It causes the patient's jaw to become tightly clenched <note>original: "ya guan ji" (牙關急), referring to lockjaw or trismus.</note>, and the waist, spine, legs, and knees become numb; if not numb, they are instead painful. If this occurs, disaster will surely strike within three years, and it cannot be cured. This is because the **Kidney Qi** <term>Kidney Qi (Shen Qi): In Chinese medicine, the fundamental energy of the kidneys which governs growth, reproduction, and the strength of the bones and lower back.</term> is gradually becoming exhausted. The patient should be cautious regarding sexual matters.

The **Blue-Green Clove-Sore** <term>Qing Ding (青疔): A variety associated with the Liver, often hard in texture and linked to emotional disturbances.</term> arises below the eyes. At its onset, it looks like a tumor or a dark patch. Its color is blue-green, and it is hard like a stone. It causes the patient's vision to become blurred and dim until they can see nothing. They will frequently suffer from fear, palpitations, and tremors, and their sleep will be restless. If the condition persists for a long time, it causes blindness or the **loss of essence** <note>original: "tuo jing" (脱精), referring to the leakage of vital fluids or seminal essence, indicating a collapse of the body's systems.</note>. If these symptoms are present, disaster will surely strike within one year.

The **White Clove-Sore** has its root in the **Lungs**. The **Red Clove-Sore** has its root in the... <note>The text cuts off here; based on traditional theory, the Red Clove-Sore is rooted in the Heart.</note>

### On Carbuncles, Abscesses, Sores, and Swellings: Section Forty-One

Now, the occurrence of carbuncles <term>Yong (癰): Large, red, and hot inflammatory swellings on the skin surface, often translated as acute abscesses.</term>, gangrenous sores <term>Ju (疽): Deep-rooted, dark-colored sores that typically indicate a more internal or systemic deficiency.</term>, sores, and swellings is because the **Five Zang and Six Fu Organs** <term>Zang-Fu (五臟六腑): The internal organs according to traditional Chinese medicine; Zang (Yin) organs include the Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lung, and Kidney, while Fu (Yang) organs include the Stomach, Gallbladder, and Intestines.</term> have accumulated toxins that do not circulate; thus these conditions emerge <gloss>the original text says: "all contain [toxins]"</gloss>. It is not merely because of the obstruction of the **Nutritive and Defensive Qi** <term>Rong Wei (榮衛): 'Rong' refers to the nutritive energy that flows within the blood vessels, while 'Wei' is the defensive energy that circulates on the exterior to protect the body.</term> that these conditions break out.

Their progression follows specific locations, and their manifestation can be traced to specific origins. For instance, if they appear on the throat or tongue, it is the toxin of the Heart. If they appear on the skin and hair <note>In traditional medical theory, the skin and hair are governed by the Lungs; the original text here omits the specific organ name, moving directly to the next category.</note>, or if they appear in the muscles and flesh, it is the toxin of the Spleen. If they appear in the bone marrow, it is the toxin of the Kidney <gloss>missing the Liver</gloss>.

Those that appear in the lower part of the body are toxins from within the **Yin** <term>Yin (陰): Referring to the lower half of the body and the internal, cooling energies.</term>. Those that appear in the upper part of the body are toxins from within the **Yang** <term>Yang (陽): Referring to the upper half of the body and the external, warming energies.</term>. Those that appear in...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Section Forty-One: "On Carbuncles, Abscesses, Sores, and Swellings," moving from the root causes of these conditions to the diagnostic distinctions between internal and external origins, as well as their prognostic signs.</meta>

Those that manifest on the exterior result from toxins of the **Six Fu organs** <term>Six Fu (六腑): The 'hollow' organs—Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Gallbladder, Bladder, and Triple Burner—responsible for digestion and elimination.</term>; those that erupt from the interior result from toxins of the **Five Zang organs**. Therefore, an internal manifestation is called **decay** <term>Huai (壞): A term for internal rotting or spoilage of tissue.</term>, while an external one is called **ulceration** <term>Kui (潰): The rupturing or breaking of a sore on the surface.</term>. When they appear on the upper body, it is called **conforming**; when on the lower body, it is called **contrary** <note>In traditional diagnosis, 'conforming' (cong) suggests a more natural or manageable progression, whereas 'contrary' (ni) suggests a pathological movement against the body's healthy flow.</note>.

Those that erupt on the upper body are acquired rapidly; those on the lower body are acquired slowly. If the affliction is felt in the **Six Fu organs**, it is easy to treat; if it is felt in the **Five Zang organs**, it is difficult to heal.

Furthermore, those sores located near the bone are mostly **cold** in nature; those located near areas of **deficiency** <note>Refers to soft tissue or areas where the body's vital energy is weak.</note> are mostly **heat** in nature. If those near the bone do not heal for a long time, they consume the blood until it is exhausted. If those near areas of deficiency do not heal for a long time, they transmit the **Qi** <term>Qi (氣): The vital life force or energy that circulates through the body's meridians.</term> and become a **fistula** <term>Lou (漏): A 'leaking' sore or chronic sinus tract that fails to close.</term>.

If the sore produces parasites <note>original: "worms" (chong 蟲). In ancient texts, this can refer to literal parasites or the sensation of "crawling" caused by certain types of infection.</note>, there will be much itching and little pain, or perhaps itching followed by pain. If it becomes a fistula, there is much pain and little itching, or sometimes neither pain nor itching.

When the interior is deficient and the exterior is excess <term>Shi (實): Often translated as 'solid' or 'excess,' indicating an acute, forceful, or inflammatory condition.</term>, there is much itching and little pain. When the exterior is deficient and the interior is excess, there is much pain and little itching. If the bleeding does not stop, the patient will often die; if the pus-filled affliction ruptures and drains, the patient will often live.

Instances of excessive vomiting or irregular eating are all caused by these carbuncles and abscesses. There are ten thousand <gloss>one version says: "many"</gloss> types of signs and symptoms; one must rely on a detailed examination to determine the proper treatment.

## Section 42: Discussion on the Different Symptoms and Signs of Leg Weakness

What is the difference between a person suffering from **Leg-Qi** <term>Jiao Qi (腳氣): Historically associated with beriberi, this refers to a condition of the legs characterized by swelling, numbness, or weakness.</term> and **Qi-Leg** <term>Qi Jiao (氣腳): A specific condition where internal emotional or metabolic imbalances manifest as pain or weakness in the legs.</term>? 

When the **Qi** <term>Qi (氣): The vital life force or energy that circulates through the body.</term> of joy, anger, worry, and contemplation, or the **pathogenic toxins** <term>Xie du (邪毒): Harmful energies or substances that cause disease.</term> of cold and heat, flow from the interior and pour into the legs, it is called **Qi-Leg**. 

When the pathogenic toxins of wind, cold, summer heat, and dampness enter the legs and knees from the exterior and gradually transmit to the interior, it is called **Leg-Qi**.

However, in both internal and external cases, the **pathogenic** forces displace the **upright energy** <term>Zheng (正): The body's natural, healthy, and defensive energy.</term>. Therefore, the physical symptoms of these diseases appear quite similar. Regarding their treatment, there are also distinctions of upper and lower body priority, and what must be addressed first versus last. Therefore, their categories must be distinguished. If one treats them as identical without investigating their origins, there is no logical way to achieve a **cure** <note>original: "瘳" (chou), specifically referring to recovery from a long or difficult illness.</note>.

Indeed, when the **Qi** of joy, anger, worry, and contemplation, or the pathogenic toxins of cold and heat, flow into the limbs and joints or pour into the legs and knees, their symptoms resemble various types of **Wind disorders** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, 'Wind' (Feng) refers to symptoms that move quickly, change often, or involve tremors and paralysis.</note>, **joint pain** <term>Li jie (歷節): Often associated with severe, migrating joint pain similar to arthritis.</term>, **hemiplegia** <term>Pian ku (偏枯): Literally 'one-sided withering,' referring to the paralysis or wasting of one side of the body.</term>, and **carbuncles** <term>Yong (癰): Large, deep-seated abscesses or swellings.</term>.

<summary>This section distinguishes between 'Qi-Leg' (internally caused by emotions or internal heat/cold) and 'Leg-Qi' (externally caused by climate/environment), noting that while their symptoms appear similar, their treatments must address their specific origins.</summary>
<keywords>Leg-Qi, Qi-Leg, Pathogenic Toxins, Upright Qi, diagnostic differentiation, Hua Tuo, Chinese medicine history</keywords>

-># Central Treasury Medical Classic<-

When the symptoms of swelling enter the legs and knees, it is referred to as **Qi-Leg** <term>Qi Jiao (氣腳): A condition where internal imbalances, such as emotional distress or metabolic issues, manifest as swelling in the lower limbs.</term>. However, if a condition enters through the feet from the outside and travels from the feet into the **internal organs** <term>Zang (臟): The five core "solid" organs—Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys—which store vital essence.</term>, it is then called **Leg-Qi** <term>Jiao Qi (腳氣): Historically associated with beriberi, this refers to a condition characterized by numbness, swelling, and weakness caused by external environmental factors.</term>.

For **Qi-Leg**, one must first treat the interior and then the exterior. For **Leg-Qi**, one must first treat the exterior and then the interior. If the condition is one of **excess** <note>original: "實" (shi), indicating a state where a pathogen is strong and the body's reaction is vigorous.</note>, one should drain it; if the condition is one of **deficiency** <note>original: "虛" (xu), indicating a lack of vital energy, blood, or fluids.</note>, one should replenish it.

Furthermore, why is it that so many people suffer from **Leg-Qi**? It is because the two **meridians** <term>Jing (經): The pathways through which Qi and blood circulate.</term> of the **Heart and Lungs** begin at the hands, while the three meridians of the **Spleen, Kidneys, and Liver** begin at the feet. The hands are struck by **clear pathogens** <term>Qing xie (清邪): "Light" or subtle harmful influences that tend to affect the upper body.</term>, whereas the feet are struck by **turbid pathogens** <term>Zhuo xie (濁邪): "Heavy" or dense harmful influences, such as dampness or cold, that naturally sink to the lower body.</term>.

Of all the parts of the human body that endure hardship, the hands and feet are the most taxed, but the feet suffer the heaviest toil and most difficult labor. Therefore, the **Qi** <term>Qi (氣): In this context, the environmental energies or "breaths" of the seasons.</term> of **wind, cold, summer heat, and dampness** <note>These are four of the "Six Excesses," environmental factors that Chinese medicine identifies as primary causes of disease.</note> mostly strikes the feet. This is why cases of **Leg-Qi** are so numerous.

However, those who contract this disease develop the illness gradually. It begins as a mere sprout, and the patient does not realize it; even if they sense a change, they do not understand it. Physicians often fail to recognize it as **Leg-Qi**, instead treating it as some other ailment. Because the treatment is not based on a clear diagnosis, the condition is allowed to persist until it becomes severe. The patient then finds themselves in a dangerous state, for the root of the illness originally...

Subtle beginnings gradually develop into severe symptoms, flowing into the **internal organs** <term>Zang-Fu (臟腑): The collective term for the solid "Zang" organs (like the Heart and Liver) and the hollow "Fu" organs (like the Stomach and Intestines).</term> and causing injury to the four limbs, the head and neck, and the abdomen and back. Yet, while the disease has not yet become severe, it remains entirely imperceptible. 

It only manifests when triggered by other factors, appearing perhaps like **Cold Damage** <term>Shanghan (傷寒): A category of acute febrile diseases caused by external cold pathogens.</term>, or like **Heat Stroke** <term>Zhongshu (中暑): Illness caused by exposure to extreme summer heat.</term>. It may present as pain in the abdomen and back, **numbness in the joints** <term>Bu ren (不仁): Literally "not benevolent," a classical medical term for a loss of sensation, paralysis, or "dead" feeling in the limbs.</term>, **confused speech** <note>original: "語言錯亂" (yuyan cuoluan), indicating a disturbance where the mind or spirit is no longer governing the tongue correctly.</note>, or **clouded consciousness** <note>original: "精神昏昧" (jingshen hunmei).</note>. At times there is shortness of breath and fatigue, sudden blindness or deafness, an inability to eat or drink, or the **internal organs being blocked** <note>original: "臟腑不通" (zangfu butong), referring to a cessation of bodily eliminations like bowel movements or urination.</note>.

Or there may be **acute contractions** <term>Luanji (攣急): Spasticity or painful cramping where the muscles tighten and cannot be extended.</term> preventing function, or **flaccidity** <term>Shuhuan (舒緩): Excessive looseness or weakness of the muscles where they cannot be retracted or held up.</term>. The mouth and eyes may **twitch and pull** <note>original: "牽搐" (qianchu), describing facial tics or the pulling of the mouth to one side, similar to symptoms of a stroke.</note>, or the hands and feet may **tremble and shake** <note>original: "顫掉" (chandiao).</note>.

Because its manifestations are so varied, no one truly understands them. Thus, the common folk are left helpless, suffering from the disease and dying without any means of redress. <insert>Chen.</insert> <note>The character "Chen" (陳) likely refers to a commentator or the scholar presenting this specific edition of the text.</note>

When a person of virtue sees this, how could they not be grieved? I now recount the full details of the disease from beginning to end to briefly instruct future students; I ask that you examine these signs deeply.

These conditions arise from such actions as entering the bedroom for intimacy while intoxicated, sleeping under the falling dew with a full stomach, exposing oneself to the wind to find cool air, or pursuing excessive pleasures under the moonlight. It comes from falling into a deep sleep before drying off after a bath, or rushing to an open **porch** <term>Chongxuan (衝軒): Rushing into a drafty, open-air walkway or balcony, particularly dangerous when the pores are open after exertion.</term> immediately after sexual activity. It comes from standing for a long time in **low-lying dampness** <term>Dixie (低濕): Humid, marshy, or damp environments believed to harbor "dampness" pathogens that enter through the feet.</term>, or remaining stationary for a long time in...

### The Classic of Leg-Qi

Walking along the water’s edge while braving the rain, sleeping while soaked in cold, suffering from exhaustion and sweating, or indulging in raw and cold food and drink—violating these various taboos leads to the formation of this disease. 

When **Unrighteous Qi** <term>Bu zheng zhi qi (不正之氣): Pathogenic factors or "evil" influences from the environment that disrupt the body’s natural balance.</term> strikes the upper body, it harms the head and eyes; when it harms the middle, it fills the **heart and abdomen** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, this refers to the entire chest and gastric region.</note>; when it manifests in the lower body, it brings disaster to the waist and legs; and when it reaches the sides, it obstructs the **joints of the limbs**. Although there are a thousand forms and ten thousand symptoms, all belong to the movement of **Qi** <term>Qi (氣): The vital energy or life force that flows through the body; here, it refers to the pathogenic energy driving the illness.</term>. Because the condition specifically begins in the feet and knees, it is called **Leg-Qi** <term>Jiaoqi (腳氣): Historically identified with beriberi, a deficiency of Vitamin B1, but described here as a syndrome of swelling, pain, and weakness caused by environmental pathogens.</term>.

The outward signs and **pulse patterns** <term>Maizheng (脉證): The diagnostic practice of reading the pulse to determine the nature and location of a disease.</term> must also be clearly distinguished:

*   A pulse that is **floating and wiry** <note>original: "浮而弦" (fu er xian)</note> originates from **Wind**.
*   A pulse that is **soft and weak** <note>original: "濡而弱" (ru er ruo)</note> originates from **Dampness**.
*   A pulse that is **flooding and rapid** <note>original: "洪而數" (hong er shu)</note> originates from **Heat**.
*   A pulse that is **slow and choppy** <note>original: "遲而濇" (chi er se)</note> originates from **Cold**.
*   A pulse that is **slippery and faint** <note>original: "滑而微" (hua er wei)</note> originates from **Deficiency**.
*   A pulse that is **firm and hard** <note>original: "牢而堅" (lao er jian)</note> originates from **Excess**.

If the illness is in the upper, it is due to the upper; if in the lower, it is due to the lower; if in the middle, it arises from the middle. A **knotted** pulse is caused by [stagnant] Qi; a **scattered** pulse is caused by sorrow; a **tight** pulse is caused by anger; and a **thin** pulse is caused by grief. For those afflicted by **Wind**, promote sweating and they will recover; for those afflicted by **Dampness**...

<keywords>Leg-Qi, Beriberi, Pulse Diagnosis, Unrighteous Qi, Environmental Pathogens, Wind-Damp, Traditional Chinese Medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leg-Qi (Jiaoqi) from the previous page, transitioning from the causes and pulse types to the specific clinical principles of treatment, the signs of a master physician, and the terminal symptoms when the disease attacks the internal organs. When warming a patient results in even greater heat, one must release it; when resolving a condition results in even greater cold, one must apply **heat therapy** <term>Yun (熨): The practice of applying medicinal compresses, hot stones, or heated cloths to the body to dispel cold.</term>. For those who are **deficient** <note>Referring to a lack of vital energy or bodily substance.</note>, supplement them; for those with **excess** <term>Shi (實): A state where pathogenic factors are strong or the body's reaction is overactive; "draining" (xie) is used to clear this excess.</term>, drain them. For stagnant **Qi**, allow it to flow. For the worried, broaden their minds; for the angry, bring them joy; for the grieving, harmonize them.

One who can master these principles is truly called an **excellent physician** <note>original: "良醫" (liangyi)</note>.

Furthermore, if the disease of **Leg-Qi** <term>Jiaoqi (脚氣): A condition characterized by swelling, numbness, and weakness of the legs, historically linked to beriberi.</term> spreads to the **Heart and Kidneys**, ten out of ten will die; it is incurable. 

If it enters the **Heart**, the symptoms are trance-like confusion and incoherent speech, vomiting and inability to keep food down, restless sleep, and instability of the mouth and eyes. The pulse at the **Cun position** <term>Cun kou (寸口): The "inch" position of the pulse on the wrist, used here to diagnose the condition of the Heart and upper body.</term> of the left hand will be suddenly large and suddenly small, or suddenly present and suddenly absent. 

If it enters the **Kidneys**, both the waist and legs will swell, urination will be obstructed, and the patient will groan without ceasing. A dark black color will appear on the forehead and around the eyes. The **Qi** will frequently rush upward toward the stomach and abdomen, causing panting. These signs occur when the pulse at the **Chi position** <term>Chi zhong (尺中): The "cubit" position, the pulse point furthest from the hand, used to diagnose the Kidneys and lower body.</term> of the left hand is severed <note>Meaning the pulse is no longer palpable at that location.</note>. These conditions must be examined with the utmost detail and caution.

## Chapter 43: Discussion on the Pulse and Symptoms of Edema and Signs of Life and Death

<summary>This page details the holistic treatment of Leg-Qi through both physical and emotional regulation, while providing a grim prognosis and specific pulse diagnostics for when the disease turns fatal by attacking the Heart and Kidneys.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of Leg-Qi to a specific treatise on water diseases (edema). It establishes the Kidneys as the root of fluid regulation and explains the pathological movement of water when the body's vital energies are compromised.</meta>

<sig>Five</sig>

Among the hundred diseases that afflict people, none are more difficult to cure than **water diseases** <term>Shuibing (水病): A general category in traditional Chinese medicine for conditions involving abnormal fluid accumulation, similar to modern definitions of edema or dropsy.</term>. Water is governed by the **Kidneys**; the Kidneys are the very foundation of a human being. When **Kidney Qi** <note>The vital energy and functional power of the Kidney system, believed to govern growth, reproduction, and fluid metabolism.</note> is robust, water returns to the "sea" <note>original: "海" (hai). This likely refers to the bladder or the body's proper internal reservoirs.</note>; when Kidney Qi is **deficient** <note>Referring to a state of weakness or insufficiency in the body's vital resources.</note>, water scatters into the skin.

Furthermore, when the **Triple Burner** <term>San Jiao (三焦): A unique concept in Chinese medicine describing a three-part functional system that regulates the movement of fluids and energy throughout the torso.</term> is obstructed and the **Nutritive and Defensive Qi** <term>Ying-Wei (榮衛): 'Ying' refers to the nutritive energy that flows within the vessels, while 'Wei' refers to the defensive energy that circulates on the exterior to protect the body.</term> are blocked and separated, the blood and Qi no longer follow their proper courses. As states of **deficiency and excess** <note>The fluctuating balance between the body's internal strength and external pathogenic pressures.</note> shift and transform, water follows the flow of Qi. This is what constitutes water disease. 

There are types where swelling occurs in the head and eyes; some swell in the waist and legs; some in the four limbs; and some in the abdomen. Some cases are triggered by coughing; some arise from overexertion; some begin due to stagnation; and some are formed from exhaustion and lack of vitality. Some originate from the **Five Zang-Organs** <note>The "solid" internal organs: Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys.</note>, while others come from the **Six Fu-Organs** <note>The "hollow" organs: Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Gallbladder, Bladder, Stomach, and the Triple Burner.</note>.

The categories are numerous and their manifestations are each different. The reason they are so difficult to treat is because these hundred variations are hard for people to fully comprehend. Even if one understands the root of the problem, the physician is often frustrated by patients who are self-indulgent and do not follow the medical principles, violating **prohibitions** <note>Refers to "Jinji" (禁忌), the specific dietary or behavioral restrictions essential for the treatment to work.</note> and failing to be fully prepared. Therefore, when people are struck by water diseases, they often die.

### Diseases of the Heart and Abdomen

...are many. There are ten named types of **water [diseases]**, which are listed at the end of this chapter. First is **Blue Water**; second is **Red Water**; third is **Yellow Water**; fourth is **White Water**; fifth is **Black Water**; sixth is **Dark Water** <note>original: "元水" (yuanshui). 'Yuan' can mean primordial or deep/dark; in this context of colors, it refers to a deep, dark hue.</note>; seventh is **Wind Water**; eighth is **Stone Water**; ninth is **Enveloped Water**; tenth is **Qi Water**.

**Blue Water** has its root arising in the **Liver** <term>Gan (肝): In traditional medicine, the Liver system governs the flow of Qi and is associated with the element Wood and the color blue-green.</term>. Its manifestation begins with swelling of the face, which then gradually spreads throughout the entire body.

**Red Water** has its root arising in the **Heart** <term>Xin (心): The Heart system governs the blood and vessels and is associated with the element Fire and the color red.</term>. Its manifestation begins with swelling in the **stomach region** <note>The text specifies the "stomach" (wei) as the initial site of swelling for a heart-rooted water condition.</note>.

**Yellow Water** has its root arising in the **Spleen** <term>Pi (脾): The Spleen system is responsible for transformation and transportation of fluids and is associated with the element Earth and the color yellow.</term>. Its manifestation begins with swelling of the abdomen.

**White Water** has its root arising in the **Lungs** <term>Fei (肺): The Lung system governs respiration and the "water passages," associated with the element Metal and the color white.</term>. Its manifestation begins with swelling of the feet, followed by **upward-rushing Qi** <note>A pathological condition where energy moves contrary to its natural downward path, often causing respiratory distress.</note>, wheezing, and coughing.

**Black Water** has its root arising in the **Kidneys** <term>Shen (腎): The Kidney system is the primary regulator of water in the body, associated with the element Water and the color black.</term>. Its manifestation begins with swelling of the **insteps** <note>original: "足跌" (zudie). Specifically the top surface of the feet.</note>.

**Dark Water** has its root arising in the **Gallbladder**. Its manifestation is characterized by swelling that begins in the head and face and reaches down to the feet.

**Wind Water** <note>A condition where external "Wind" pathogens interfere with the body's fluid metabolism.</note> has its root arising in the **Stomach**. Its manifestation begins in the four limbs; the abdomen becomes full and enlarged, and the swelling spreads throughout the entire body.

<summary>This page details a diagnostic system for edema, categorizing ten types of "water diseases" based on their organ of origin and initial site of swelling, largely following the Five Phases (Wu Xing) color correspondences.</summary>
<keywords>edema, water diseases, Five Phases, Liver, Heart, Spleen, Lungs, Kidneys, diagnosis, Hua Tuo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific symptoms of the ten types of "water diseases" (edema). It concludes the descriptions of the internal organ roots and introduces the prognostic value of the pulse, as well as the progression from Wasting-Thirst (diabetes) to systemic edema.</meta>

...swelling. 

**Stone Water** <note>original: "石水" (shishui)</note> has its root in the **Bladder**. Its manifestation is that it arises below the navel, and the abdomen alone becomes enlarged. 

**Enveloped Water** <term>Li shui (裏水): Literally "Internal" or "Enveloped" water. This refers to deep-seated fluid accumulation within the digestive tract or abdominal cavity.</term> has its root in the **Small Intestine**. Its manifestation is that first, the lower abdomen distends without swelling, and then it gradually swells. <gloss>One version says: the lower abdomen distends and then suddenly swells.</gloss> 

**Qi Water** <term>Qi shui (氣水): Fluid accumulation related to the stagnation or dysfunction of vital energy (Qi), often characterized by its fluctuating nature.</term> has its root in the **Large Intestine**. Its manifestation is that it suddenly comes and goes, suddenly flourishing and suddenly declining.

These conditions are truly caused by a lack of communication between the upper and lower parts of the body, the obstruction of the joints and orifices, the blockage of **Qi and Blood** <term>Qi and Blood (氣血): The fundamental substances of the body; Qi is the functional energy and Blood is the nourishing substance. Their free flow is essential for health.</term>, and the disharmony of **Yin and Yang** <term>Yin and Yang (陰陽): The dualistic forces of nature; here referring to the balance of bodily functions and temperatures.</term>. If the pulse is flooding and large, the condition can be treated; if it is minute and thin, it cannot be treated.

Furthermore, if the affliction of **Wasting-Thirst** <term>Xiao ke (消渴): A condition characterized by excessive thirst, hunger, and urination, historically corresponding to what we now identify as diabetes mellitus.</term> remains uncured for a long time, it causes the person to suffer from "water vapors." When these fluids disperse and return to the **five Zang and six Fu organs** <term>Five Zang and Six Fu (五臟六腑): A collective term for the major internal organs. The Zang are "solid" organs (Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lung, Kidney) and the Fu are "hollow" organs (Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Gallbladder, Stomach, Bladder, Triple Burner).</term>, they manifest as disease. 

Wasting-Thirst is caused by exposure to wind and heat, lack of moderation in hunger and satiety, excessive consumption of alcohol, frequent injury from overindulgence in desires, or the ingestion of **mineral elixirs** <term>Jin shi (金石): Literally "metals and stones." This refers to alchemical compounds containing minerals like arsenic or mercury, which were consumed for longevity but often resulted in chronic poisoning and organ failure.</term> which accumulate over time to cause this condition.

<summary>This page describes the final three types of "water diseases" (Stone, Enveloped, and Qi Water), provides a pulse-based prognosis, and explains how chronic diabetes (Wasting-Thirst) and alchemical poisoning lead to systemic edema.</summary>

## Section 44: On Various Strangury Disorders and Difficulty in Urinating

Various **strangury disorders** <term>Lin (淋): A category of diseases characterized by frequent, urgent, and painful urination. In modern terms, this often encompasses urinary tract infections, stones, or prostate issues.</term> and **difficulty in urinating** <note>original: "小便不利" (xiaobian buli)</note> are all caused by the lack of communication between the **five viscera** and the **six bowels** <term>Viscera and Bowels (五臟六腑): The internal organs according to Chinese medicine. The five viscera (heart, liver, spleen, lungs, kidneys) store essence, while the six bowels (stomach, intestines, etc.) process and transport materials.</term>, the stagnation and obstruction of the **Triple Burner** <term>Triple Burner (三焦): A functional organ system that regulates the movement of fluids and energy (Qi) throughout the upper, middle, and lower sections of the torso.</term>, and the depletion of the **Nutritive and Defensive Qi** <term>Ying Wei (榮衛): "Nutritive" (Ying) flows within the vessels to nourish, while "Defensive" (Wei) flows outside to protect the body.</term>. 

These conditions arise from several causes:
* Braving heat to drink wine and becoming excessively drunk.
* Engaging in sexual intercourse while intoxicated, which exhausts the spirit and essence.
* Overworking the **Qi and Blood**. 
* Sexual arousal that results in "failed essence" <note>This refers to sexual stimulation that does not lead to ejaculation, causing "stagnant" fluids to block the pathways.</note> being unable to exit.
* Ceaseless indulgence in sexual infatuation, leading to the excessive loss of the **True Marrow**.
* Sudden panic or irregular emotional states.
* Anxiety and lack of mental peace.
* Eating at irregular times (either starving or over-satiated).
* Constant, restless traveling or running.
* Suppressing the natural urge to urinate or defecate.
* Excessive sexual emission over a long period.
* Cold entering the **Bladder** or heat striking the urinary sac.

When one is not careful regarding these matters, these diseases arise. Their symptoms and manifestations vary, and their names differ accordingly. In total, there are eight types: **Cold, Heat, Qi, Overstrain, Fatty, Sand, Deficiency, and Excess**. 

**Cold Strangury** <note>original: "冷淋" (leng lin)</note> is characterized by frequent urination where the color is white, appearing like rice-water. 

**Heat Strangury** <note>original: "熱淋" (re lin)</note> is characterized by difficult, obstructed urination that is red in color, appearing like blood.

**Qi Strangury** <note>original: "氣淋" (qi lin)</note> is characterized by... <note>The text provided ends here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This section identifies the multifaceted causes of urinary disorders (Strangury), attributing them to lifestyle excesses, emotional distress, and internal organ disharmony, while introducing the eight clinical classifications of the disease.</summary>

...fullness and oppression in the umbilical region and abdomen, where urination is obstructed and painful. 

**Fatigue Strangury** <term>Fatigue Strangury (Lao Lin 勞淋): A chronic urinary condition characterized by symptoms that are triggered or worsened by physical exhaustion or overwork.</term> is characterized by the incessant dribbling of urine, much like a constant, unbroken leak from a water vessel. 

**Lipid Strangury** <term>Lipid Strangury (Gao Lin 膏淋): A condition where the urine appears cloudy, milky, or contains greasy substances resembling fat or oil.</term> is characterized by the presence of substances resembling grease or fat within the urine. 

**Sand Strangury** <term>Sand Strangury (Sha Lin 砂淋): This corresponds to modern urolithiasis (kidney or bladder stones), where mineral deposits form gritty "sand" or small stones in the urinary tract.</term> involves a dull, hidden ache within the umbilical region and abdomen. Urination is difficult and the pain is unbearable. In a short moment, substances like sand or stones are passed through the urine. The larger ones are like **soapberries** <note>original: "皁子" (zao zi). These are the seeds of the Gleditsia tree, roughly the size of a large bean.</note>, and they may be red or white <gloss>one version says yellow</gloss> in color, with an inconsistent luster. 

This condition is caused by the weakness of the **Kidney Qi** and a greedy indulgence in sexual desire—either engaging in intercourse without ejaculation, or ejaculating excessively without restraint. This vacuity damages the **True Qi** <term>True Qi (Zhen Qi 真氣): The fundamental, life-sustaining energy of the body that protects against disease.</term>, while **Pathogenic Heat** <note>original: "邪熱" (xie re). In this context, it refers to inflammatory "heat" that dries out the body's fluids.</note> gradually strengthens. This heat causes fluids to congeal and gather, eventually forming "sand." 

It is much like the process of boiling salt in water: if the fire is intense and the water is scarce, the salt gradually hardens into stone. The Kidneys are associated with the element of Water, and the salty flavor is governed by the Kidneys. When the Water is depleted from below and the **Vacuity Heat** <note>Heat arising not from an external infection, but from the exhaustion of cooling fluids (Yin).</note> increases day by day, it decocts and binds the fluids into stones. This does not happen in a single day, but develops over a long period. From the time the condition forms, it takes five years to manifest; it takes three years to cause total decay. Even for a strong person, it takes five years for the disaster to fully take hold.

[Death] will surely arrive; it is appropriate to attack the disease urgently. Among the **Eight types of strangury** <term>Eight Stranguries (Ba Lin 八淋): A classification of eight distinct urinary disorders in traditional Chinese medicine, ranging from stones to emotional or fatigue-related blockages.</term>, this one is the most dangerous. If the pulse is very large and **Full** <term>Full Pulse (Shi 實): A pulse that feels forceful and long, usually indicating that the body's upright energy is actively fighting a pathogen.</term>, it can be treated; if it is **Vacuous** <term>Vacuous Pulse (Xu 虛): A weak, empty pulse indicating a depletion of vital energy (Qi) or blood.</term>, small, and **Choppy** <term>Choppy Pulse (Se 濇): A pulse that feels rough and jagged, often compared to a knife scraping bamboo; it indicates stagnant blood or exhausted fluids.</term>, it cannot be treated.

Furthermore, in all cases of strangury, if the symptoms follow the natural course of the condition, the patient will live; if the symptoms are contrary to the condition's nature, the patient will die. In all instances of treatment, one must exercise careful and detailed judgment.

### Chapter 45: On the Success and Failure of Ingesting Medicinals

Regarding the ingestion of minerals and metals, there are successes and failures because of their suitability or unsuitability for the patient. Whether the medicine consists of herbs or wood, metals or minerals; whether a single ingredient proves effective or a compound formula achieves success; whether the toxicity of metals and minerals breaks out and leads to death, or the potency of herbs and wood provides support and enables recovery—why are the results so inconsistent? It is because...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Volume 3, specifically moving from the diagnosis of urinary disorders (strangury) into the broader philosophical and practical principles of therapeutic intervention. It emphasizes the critical need to match treatment to the patient's underlying constitution—Deficiency or Excess—and critiques the lifestyle of those who rely on medicine to offset self-indulgence.</meta>

Those whose fundamental constitution is characterized by **Excess** <term>Excess (Shi 實): A state where a pathogen is strong or the body’s functions are overactive, requiring treatments that clear, drain, or unblock.</term> will surely extend their lifespan if they receive treatments with the property of **promoting flow and unblocking**. Conversely, those whose fundamental constitution is characterized by **Deficiency** <term>Deficiency (Xu 虛): A state of depletion of the body's vital resources—such as Qi, Blood, Yin, or Yang—requiring nourishing and tonifying treatments.</term> will surely lengthen their years if they receive treatments of a **supplementing and nourishing** nature. 

If a patient is in a state of Deficiency yet is subjected to excessive purging, or if they are in a state of Excess and are given further supplementation, then out of a thousand such cases, a thousand will die; out of ten thousand, ten thousand will perish. This outcome is certain.

Furthermore, there are those of the younger generation and people of wealth and status who rely on the power of their medicines to indulge their desires for wine and lust. They flaunt their physical techniques and secretly cause internal damage to their **Vital Essence and Spirit** <term>Spirit (Jing Shen 精神): In this context, the refined essence (Jing) and the cognitive/vitality aspect (Shen) which together form the foundation of health and longevity.</term>, using the power of drugs as a mere support. When a disease suddenly erupts, how can they be rescued or healed? People such as these do not realize that disaster is born from within; they only fear that their medicines show no evidence of success, yet the actual toll of their actions can be clearly counted.

Regarding the long-term consumption of formulas and medicines, the key lies in examining their appropriateness. When the person and the medicine are in harmony, how could the results be anything but effective? 

For example:
- If the **Viscera** <term>Viscera (Zang 臟): The five "solid" internal organs (Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, Kidneys) responsible for storing vital essence.</term> are insufficient, then supplement the Viscera.
- If the **Bowels** <term>Bowels (Fu 腑): The six "hollow" organs (Stomach, Small Intestine, Large Intestine, Gallbladder, Bladder, Triple Burner) responsible for processing and transporting food and waste.</term> have an abundance, then purge the Bowels.
- If the exterior is in a state of Excess, then regulate the exterior.
- If the interior is in a state of Deficiency, then nourish the interior.
- If the upper region is obstructed, then lead the energy upward.
- If the lower region is blocked, then unblock the lower region.
- If the middle is walled <gloss>One version of the text says "knotted"</gloss>, then release the middle.
- If the disease is on the left, then treat the left; if the disease is on the right, then treat the right. Up, down, left...

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental principle of "treating by contrast"—tonifying deficiency and purging excess—while warning that reversing these treatments is invariably fatal. It also provides a moralizing critique of wealthy patients who abuse their health and expect medicine to act as a shield for their vices.</summary>

If the internal and external states of **Deficiency** or **Excess** are treated according to their specific methods as described above, how could there be any premature deaths? Thus, no medicine is ineffective and no illness is incurable; the crucial task lies in careful and diligent observation.

### On the Discrimination of the Three Types of Stoppage: Treatises and Formulas, Number 46

Whether they be minerals, metals, herbs, or plants, these substances taken individually all have the potential to prevent death <note>This refers to the "immortality" or extreme longevity sought in medieval Chinese alchemy and medicine.</note>. Whether they prove effective or not depends entirely on the presence or absence of a patient's resolve. Even if one takes these substances over a long period, the medicinal heat may become obstructed and fail to dissipate. This can manifest in the upper or lower parts of the body, appearing as a **stoppage** <term>Stoppage (Pi 痞): A sensation of fullness, blockage, or obstruction that inhibits the natural flow of Qi and fluids.</term> or as a **constriction**, each with its own distinct symptoms. One must urgently and clearly understand these signs and apply these methods to avoid ruining one's aspirations for health; doing so leads to long life. The detailed discussions of these symptoms and their corresponding formulas are provided in the chapters that follow.

### Symptoms and Formulas for Discriminating the Upper Stoppage

**Upper Stoppage** <term>Upper Stoppage (Shang Pi 上痞): An obstruction occurring in the "Upper Burner" (the chest and head region), often caused by the accumulation of "heat" from potent medicines that has failed to circulate properly.</term> is characterized by dizziness, blurred vision, a flushed face, heart palpitations, and pain in the joints of the limbs. There is also **numbness** <note>original: "bu ren" (不仁) — literally "unbenevolence," a classical medical term for a loss of sensation or motor control.</note> in the anterior and posterior aspects of the body, accompanied by an abundance of phlegm.

Chop the above ingredients into a coarse powder. Simmer in five pints of water until three pints remain. Extract the clear liquid and divide it into two <gloss>one version says three</gloss> doses. Take after meals and just before retiring to bed.

# On the Discrimination of the Middle Stoppage and its Formula

**Middle Stoppage** is characterized by a sensation of fullness in the intestines, fatigue in the four limbs, and difficulty in walking or standing. The patient may vomit after eating, feel dizzy or clouded <note>The original term "maomei" (冒昧) here suggests a foggy or dimmed state of consciousness or vision.</note>, have a reduced appetite, or experience thirst. For these symptoms, the following formula should be used:

**Rhubarb** <gloss>one ounce; wrap in wet paper and roast until fragrant and cooked through, then slice into pieces</gloss>
**Areca Nut** <gloss>one piece</gloss>

<note>This text focuses on "Stoppage" (Pi), a traditional diagnostic category for internal blockages. The use of "roasting in wet paper" (wei 煨) was a specific pharmaceutical technique used to moderate the harsh purgative properties of herbs like Rhubarb, making them safer for the patient's stomach.</note>

### Formula

Grind the above into a fine powder. Mix with honeyed wine and take two mace <note>Approximately 7.5 grams.</note> per dose. Continue the treatment until the bowels are loosened.

### Discriminating the Signs of Lower Stoppage and its Formula

**Lower Stoppage** <term>Lower Stoppage (Xia xuan 下痃): A medical condition involving hard, cord-like accumulations or masses in the lower abdomen, often linked to stagnant fluids or energy.</term> is identified when there is difficulty urinating, a sensation of fullness and hardness below the navel, slurred or hesitant speech, pain in the lower back and spine, and a feeling of heaviness in the legs that makes it impossible to walk or stand. In such cases, the following formula should be used:

**Fringed Pink Seed Heads** <note>original: "Qumai touzi" (瞿麥頭子)</note> one ounce
**Cassia Bark** <note>original: "Guangui" (官桂)</note> one *fen* <note>A minute unit of weight, approximately 0.37 grams.</note>

Grind the ingredients listed above into a fine powder. Take one **boar’s kidney** <note>original: "fenzhúshen" (獖豬腎), specifically the kidney of a castrated pig. Wrap the kidney in wet paper and roast it slowly over a low fire until cooked through. On an empty stomach, chew the preparation thoroughly and wash it down with warm wine.

The patient should follow this regimen until the bowels are significantly loosened. If urination remains difficult and the area around the navel and abdomen has not softened, then administer the **supplementary formula**.

### Supplementary Formula

Take a one-inch section of **scallion stalk** <note>The white part of the green onion.</note> and remove the core. Insert one mace of **sal ammoniac** <term>Sal Ammoniac (Naosha 硇砂): A naturally occurring mineral salt (ammonium chloride) used in traditional medicine to "soften the hard" and break down stubborn abdominal masses or "stoppages."</term> powder into the hollowed center of the scallion and tie both ends with string. Wrap it in wet paper and roast until cooked. On an empty stomach, take this with cold, pure wine. Continue the treatment until it proves effective.

## Section 47: Discussion on the Intermixing of Treatments for Various Illnesses Leading to Fatal Signs

<summary>This page concludes the treatment for "Lower Stoppage" (Xia xuan) with formulas involving boar kidney and sal ammoniac, and begins Section 47 regarding the dangers of improper treatment combinations.</summary>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the discussion of specific formulas for "Lower Stoppage" (abdominal masses and urinary issues). Having provided specific remedies, the author now broadens the scope to discuss the general principles of selecting the correct medical modality and warns against the catastrophic errors of unlearned physicians.</meta>

Now, among those who are ill, there are some for whom **decoctions** <note>original: "tang" (湯), medicinal soups or liquid extracts.</note> are suitable, some for whom **pills** <note>original: "yuan" (圓/丸), medicinal ingredients ground and bound into spheres.</note> are suitable, and some for whom **powders** <note>original: "san" (散), medicine in loose, dry form.</note> are suitable. There are those suited for **purgation** <note>Methods to induce bowel movements to clear heat or blockages.</note>, those for **emetics** <note>Methods to induce vomiting to clear the upper body.</note>, those for **sweating** <note>Diaphoretic therapies to expel surface-level pathogens.</note>, those for **moxibustion** <note>The burning of mugwort (moxa) on or near the skin to stimulate healing.</note>, those for **acupuncture** <note>original: "zhen" (鍼), the use of needles to regulate energy flow.</note>, and those for **tonification** <note>Methods to supplement deficiencies in the body's vital energy.</note>. 

There are also those for whom **massage** is suitable, those for whom **guided breathing and stretching** <term>Daoyin (導引): An ancient system of movement, breath control, and stretching, serving as a precursor to modern Qigong.</term> are suitable, those for whom **steaming and hot compresses** <note>original: "zheng yun" (蒸熨), the application of heat and herbal vapors to the body.</note> are suitable, those for whom **medicinal bathing** is suitable, those for whom **joy and pleasure** are suitable, and those for whom **gentle relaxation** is suitable. Some are suited to the application of **water**, and some to the application of **fire**. Since the methods are so various, how could they ever be reduced to a single approach?

If a physician is not virtuous, skilled, and extensively learned, it is difficult for them to achieve a cure. Those of mediocre talent and shallow knowledge recklessly administer decoctions, pills, purgatives, sweating agents, tonics, or emetics, frequently causing these treatments to conflict or be misapplied. This causes mild illnesses to become severe, and severe illnesses to become fatal. This is a common occurrence throughout the world.

Furthermore, **decoctions** can cleanse the **internal organs** <term>Zang-Fu (臟腑): The collective term for the solid (Zang) and hollow (Fu) organs in traditional medicine.</term>, open the **channels and collaterals** <term>Jingluo (經絡): The network of pathways through which vital energy (Qi) and blood circulate.</term>, and harmonize **Yin and Yang** <note>The fundamental opposing yet complementary forces of the body and universe.</note>. They can expel and separate **evil influences** <note>original: "xie" (邪), referring to external pathogens like Wind, Cold, or Heat.</note>, moisten what is dry and decayed, nourish the skin, replenish vital energy and strength, and support those who are exhausted. For these purposes, one must never abandon the use of decoctions.

**Pills** can be used to expel Wind and Cold, break up **hard abdominal masses** <note>original: "jia" (瘕), specifically referring to masses that seem to have a distinct shape or location.</note>, eliminate accumulations and blockages, and improve the intake of food and drink.

<summary>This page emphasizes the necessity of matching the specific medical treatment—such as acupuncture, massage, or medicine—to the patient's unique condition, while criticizing incompetent doctors for misapplying these varied methods. It concludes by detailing the therapeutic functions of medicinal soups (decoctions) and pills.</summary>
