### Central Treasury of the Great Radiance

...to relax the nutritive and defensive energies <note>original: "rong wei" (榮衛). In Chinese medicine, "Ying" (nutritive) flows within the vessels, while "Wei" (defensive) protects the exterior of the body.</note>, and to open or close the apertures slowly and gently to achieve Great Harmony, nothing surpasses the use of **pills** <note>original: "yuan" (圓/丸), medicinal ingredients ground and bound into spheres.</note>.

As for **powders** <note>original: "san" (散), medicine in loose, dry form.</note>, they are capable of dispelling the seasonal vapors of wind, cold, summer-heat, and dampness. They seize the evils of cold-dampness and foul toxins, dispersing stagnation within the four limbs and cutting away the hidden knots within the **five viscera** <note>original: "wu zang" (五藏), referring to the heart, liver, spleen, lungs, and kidneys.</note>. For opening the intestines and harmonizing the stomach, or for moving the pulse and unblocking the channels, nothing surpasses the use of powders.

**Purgation** <note>original: "xia" (下), methods used to drain or clear the body downwards.</note> clears and opens blockages and obstructions. **Tonification** <note>original: "bu" (補)</note> supplements and assists where there is deficiency and exhaustion. **Moxibustion** <note>original: "jiu" (灸)</note> rouses the *Yin* and connects the *Yang*. **Acupuncture** <note>original: "zhen" (鍼)</note> moves the nutritive energy and guides the defensive energy. **Guided breathing and stretching** <term>Daoyin (導引): An ancient system of movement, breath control, and stretching, serving as a precursor to modern Qigong.</term> can drive out "guest evils" <note>External pathogens that have entered the body from the outside.</note> from the joints. **Massage** <note>original: "an mo" (按摩)</note> can drive out floating excesses from the flesh and muscles.

**Steaming and hot compresses** <note>original: "zheng yun" (蒸熨)</note> repel cold; **warm washing** generates *Yang*. **Joy and pleasure** refresh the spirit, while **gentle relaxation** settles the *Qi*.

If a condition is one of **Excess** <note>original: "shi" (實), an overabundance of a pathogen or internal pressure.</note> but one does not use purgation, it causes the heart and abdomen to become distended and full, leading to agitation, disorder, and drum-like swelling. If a condition is one of **Deficiency** <note>original: "xu" (虚), a lack of vital energy or blood.</note> but one does not use tonification, it causes the *Qi* and Blood to dissipate, the Essence and Spirit to be consumed, the muscles to waste away, and the consciousness to become clouded and confused. If **sweating** is appropriate but one does not induce it, it causes the pores to remain closed and blocked, leading to a suffocating death. If **vomiting** is required...

Food and water cannot be taken in, eventually leading to death.

When **massage** <note>original: "an mo" (按摩)</note> is suitable but not performed, it causes excess fluids to saturate the marrow and muscles, lingering there indefinitely without dispersing. 

When **steaming and fomentation** <note>original: "zheng yun" (蒸熨), the application of medicinal heat, steam, or heated wraps to the body.</note> are suitable but not performed, it allows cold vapors to lie latent, gradually developing into **numbness and reversal** <note>original: "bi jue" (痹厥), a condition characterized by the blockage of circulation and sensation, often resulting in cold, insensitive limbs.</note>. 

When **medicinal bathing** <note>original: "zao xi" (澡洗)</note> is suitable but not performed, it causes the *Yang* energy to rise excessively while *Yin* evils cause internal harm. 

Conversely, if **purgation** <note>original: "xia" (下), the use of medicine to drain the bowels.</note> is applied when it is not appropriate, it scours the stomach and opens the intestines, leading to uncontrollable and profuse diarrhea. 

If **diaphoresis** <note>original: "han" (汗), the induction of sweating to release pathogens from the surface of the body.</note> is applied when it is not appropriate, it causes the muscles to waste away and the **vital fluids** <note>original: "jin ye" (津液), the essential moisture of the body including sweat, saliva, and joint fluids.</note> to be utterly depleted.

If the mind and spirit are agitated and the **internal organs** <note>original: "zang fu" (臟腑), the functional systems of the body.</note> are surging and clashing, to apply **moxibustion** <note>original: "jiu" (灸), the therapeutic burning of aged mugwort on the skin.</note> when it is not indicated causes severe injury to the **meridians and collaterals** <note>original: "jing luo" (經絡), the network of pathways for vital energy and blood.</note>. 

Inflammatory toxins will accumulate within, harming the body's internal harmony and leading to an incurable state. If one performs **massage** <note>original: "an mo" (按摩)</note> when it is not indicated, it causes the muscles to swell and distend, and the tendons and bones to become overly loosened. 

If one applies **steaming and fomentation** <term>Zheng yun (蒸熨): the application of herbal steam or heated herbal packs to the body.</term> when it is not indicated, it causes **Yang Qi** <note>the active, warming energy of the body</note> to circulate excessively while **Ying Qi** <note>the nourishing, "shadow" energy</note> clusters too heavily within. 

If one applies **herbal drenching** <note>original: "lin qi" (淋漆), the practice of pouring or bathing in medicinal liquids.</note> when it is not indicated, it allows dampness to invade the skin and heat to be generated within the physical body. 

If one indulges in **pleasure and stimulation** <note>original: "yue yu" (悅愉). While usually a positive emotion, in this medical context it refers to excessive or inappropriate emotional arousal or sensory indulgence prescribed as therapy.</note> when it is not indicated, it causes the spirit to be lost and the Qi to vanish, leaving the mind unhappy. 

If one is too **relaxed or lenient** <note>original: "he huan" (和緩). Refers to a treatment style or lifestyle that is overly gentle or slow when a more vigorous intervention is required.</note> when it is not indicated, it causes the Qi to stagnate and the will to be broken, leading to forgetfulness and injury to one's resolve. 

In general, all medical treatments must be suited to the specific needs of the patient. If sweating has been induced but the pulse is not rapid, one must not use **purgatives** <note>original: "xia" (下), the "downward" method of clearing the bowels to remove heat or blockages.</note>. If the openings of the heart and stomach are not obstructed and the **proximal pulse** <note>original: "chi mai" (尺脈), the pulse felt at the third position of the wrist, associated with the kidneys and the lower body.</note> is faint and weak, one must not induce vomiting. If the joints are not constricted and the **Nutritive and Defensive Qi** <term>Ying Wei (榮衛): 'Ying' is the nutritive energy that flows with the blood, while 'Wei' is the protective energy that circulates on the body's surface.</term> are not obstructed, one must not use **acupuncture** <note>original: "zhen" (鍼)</note>. 

If the **Yin energy** is not excessive and the **Yang energy** is not deficient, do not use **moxibustion** <note>original: "jiu" (灸)</note>. If there are no "guest pathogens" <note>external environmental factors that have invaded the body</note> within, do not perform **guided breathing and stretching** <term>Daoyin (導引): therapeutic exercises combining movement and breath.</term>. If there is no "excessive Qi" on the exterior, do not perform massage. If the skin is not suffering from **numbness** <note>original: "bi" (痺), a condition of blockage causing pain or loss of sensation.</note>, do not use **steaming or heat-pressing** <term>Zheng yun (蒸熨): the application of medicinal steam or heated compresses to the body.</term>. 

If the muscles and flesh are not cold, do not use warm washing. If the spirit is not clouded or confused, do not [artificially] stimulate joy. If the vital energy is not rushing wildly, do not [attempt to] harmonize and slow it down. To follow these rules is to live; to go against them is to die. Furthermore, the methods for diagnosing pulse and disease have been fully described in the preceding sections.

In such a state, not one in a hundred will survive. Although

One who is still able to eat and drink, walk and stand, and appears composed, yet is unaware and does not realize that death is pressing near... <note>original: "bu wu bu zhi si zhi bi yi" (不悟不知死之逼矣). This refers to a deceptive state where the patient's spirit seems intact even as the body fails.</note> 

The general rules for these conditions are listed as follows:

In a disease where the patient has staring eyes, a craving for water, and a hard fullness below the heart <note>the epigastric region</note>, but the **pulse** <term>Mai (脉): the pulse, which in Chinese medicine reflects the state of the internal organs and the flow of Qi and Blood.</term> is **soggy and minute** <note>original: "ru er wei" (濡而微); "soggy" pulses are soft and floating, while "minute" pulses are extremely thin and weak.</note>.

Regarding cases of vomiting blood, nosebleeds, or blood in the stool, where the pulse is **floating, large, firm, and rapid** <note>original: "fu da lao shu" (浮大牢數); a "floating" pulse is felt at the surface, "large" is wide, "firm" is deep and hard, and "rapid" is fast.</note>.

In a disease where there is delirious speech, a feverish body, and cold hands and feet, but the pulse is **fine and minute** <note>original: "xi wei" (細微); a "fine" pulse is thin like a thread.</note>.

In a disease with severe, unstoppable diarrhea, but the pulse is **tight, large, and slippery** <note>original: "jin da er hua" (緊大而滑); a "tight" pulse feels like a strained rope, while "slippery" feels like pearls rolling on a plate.</note>.

In a disease where there is pain in the head and eyes, but the pulse is **choppy and short** <note>original: "se duan" (濇短); a "choppy" pulse feels hesitant or rough, like a knife scraping bamboo, and "short" means it does not fill its position in the wrist.</note>.

In a disease with pain inside the abdomen, but the pulse is **floating, large, and long** <note>original: "fu da er chang" (浮大而長); a "long" pulse extends beyond its normal boundaries.</note>.

In a disease with abdominal pain and panting <note>shortness of breath</note>, but the pulse is **slippery, smooth, rapid, and tight**.

In an **obese person** whose pulse is **fine and on the verge of vanishing**.

# Master Hua’s Central Treasury Medical Classic: Volume II

If a person’s **pulse** <term>Mai (脉): the pulse, used in traditional medicine to diagnose the flow of Qi and Blood.</term> is naturally **slippery and smooth** <note>original: "hua li" (滑利); a pulse that feels fluid and unhindered, often described as pearls rolling in a basin, indicating health or the presence of sufficient fluids.</note>, but conversely becomes **choppy** <term>Se (濇): a hesitant, rough pulse that feels like a knife scraping bamboo, indicating a depletion of blood or vital essence.</term>.

If a person’s pulse is naturally **long**, but conversely becomes **short** <note>A "long" pulse extends beyond its normal position along the radial artery; a "short" pulse fails to fill its position, suggesting a sudden collapse of the body's upright energy.</note>.

If a person’s **cubit pulse** <term>Chi (尺): the "proximal" pulse position located furthest from the hand, associated with the kidneys and the lower body.</term> corresponds to the **wrist position** <term>Cun kou (寸口): the "inch" or distal pulse position closest to the hand, associated with the lungs and the upper body.</term> but is **excessively slow**.

In a **warm-febrile disease** <term>Wen bing (溫病): a category of acute, infectious illnesses characterized by rapid onset, heat, and seasonal influence.</term>, when three or four days have passed without **sweating**, yet the pulse is **excessively rapid**.

In a warm-febrile disease where the pulse is **fine and faint**, its movement is **sluggish**, and the **stomach is obstructed** <note>original: "wei zhong bi" (胃中閉); a state where the digestive system is shut down, preventing the absorption of medicine or the movement of energy.</note>.

In a warm-febrile disease where the **fever is extreme**, yet the pulse is conversely **small**.

In a severe illness where the rhythm of the pulse is **irregular** <note>original: "bu tiao" (不調); literally "un-tuned," referring to a pulse that lacks a steady beat or consistent strength.</note>.

In a warm-febrile disease accompanied by **abdominal pain and diarrhea** <note>original: "xia li" (下痢); this combination of internal heat and lower depletion is considered a very dangerous sign.</note>.

In a warm-febrile disease where **sweat does not emerge**, or if it does emerge, it **does not reach the feet** <note>Traditional theory holds that for a fever to break properly, the "sweat of resolution" must be uniform. If it stops at the knees or waist, it indicates that the body's energy is too weak to push the pathogen all the way out.</note>.

In a febrile disease where sweat has already been produced, yet the pulse has not settled and instead continues to **fluctuate and grow larger** <note>Typically, sweating should "release" the heat and calm the pulse; if the pulse grows larger after sweating, it suggests the disease is worsening despite treatment.</note>.

In a case of **coughing** where the **pulse** <term>Mai (脉): the radial pulse, used to assess the state of internal organs and the flow of Qi.</term> is **rapid** <note>original: "shu" (數); a pulse with more than five beats per breath, often indicating heat.</note> and the body is **emaciated**.

In a case of **sudden, violent coughing** where the pulse is **scattered** <note>original: "san" (散); a pulse that feels chaotic and lacks a root, signifying the dispersion of vital energy.</note>.

In a case of coughing where the patient is **obese** in physique yet the pulse is **extremely urgent** <note>original: "ji jin" (緊急); a tight, forceful pulse like a stretched cord, which contradicts the damp nature of an obese physique.</note>.

In a case of coughing accompanied by **vomiting** and **fecal incontinence**, where the pulse is **wiry** <note>original: "xian" (弦); a pulse that feels like a guitar string.</note> and **about to cease**.

In cases of **various coughs and wheezing** where the pulse is both **deep** and **floating** <note>original: "chen er fu" (沉而浮); a contradictory pulse where it is felt at both the deepest and most superficial levels, suggesting a separation of Yin and Yang energies.</note>.

In a case of **ascending Qi** <term>Shang qi (上氣): a condition of severe breathlessness or asthma where the breath seems to move upward without descending.</term> where the pulse is **rapid**.

In a case where the **muscles are feverish**, the body is **emaciated**, and there is a **prolapsed rectum** with a fever that will not subside, while the pulse is **extremely urgent**.

In a case of **intestinal stagnation** <term>Chang pi (腸癖): a chronic digestive disorder often involving blood or mucus in the stool, similar to dysentery.</term> with **muscle contractures** <term>Zhuan jin (轉筋): painful cramping or spasms of the tendons and muscles.</term>, where the pulse is **extremely rapid**.

In a case of **Wind-stroke** <term>Zhong feng (中風): a sudden affliction causing paralysis or loss of consciousness, traditionally attributed to internal or external "wind."</term> leading to **atrophy** <term>Wei ji (痿疾): a "wilting" disease characterized by the weakening and wasting of the limbs.</term> and **numbness**, where the pulse is **urgent and tense**.

<summary>This page lists specific clinical scenarios involving respiratory and digestive distress where the pulse qualities (such as scattered, rapid, or urgent) signal a terminal prognosis. These "counter-indications" highlight the importance of the pulse matching the patient's physical condition for a favorable recovery.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, pulse diagnosis, coughing, Wind-stroke, atrophy, prognosis, Chinese medicine, respiratory illness, dysentery</keywords>

In a case of **breathlessness** and **panting** with urgent respiration, where the **pulse** <term>Mai (脉): the radial pulse, used to assess the state of internal organs and the flow of Qi.</term> is **choppy** <note>original: "se" (澀); a rough pulse that feels like a knife scraping bamboo, indicating a depletion of blood or essence.</note> after four cycles.

In a case of **chills and fever** accompanied by **scrofula** <term>Luoli (瘻癧): swellings or nodules on the neck, often associated with chronic wasted conditions or tuberculosis of the lymph nodes.</term>, where the pulse is **large** <note>original: "da" (大); a pulse that feels wide and fills the finger, which in a chronic wasting disease suggests a dangerous flare of "false fire" without substance.</note>.

In a case of **incised wounds** <term>Jinchuang (金瘡): literally "metal wounds," referring to injuries caused by knives, swords, or other metal tools.</term> where the **bleeding does not stop** and the pulse is **large**.

In a case of **injuries from falling** and **internal trauma**, where the pulse is **small and weak**. <note>A weak pulse after trauma suggests that the body's vital energy (Qi) has collapsed and cannot respond to the injury.</note>

In a case of **cold-damage** <term>Shanghan (傷寒): a broad category of acute febrile diseases caused by external pathogens entering the body.</term> where the **body heat is extreme**, but the pulse is **contrarily small**. <note>This is a "discordant" sign; a high fever should produce a large, rapid pulse. A small pulse indicates the body's strength is exhausted while the fever rages.</note>

In a case of **cold reversal** <term>Jue ni (厥逆): a condition where the limbs become icy cold due to the failure of Yang energy to reach the extremities, often preceding collapse.</term> with **sweating**, where the pulse is **empty** and **relaxed** <note>original: "xu er huan" (虛而緩); "empty" means it lacks force when pressed, and "relaxed" refers to a slow, loose beat.</note>.

In a case of **profuse diarrhea** <term>Dong xie (洞泄): literally "cavernous" or "penetrating" diarrhea, where food passes through the body undigested and at high frequency.</term> where the patient **cannot eat** and the pulse is **urgent**.

In a case of **dysentery** <term>Chang pi (腸澼): a disorder characterized by blood and mucus in the stool, similar to modern dysentery.</term> where the patient **discharges white pus**.

In a case of **dysentery** where the patient **discharges pus and blood**, and the pulse is **suspended and severed** <note>original: "xuan jue" (懸絕); a pulse that feels isolated from the normal rhythm or intermittently disappears, signaling the end of the organ's vital energy.</note>.

When the body is full of fluid but the pulse is thin and rough, it indicates the vital essence is depleted.</note>

In diarrhea, a large and slippery pulse indicates a dangerous excess of heat or dampness that is overwhelming the patient's strength.</note>

In cases of **sudden death** <term>Zusi (卒死): refers to a sudden collapse or loss of consciousness where the patient appears dead or is near death.</term> where the pulse is **hard** <note>original: "jian" (堅); unyielding and stiff.</note> yet **fine and minute**. <note>A pulse that is both hard (signifying extreme tension) and minute (signifying exhaustion of vital energy) indicates a complete breakdown of the body's internal balance.</note>

In cases of **febrile disease** <note>Ilnesses characterized by high fever, typically of an infectious nature.</note> lasting three to five days, with **headache and body heat**, where the patient **eats as usual**, but the pulse is **straight and rapid**: they will die on the **eighth day**. <note>original: "zhi er ji" (直而疾). Also in chronic illness, when the pulse is **empty and slow**, **empty and minute**, **empty and slippery**, or **wiry and urgent**. <note>These variations of "empty" (xu) pulses in a long-term patient signal the fundamental depletion of the body's life force.</note>

In cases of **sudden illness** where the pulse is **wiry and rapid**. <note>A "wiry" (xian) pulse feels like a guitar string; when combined with rapid speed in a sudden onset, it indicates extreme internal agitation or "fire."</note>

**All these are ominous pulses and cannot be treated.**

## Chapter 49: Determining Death by Observing Voice, Color, Form, and Signs

In general, for a human being, the **five viscera and six bowels** <term>Zang-fu (臟腑): the internal organs. The five Zang (Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lung, Kidney) store essence, while the six Fu (Gallbladder, Stomach, Large Intestine, Small Intestine, Bladder, Triple Burner) process and transport substances.</term>, as well as the **nutrient and defensive systems** <term>Ying-wei (榮衛): 'Ying' refers to the nutrient energy circulating in the blood, while 'Wei' refers to the defensive energy circulating outside the vessels to protect the body.</term> and the **sensory orifices**, should all be in a state of balance. The **vital breath and blood** <term>Qi and Xue (氣血): the two fundamental substances of life; Qi is the motive force, and blood is the nourishing fluid.</term> should follow a smooth and orderly **circulation** without... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, leading into the specific physical signs of impending death on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a list of "fatal" pulse readings, emphasizing that discordant signs—such as a healthy appetite during a lethal fever—predict death. It begins a new chapter on diagnosing terminal cases through the observation of physical appearance and the flow of vital energy.</summary>

<margin>Faint notes appear here in the gutter of the page, likely regarding the cultivation of the physician’s internal sight.</margin>

Ultimately, this constitutes the **foundation of remaining free from illness**. If there is any deficiency or severance of this foundation, then calamity will surely follow. The essential key is that when attending to the sick, one must **preserve the spirit** <term>Cunshen (存神): A meditative practice of maintaining internal focus to prevent the dissipation of vital energy and to sharpen the senses.</term> and practice **inner reflection**; one must **calm the breath** and engage in **inner observation**. <term>Neiguan (內觀): A Daoist-influenced technique of "looking inward" to perceive the state of the internal organs and the flow of Qi through the body.</term> 

The heart-mind must not gaze outward randomly, but rather focus one's intent on precise investigation. Only then can one communicate with **divine illumination** <note>original: "Shenming" (神明); in this context, it refers to a state of heightened clinical intuition and spiritual clarity that allows a doctor to see beyond surface symptoms.</note>, explore the hidden and the subtle, and determine life or death with such accuracy that there is not a single error in a thousand cases. The **symptoms and omens** <term>Zhengzhao (證兆): Clinical signs that serve as prognostic indicators, often signaling the final stages of a disease.</term> are as follows: <insert>below</insert>

*   A **black color** arising from the ears, eyes, or above the nose, which gradually moves toward the mouth. <note>In traditional Chinese face-reading diagnosis, black indicates the exhaustion of Kidney essence or the presence of extreme "Cold" and stagnation.</note>
*   A **red color** appearing at the ears, eyes, and forehead that has persisted for five days.
*   **Black and white colors** entering the mouth, nose, and eyes that have persisted for five days.
*   A black color, or one resembling **horse-liver**, which looks blue-green from a distance but black when viewed closely. <term>Magansi (馬肝色): A dark, dull, and lusterless color associated with the total failure of Liver Qi; it is considered one of the "dead" colors in traditional physiognomy.</term>
*   The **mouth held open like a fish**, where the breath goes out but does not return. <term>Yukou (魚口): "Fish-mouth" breathing; a terminal sign of impending respiratory failure where the patient gasps for air but can no longer effectively inhale.</term>

**Those who suffer from chronic pain of the head and eyes and then suddenly lose the clarity of their vision.** <note>original: "toumu jiutong cu shi buming" (頭目久痛卒視不明); chronic pain suggests long-term depletion, while the sudden loss of sight indicates the total failure of the "Essence" of the five viscera, which is said to ascend to nourish the eyes.</note>

**Those whose tongues are curled and whose testicles are shrunken.** <note>original: "shejuan luansuo" (舌卷卵縮); in traditional Chinese medicine, the Liver meridian wraps around the genitals and connects to the root of the tongue. The retraction of these two areas indicates the complete collapse of Liver-energy.</note>

**Those whose faces are black and who stare straight ahead with a fixed gaze.** <note>original: "mianhei zhishi" (面黑直視); a black complexion signifies the exhaustion of the Kidneys, while a fixed, unmoving gaze (straight-staring) indicates that the spirit (*Shen*) has left the eyes.</note>

**Those whose faces are blue-green and whose eyes are white.** <term>Qing (青): A blue-green, leaden, or ashen color associated with the Liver and extreme pain, internal cold, or blood stasis.</term>

**Those whose faces are yellow and whose eyes are white.** <note>original: "mianhuang mubai" (面黃目白); yellow indicates Spleen failure, while white eyes suggest a total loss of blood and vital essence.</note>

**Those whose faces and eyes are both white.** <note>This "whiteness" refers to a ghostly, bloodless complexion and a lack of luster in the eyes, showing that both the Qi and the Blood have been depleted.</note>

**Those whose faces and eyes are blue-black.** <note>original: "mianmu qinghei" (面目青黑); the combination of the colors of the Liver (blue-green) and Kidneys (black) without any healthy luster indicates that the "Mother-Son" relationship of these two organs has collapsed into stagnation and death.</note>

**Those whose faces are blue-green and whose lips are black.** <note>The lips are the manifestation of the Spleen; black lips indicate the "earth" (Spleen) has been overcome by "water" (Kidney failure) or extreme cold.</note>

**Those whose hair is dry like hemp and whose joy and anger are unregulated.** <note>original: "fa ru ma, xi nu bu tiao" (髪如麻喜怒不調); hair is the "surplus of the blood." Hair that becomes brittle and dry like hemp fibers indicates the internal fluids are dried up. Erratic emotions signify that the Mind/Spirit is no longer housed by the internal organs.</note>

**When the internal and external functions of the five viscera are exhausted, the spirit-energy is no longer guarded within, and the voice becomes hoarse.** <note>original: "wuzang neiwai jue shenqi bushou qi sheng si zhe" (五臟內外絕神氣不守其聲嘶者). The "Five Viscera" (Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lung, Kidney) are the pillars of life; "Spirit-energy" (*Shenqi*) refers to the vital spark that maintains consciousness and bodily integrity. A hoarse, "broken" voice indicates the collapse of Lung-Qi.</note>
<column-break/>
**When both Yin and Yang are exhausted and the eye sockets have sunken inward.** <note>Sunken eyes indicate the total depletion of "Essence" (*Jing*) and fluids, leaving the "empty" sockets as a sign that the body can no longer nourish its sensory portals.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the lips are turned outward and the philtrum is full.** <term>Renzhong (人中): The vertical groove between the nose and the upper lip. In Chinese medicine, this is a vital junction point; if it becomes "full" (losing its groove) while the lips retract, it signifies the exhaustion of the Spleen and the governing vessels.</term>
<column-break/>
**When sweat emerges but does not flow.** <note>original: "han chu bu liu zhe" (汗出不流者). This refers to "oil sweat" or "beady sweat," where perspiration clings to the skin like pearls without running. This is a classic sign of the "Separation of Yin and Yang," where the body's protective energy can no longer hold fluids inside.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the flesh beneath the nails of the hands and feet turns a dark, black color.** <note>Blackness under the nails indicates that blood circulation has ceased and the "Kidney-water" energy is overwhelmed by cold or total stagnation.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the palms are swollen until the lines disappear, the navel protrudes, and both the scrotum and penis are swollen.** <note>The loss of palm lines due to edema suggests the total failure of the Spleen to transform dampness. The swelling of the genitals and navel indicates the collapse of the "Ancestral Qi" and the Liver meridian, which governs the lower regions.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the complexion is a deathly ashen-black and sudden swelling occurs.** <note>A "蒼黑" (cāng hēi) complexion—resembling the color of weathered lead or deep charcoal—combined with sudden edema (swelling) indicates that the body's fundamental "Qi" has been completely extinguished.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the complexion is black, the flanks feel full and distended, and the patient cannot turn from side to side.** <note>Distension in the flanks (the sides of the torso) combined with an inability to move suggests that the Liver and Gallbladder energies are entirely blocked, and the life-force can no longer facilitate movement.</note>
<column-break/>
**When the hair on the head stands straight up as if being thrust upward.** <note>original: "fajian ru chongqi zhe" (髮肩如衝起者). This describes hair that becomes dry, brittle, and stands on end, a phenomenon believed to occur when the last vestiges of "Qi" rush upward to escape the body through the crown of the head.</note>

**When the Nutritive and Defensive energies are dissipated, and the face and eyes exhibit swelling.** <term>Nutritive and Defensive Qi (Rong-Wei): The two primary systems of circulation. "Rong" (Nutritive) flows within the vessels to sustain the organs, while "Wei" (Defensive) flows outside them to protect the body. Their dissipation signifies the total collapse of the body's internal and external integrity.</term>

---

**When the Heart is exhausted in relation to the Kidney; the patient gasps with the shoulders, the gaze wanders, or the eyes are fixed straight—death occurs within one day.** <note>"Shoulder-breathing" (*jianxi*) is the use of the accessory muscles to breathe when the lungs and heart can no longer function, causing the shoulders to rise and fall rhythmically with each gasp.</note>

---

**When the Lungs are exhausted, the vital breath departs and does not return, and the mouth is shaped like a fish's mouth—death occurs within three days.** <note>A "fish-mouth" (*yukou*) refers to a specific type of terminal gasping where the mouth opens wide but no air seems to reach the lungs, resembling a fish out of water.</note>

---

**When the Bones are exhausted, there is pain in the lower back and spine, a feeling of heaviness within the Kidneys such that one cannot turn over, and the hollows behind the feet and knees become flat—death occurs within five days.** <note>Traditional Chinese medicine posits that the Kidneys govern the bones. The flattening of the natural contours of the joints suggests the total loss of muscle tone and "Essence" (*Jing*) that normally fills the structural frame.</note>

---

**When the Kidneys are exhausted, the stool is red and difficult or contains blood, the ears are dry, the feet are swollen, and the tongue is swollen—death occurs within six days.** 

**Furthermore, if the feet are swollen, death occurs within nine days.** <note>The Kidneys open into the ears and regulate the water passages; thus, dryness of the ears and edema (swelling) of the lower extremities are signs of the Kidneys' failure to manage the body's fluids.</note>

<summary>This page details the specific physical manifestations of organ failure—specifically the Heart, Lungs, and Kidneys—and provides a prognostic timeline for the remaining days of life.</summary>
<keywords>terminal diagnosis, death signs, Heart exhaustion, Lung exhaustion, Kidney exhaustion, Nutritive and Defensive Qi, prognostic timelines</keywords>

# Central Treasury Medical Classic: Volume II

**When the Spleen is exhausted, the mouth becomes cold, the feet swell with edema, and the abdomen distends. If there is involuntary diarrhea—death occurs within twelve days.** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, the Spleen is the "root of post-natal energy," responsible for transforming food into vitality and managing fluids. A cold mouth and distension indicate the total collapse of digestive fire and the body's inability to hold back waste.</note>

---

**When the Sinews are exhausted, the Ethereal Soul is startled, and the patient suffers from irrational terror. The fingernails and toenails turn a blue-green hue, and the patient shouts and curses without ceasing—death occurs within eight or nine days.** <term>Ethereal Soul (Hun): The "Hun" is the aspect of the human spirit associated with the Liver and the element of Wood. It is responsible for dreaming, planning, and mental movement. When the "Hun" is startled or detached, the patient loses their grip on reality, often manifesting as delirium or outbursts.</term> <note>The fingernails are considered the "surplus of the sinews." Their blue-green color (*qing*) indicates a total cessation of blood flow and the failure of the Liver-Wood system.</note>

---

**When the Liver is exhausted, sweat pours forth like water, and the patient is fearful and restless. They lie face down with eyes fixed in a straight stare, and the face turns a blue-green hue—death occurs within eight days.** <note>The "straight stare" (*mu zhi*) refers to a loss of ocular movement, suggesting that the Liver, which "opens into the eyes," can no longer provide the vitality needed for sight or focus.</note>

---

**When the Stomach is exhausted, the teeth fall out and the face turns a deep yellow—death occurs within seven days.** <note>The Stomach and Large Intestine channels govern the gums. The falling of teeth indicates the withdrawal of vital energy from the "Sea of Water and Grain" (the Stomach), while the yellow hue is the color of the Earth element reflecting its final, failing state.</note>

---

**In all such cases, one must observe and listen to the patient with the utmost care, deliberating on these signs with great detail.**

<note>original: "華氏中藏經卷中終"</note>

<note>Master Hua refers to the legendary physician Hua Tuo (c. 140–208 CE), a figure of near-mythic status in Chinese medicine known for his skill in surgery and anesthesia. Although the *Central Treasury* (Zhong Zang) is attributed to him, scholars generally date this specific compilation to the late Tang Dynasty (circa 800 CE). The "Central Treasury" refers to the internal organs, which were viewed as "storehouses" or "treasuries" for vital essence and spirit. In traditional book structures, the "Middle Volume" (*Juan Zhong*) often contains the core clinical observations, following the theoretical foundations of the first volume and preceding the specific medicinal formulas of the third.</note>

# Master Hua’s Central Treasury Medical Classic, Volume III

## Written by Hua Tuo (Yuanhua) of Qiao Commandery, Han Dynasty

<note>Hua Tuo (c. 140–208 CE) is arguably the most famous physician in Chinese history, credited with pioneering anesthesia and surgery. "Yuanhua" was his courtesy name. While tradition attributes this work to him, the text's sophisticated categorization suggests it was compiled during the Tang Dynasty (c. 800 CE) to preserve his school's teachings on the "Treasury" (the internal organs).</note>

## Sixty Medicinal Formulas for Treating Various Diseases

## The All-Effective Pill

<note>original: "Wan Ying Yuan" (萬應圓). This "Ten-Thousand Responses" pill is a powerful, broad-spectrum formula designed to "drive out" pathogenic accumulations of water, phlegm, and stagnant blood.</note>

Gansui <gloss>3 ounces</gloss> <term>Gansui: Euphorbia kansui, a toxic root used to purge fluids through the bowels.</term> Yuanhua <gloss>3 ounces</gloss> <term>Yuanhua: Genkwa flower, used to treat severe edema.</term> Daji <gloss>3 ounces</gloss> <term>Daji: Knoxia root, the third of the powerful "water-driving" herbs.</term> Rhubarb <gloss>3 ounces</gloss>

Bur-reed Rhizome <gloss>3 ounces</gloss> Croton Seed <gloss>2 ounces, with skins</gloss> <note>Croton seeds are among the most violent purgatives in the pharmacopoeia.</note> Dried Lacquer <gloss>2 ounces, stir-fried</gloss> Zedoary Rhizome <gloss>2 ounces</gloss>

Angelica Sinensis <gloss>5 ounces</gloss> <note>Also known as Danggui, used here to harmonize and protect the blood during the purge.</note> Mulberry Bark <gloss>2 ounces</gloss> Borax <gloss>3 ounces</gloss> Water Plantain <gloss>8 ounces</gloss>

Gardenia Seed <gloss>2 ounces</gloss> Areca Nut <gloss>1 ounce</gloss> Akebia Stem <gloss>1 ounce</gloss> Thunder Ball <gloss>1 ounce</gloss> <term>Thunder Ball (Leiwan): A medicinal fungus used primarily to kill parasites.</term>

Myrobalan Fruit <gloss>1 ounce</gloss> Black Morning Glory Seed <gloss>5 ounces</gloss> Flying Squirrel Droppings <gloss>5 ounces</gloss> <term>Wulingzhi: The feces of the complex-toothed flying squirrel, used to break up blood stasis.</term> Honey Locust Fruit <gloss>7 pieces</gloss> <gloss>remove the skin and fibers</gloss>

<margin>Central Treasury Classic, Volume III</margin>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the final volume of the Central Treasury Medical Classic, pivoting from diagnostic signs to specific medicinal prescriptions. The twenty ingredients listed above, combined with the previous twenty, make forty ingredients in total. Grind and sift them together into a fine powder. Use vinegar to boil wheat flour into a paste <note>Wheat flour paste serves as a medicinal binder to form the powder into pills, while vinegar is often used to enhance the "blood-moving" and pain-relieving properties of the herbs.</note>.

<column-break/>

Rehmannia 3 ounces <term>Rehmannia: The root of Rehmannia glutinosa, used here to nourish the blood and fluids, counteracting the dryness of the purgatives.</term>
Turtle Carapace <gloss>vinegar-toasted</gloss> 3 ounces <note>The shell of the soft-shelled turtle is toasted with vinegar to make it brittle for grinding and to enhance its ability to soften "hardness" (tumors or masses) in the body.</note>
Green Tangerine Peel 3 ounces
Arisaema <gloss>boiled in starch water until soft, sliced, and roasted</gloss> 2 ounces <term>Arisaema: A toxic tuber (Tian Nan Xing) treated here to safely resolve stubborn phlegm.</term>

<column-break/>

White Morning Glory Seed 2 ounces
Dried Ginger 2 ounces
Aged Tangerine Peel 2 ounces
Field Mustard <gloss>stir-fried</gloss> 2 ounces

<column-break/>

Poria Mushroom 1 ounce <term>Poria: A wood-decay fungus used to drain dampness and calm the spirit.</term>
Red Peony Root 1 ounce
Szechuan Lovage Root 2 ounces
Peony Tree Bark 2 ounces

<column-break/>

White Atractylodes 1 ounce <term>White Atractylodes: A classic herb for strengthening the Spleen and improving digestion.</term>
Astragalus 1 ounce <term>Astragalus: One of the most important Qi-tonifying herbs in the pharmacopoeia.</term>
Myrrh 1 ounce <term>Myrrh: An imported resin used to move blood and alleviate pain.</term>
Aconite <gloss>peeled, roasted, and with the base removed</gloss> 1 ounce <note>Aconite is highly toxic; the "roasting" (pao) process is essential to neutralize its poison while retaining its ability to restore devastated Yang energy.</note>

<column-break/>

Costus Root 1 ounce
Clove 1 ounce
Cinnamon Bark <gloss>scraped of outer bark</gloss> 1 ounce
Nutmeg 1 ounce

<column-break/>

Chop the twenty items listed above into small pieces and wash them clean. Soak them in two *dou* <note>original: "er dou" (二斗). A dou was a volume measure; in the Tang Dynasty, two dou would be roughly 12 liters.</note> of rice vinegar for three days. Place the mixture into a silver or stone vessel <note>Silver and stone were preferred over iron for this preparation to prevent chemical reactions that might alter the medicinal properties of the tannins and resins.</note> and simmer over a slow fire until the vinegar has completely evaporated. Roast until dry and slightly charred, then stir-fry until the herbs turn a yellow color, ensuring you preserve the inner medicinal essence <note>original: "cun xing" (存性), a technical term meaning to char the outside of an herb while keeping the internal chemical properties intact.</note>. Finally, incorporate the subsequent medicines.

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous list of forty ingredients, moving from the physical preparation of the medicinal powder to the ritual requirements for compounding the "All-Effective Pill" and the specific dosages required for various internal conditions.</meta>

Form the mixture into pills the size of **mung beans**. The specific dosages and methods of administration are carefully detailed below. When compounding this medicine, one must remain in a single, clean, and quiet room. Inside, first practice strict purification: fast the mind and cleanse it of worldly worries. Burn incense and offer sincere, devout prayers to the holy spirits of all directions to assist the potency of the medicine; this will make its effects exceptionally swift.

<column-break/>

For cases of **chest bind** <note>original: "jie xiong." A condition where pathogenic heat or phlegm is trapped in the chest, causing pain and hardness to the touch.</note> or **Cold Damage** <note>original: "shang han." A broad category of febrile illnesses caused by external pathogens.</note> disorders, take seven pills with oily rice water <term>Oily rice water: The thin, nutritious liquid skimmed from the top of cooking grain, used to protect the stomach lining from harsh medicines.</term>. This should trigger the expulsion of "evil substances" from the bowels. After enough time has passed for a person to walk twenty *li* <note>About 6 miles; a traditional measure of time based on walking speed.</note>, if there has been no bowel movement, <gloss>take another dose.</gloss>

<column-break/>

For chronic accumulations and knots, stagnant food, or **abdominal masses** <term>Abdominal masses: "Jia kuai," referring to hard lumps or tumors in the belly caused by stagnant blood or energy.</term> that have persisted for many years: take three to five pills with water before retiring to bed. Take these every night until the illness ceases. If you can recall the specific food that originally caused the accumulation, take the pills with a broth made from that same food. In such cases, take seven pills. <gloss>Children, pregnant women, and the elderly must not take this.</gloss> <note>The formula is a powerful purgative; this warning serves to protect those with fragile constitutions from its harsh effects.</note>

<column-break/>

For those suffering from **"water qi"** <note>A term for edema or fluid retention.</note> where the whole body is swollen or jaundiced, take five pills with Poria Decoction <term>Poria Decoction: A medicinal tea made from the Poria mushroom (Fu Ling), used to drain excess fluids and support the Spleen.</term>. Take two doses per day until the water retention is resolved.

If you wish to **dispel the effects of alcohol** <term>original: "xiao jiu." Referring to relieving intoxication or the toxic effects of excessive drinking.</term> or improve the intake of food, take one pill with **ginger soup**.

For all types of **abdominal pain** occurring after a meal, take seven pills with **vinegar soup** <note>Vinegar was often used in traditional medicine to "smooth" the Liver qi and alleviate sharp, stabbing pains.</note>.

For **chest congestion** <term>original: "ge qi." A sensation of qi being blocked in the diaphragm.</term> and **choking disorders**, take three pills with **clove soup**.
<gloss>Take one dose at night.</gloss>

For conditions arising from **emotional distress, exhaustion, or fright**, take seven pills with **turtle-shell decoction** <note>original: "jia tang." Likely referring to *Bie Jia* (soft-shell turtle), used in Chinese medicine to nourish the Yin and settle the spirit during times of "labor-exhaustion" or sudden shock.</note>.
<gloss>Take three doses daily; reduce the dosage as the patient gradually stabilizes.</gloss>

For **small intestine hernia** and obstructive qi <term>original: "pi qi." Refers to stagnant energy that causes bloating or hard lumps in the abdomen.</term>, take three pills with **fennel soup**.

For **blockage of the bowels or urine**, take five pills with **honey water**.
<gloss>If the passage does not open, increase the dose to seven pills.</gloss>

For the **nine types of heart pain** <note>This traditional category often encompasses what we now recognize as severe epigastric or stomach pain, as well as chest pain.</note>, take five pills with **evodia soup** <note>original: "zhuyu tang." *Evodia rutaecarpa* is a warming herb used specifically to treat "cold" pain in the middle of the body.</note>.
<gloss>The pain will stop immediately.</gloss>

For **"corpse infusion"** <term>original: "shi zhu." A traditional diagnosis for chronic, wasting illnesses that seem to "haunt" a patient, often characterized by wandering pains or parasitic infections.</term> and **roaming pains**, take three pills with **papaya soup**.

For **leg qi** <note>original: "jiao qi." Often corresponding to Beriberi, a condition causing swelling, numbness, or weakness in the legs due to nutritional deficiency or "dampness."</note>, take five pills with **photinia soup** <note>original: "shinan tang." Photinia was traditionally used to dispel "wind" and "dampness" from the joints and limbs.</note>.
<gloss>Take every day before meals.</gloss>

For cases of **sudden death** <note>original: "zu si." Refers to a sudden collapse or loss of consciousness where the person appears dead but the "qi" or vital energy has not yet fully departed the body.</note> where the breath has not yet been cut off, dissolve seven pills in **urine** <note>Historically, "boys' urine" (*tong bian*) was used in Chinese medicine as a rapid-acting vehicle to down-bear fire and transform blood stasis in emergencies.</note> and pour it into the mouth; the patient will revive immediately.

For **postpartum blood stasis** <term>original: "xie bu xing." Literally "blood not flowing," referring to the failure to discharge lochia or internal stagnation after childbirth.</term>, take three pills with **angelica wine** <note>Angelica sinensis (*Dang Gui*) is the primary herb for tonifying and harmonizing the blood in women's health.</note>.

For blood-related fainting, delusions, exhaustion, bloody dysentery, distention, stabbing pains, blood masses, accumulations, or **abdominal tumors** <term>original: "zheng jia." Fixed and moveable masses or growths in the abdomen.</term>, take two pills daily with **angelica wine**.

For **difficult labor** <note>original: "nan chan." The text specifically mentions "heng dao," referring to transverse or breech presentations where the infant is not positioned correctly for birth.</note>, take two pills with **elm bark decoction** <note>Elm bark (*yu bai pi*) has a slippery, mucilaginous quality, used in traditional formulas to "lubricate" and ease the passage of the infant.</note>.

For a **retained placenta** <note>original: "tai yi bu xia." Literally "the fetal clothing does not descend."</note>, heat a **steelyard weight** <note>original: "cheng chui." A metal counterweight from a traditional scale, used here for its mineral essence and heat-retaining properties.</note> until it is glowing red, quench it in wine, and while the liquid is still hot, take two pills. 
<insert>Pregnant women must not take this medicine under normal circumstances; it is strictly for use in cases of urgent or difficult labor.</insert>

For **spleen-related diarrhea** or bloody dysentery, take one pill with **dried ginger soup**.

For **red and white dysentery** <note>A classification referring to stools containing both blood (red) and mucus (white), typically associated with inflammatory or infectious conditions of the intestines.</note>, take one pill with **licorice and dried ginger soup**.

### Central Treasury Classic, Volume II

For **red dysentery** <term>original: "chi li." A condition characterized by bloody stools, typically associated with "damp-heat" in the intestines.</term>, take one pill with **licorice decoction**.

For **white dysentery** <term>original: "bai li." Characterized by white, mucoid stools, typically associated with "cold-dampness" in the digestive tract.</term>, take one pill with **dried ginger decoction** <note>Dried ginger (*gan jiang*) is a warming herb used here to counteract the internal cold causing the white discharge.</note>.

For **stomach cold** <note>Refers to a deficiency of functional warmth in the digestive system.</note> resulting in vomiting, reflux, or the regurgitation of food, take two pills with **clove decoction** <note>Cloves (*ding xiang*) are used in traditional medicine to warm the stomach and "down-bear" rebellious qi that causes vomiting.</note>.

For **sudden, unbearable pain** in the heart or abdomen, take three pills with **hot vinegar and salt water**.

For **regular maintenance**, take one pill with clear tea before retiring for the night.

For the **five types of epilepsy** <note>Traditional Chinese medicine historically categorized seizures into five types, often named after the animal sounds a patient might make during an episode (cow, horse, pig, sheep, or bird).</note>, take one pill with cow’s milk <gloss>twice daily</gloss>.

For the onset of **malarial chills and fever** <term>original: "nue." A broad term for intermittent fevers, often translated as malaria.</term>, dissolve ten pills in a mixture of **boys' urine** <note>Historically, "boys' urine" (*tong bian*) was considered a potent medicinal vehicle used to guide medicine rapidly through the body's channels and resolve severe stagnation.</note> and wine and pour it into the patient. Once they experience vomiting or diarrhea, they will recover immediately; its efficacy is like that of the gods.

<note>The "Six-Spirit Pill" (*Liu Shen Dan*) is a name for a highly prestigious and potent formula intended to treat a vast array of ("ten thousand") illnesses, often focusing on clearing toxins and reviving the spirit.</note>

### Central Treasury Classic, Volume II

**Realgar** one *liang* <gloss>ground</gloss>
**Alum** one *liang* <gloss>roasted</gloss>
**Croton Seed** one *liang* <gloss>peeled</gloss> <note>Croton seed (*ba dou*) is a powerful and toxic purgative used in traditional medicine to "break up" severe internal stagnation.</note>
**Aconite** one *liang* <gloss>prepared</gloss> <note>Aconite (*fuzi*) is a highly toxic root that must be "炮" (processed with heat) to reduce its toxicity while retaining its warming properties.</note>
**Veratrum** three *liang*
**Cinnabar** two *liang* <gloss>one liang ground separately</gloss> <gloss>to serve as</gloss> one *liang* <gloss>coating</gloss> <note>Cinnabar, or mercury sulfide, was often used to coat pills to preserve them and provide a sedative effect, despite its modern-known toxicity.</note>

Grind the above ingredients into a fine powder and mix with refined honey to form pills the size of small beans. A second batch should be prepared the size of broomcorn millet seeds <note>Broomcorn millet (*shu mi*) is significantly smaller than a bean, allowing for micro-dosing for children.</note>. For the hundred diseases of men, take two pills with liquid. For children, measure the dosage accordingly and administer the smaller pills. If a purge is achieved <note>The text suggests that the medicine works by inducing a bowel movement; "obtaining benefit" (li) in this context means the medicine has successfully moved the bowels to expel the illness.</note>, the patient will recover.

**Benzoin Pills**
These pills treat "Transmitted Corpse" consumption <term>original: "chuan shi." An ancient term for tuberculosis, suggesting the disease was "transmitted" from the deceased to the living.</term>, lung atrophy, "bone-steaming" fever <term>original: "gu zheng." A sensation of heat deep within the body, characteristic of chronic wasting diseases.</term>, "ghost-infusion" syndrome <note>A category of sudden, severe, or infectious illnesses traditionally attributed to malevolent influences.</note>, sudden heart and abdominal pain, "sudden turmoil" disorder <term>original: "huo luan." A condition of acute, simultaneous vomiting and diarrhea, often corresponding to cholera.</term>, seasonal miasmic malaria, the five types of bloody discharge, blood blockage <note>Commonly referring to amenorrhea or severe menstrual stasis.</note>, abdominal masses, carbuncles and boils, fright-induced seizures, and various other ailments.

**Aucklandia Root** (*Mu Xiang*)
**Musk** (*She Xiang*) <note>The OCR uses "射" as a variant for "麝" (musk), a potent aromatic used to open the "orifices" of the body.</note>
**Rhinoceros Horn** (*Xi Jiao*) <note>Historically used in Chinese medicine to cool the blood and detoxify the heart; it is now an endangered and prohibited substance.</note>

**Chebulic Myrobalan**, **Cinnabar**, **White Atractylodes**, **Long Pepper** <gloss>one *liang* each of the above</gloss>

Grind the above ingredients into a fine powder and mix with refined honey to form a paste. Pound the mixture one thousand times and form into pills the size of a paulownia seed. Administer four pills dissolved in freshly drawn water. For the elderly and children, one pill is sufficient. Place one pill the size of a pellet in a crimson sachet and hang it upon the clothing; this is miraculous for warding off evil toxins and mountain spirits <note>Ancient medicine often combined internal ingestion with external "amulet" use to protect against environmental pathogens or malevolent spirits.</note>. When compounding this medicine, avoid the presence of chickens, dogs, and women <note>These "avoidances" (*ji*) reflect medieval ritual purity standards intended to ensure the spiritual efficacy of the medicine.</note>.

## Bright Moon Pill for treating Transmitted Corpse Consumption
<note>Transmitted Corpse Consumption (original: *chuanshi lao*) refers to a severe, infectious wasting disease similar to advanced tuberculosis, believed to be passed from one victim to the next by "corpse-worms" or spirits.</note>

**Realgar** <gloss>half a *liang*</gloss>
**Rabbit Droppings** <note>original: "tu fen." In the Chinese pharmacopeia, these are poetically called "Bright Moon Sand," which gives this pill its name.</note> two *liang*
**Calomel** one *liang* <note>Calomel (*qingfen*) is mercurous chloride, a potent but toxic substance used here for its perceived ability to kill parasites and "worms."</note>
**Costus Root** <gloss>half a *liang*</gloss>
**Human Calvarium** <gloss>one *liang*, charred</gloss> <note>The use of human skull (*tianlinggai*) in medieval medicine was based on the "sympathetic" theory that it could suppress the malevolent spirits or "ghosts" responsible for terminal wasting diseases.</note>
**Softshell Turtle Shell** <gloss>one piece; if large, remove the fringe</gloss> <gloss>charred with vinegar until soft</gloss> scorched yellow. <note>Turtle shell was traditionally used to "anchor" the spirit and nourish the *yin* in patients suffering from chronic fever and consumption.</note>

<keywords>Benzoin Pill, Bright Moon Pill, Transmitted Corpse Consumption, Realgar, Calomel, Human Calvarium, Ritual Purity, Tuberculosis, Medieval Pharmacy</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>Continuing from page thirty, providing the preparation, diagnostic test, and administration instructions for the "Bright Moon Pill."</meta>

Grind the aforementioned ingredients <note>referring to the Realgar, Rabbit Droppings, Calomel, and Costus Root listed on the previous page</note> into a fine powder. Take one large *sheng* <note>a volume measure, roughly 200ml</note> of pure wine and one *liang* <note>approximately 37 grams</note> of **Rhubarb** and simmer them together until they thicken into a paste. Incorporate the powdered medicine into this paste to form pills the size of a crossbow pellet, and coat them with **Cinnabar** <note>original: *zhusha*; mercuric sulfide, used here for its presumed power to stabilize the spirit and repel "corpse-ghosts."</note>.

This formula is for **Transmitted Corpse Consumption** <note>original: *chuanshi lao*; an infectious, wasting disease like tuberculosis, believed to be caused by supernatural "worms" or pathogens passing from the deceased to the living.</note>, characterized by emaciation, a sallow yellow complexion, vomiting of blood, and persistent, irregular coughing.

### The Diagnostic Smoke Test
First, burn **Benzoin** <note>original: *anxixiang*; a fragrant resin from the *Styrax* tree used to clear blockages and repel evil.</note> and allow the smoke to rise. Have the patient inhale it. If they do not cough, the illness is not Transmitted Corpse Consumption, and this medicine should not be used. If, upon inhaling the smoke, the patient begins to cough uncontrollably, then it is indeed the "Transmitted Corpse" disease, and this medicine is appropriate.

### Administration
During the start of the fifth watch <note>the period between 3:00 AM and 5:00 AM, often considered a potent time for spiritual and physical transition</note>, and without letting anyone else know, take one cup containing a mixture of **boy’s urine** <note>original: *tongzi xiaobian*; a common "cooling" agent in medieval Chinese medicine used to guide medicine and clear heat</note> and pure wine. Dissolve one pill into the liquid and administer it to the patient.

After the time it would take a person to walk twenty *li* <note>roughly 10 kilometers, or about two hours</note>, the patient will vomit up "worms." Their appearance varies: some look like **lamp wicks**, slender and about an inch long; others look like **rotten plums**; and still others may resemble **toads**. If the medicine is not immediately effective, repeat the dose the following day, continuing until a response is observed.

This treatment should only be used in the early stages of the disease, while the patient’s **blood and qi** <note>the fundamental fluids and vital energy of the body</note> are not yet exhausted and their **vital spirit** <note>original: *jingshen*</note> has not yet been deranged by the illness.

<summary>This page details the preparation of the Bright Moon Pill, a specific smoke-inhalation test to confirm a diagnosis of "Transmitted Corpse Consumption," and the ritualized method for administering the cure to expel disease-causing "worms."</summary>
<keywords>Bright Moon Pill, Transmitted Corpse Consumption, tuberculosis, diagnostic test, Benzoin, boy's urine, medical ritual, Hua Tuo, parasites</keywords>

# Rehmannia Decoction for Resolving Exhaustion, Regenerating Muscle, Improving Appetite, Invigorating the Blood, and Nourishing the *Qi*

**Juice of Raw Rehmannia**: Five *sheng* <note>original: *shengdi huang*; a volume measure, approximately 1 liter in this context. Rehmannia is known as "Earth Yellow," a vital root for cooling the blood and nourishing the body's core fluids.</note>
**Juice of Raw Apricot Kernels**: One *sheng* <note>original: *xingren*; often referred to as bitter almonds, used here to descend lung *qi* and moisten the system.</note>
**Juice of Mint**: One *sheng*
**Juice of Raw Lotus Root**: One *sheng* <note>used to clear heat and stop bleeding.</note>
**Sugarcane Juice**: One *sheng*
**Medicinal Wine**: Two *sheng* <note>original: *fajiu*; wine prepared according to specific pharmaceutical standards to enhance the circulation of the other herbs.</note>
**White Honey**: Four *liang* <note>a weight measure, approximately 150 grams.</note>
**Juice of Raw Ginger**: One *sheng*

The ingredients listed above should be placed together in a silver or stone vessel <note>Avoiding iron or copper is crucial in medieval pharmacy, as these metals were believed to react poorly with the medicinal properties of Rehmannia and other delicate herbs.</note> and simmered over a slow fire until they thicken into a medicinal paste. Once the paste is formed, incorporate the following medicines:

**Bupleurum**: Four *liang* <gloss>remove the reed heads <note>the woody top of the root</note> and roast</gloss>
**Costus Root**: Four *liang* <note>original: *muxiang*; an aromatic root used to regulate the flow of *qi* and relieve pain.</note>
**Ginseng**: Two *liang*
**White Poria**: Two *liang* <note>original: *baifukuling*; a medicinal fungus that grows on pine roots, used to drain dampness and calm the spirit.</note>
**Chinese Yam**: Two *liang*
**Arborvitae Seed**: Two *liang* <note>original: *baiziren*; used to nourish the heart and soothe nerves.</note>
**Polygala**: Two *liang* <gloss>remove the core <note>the inner woody pith, which was thought to cause irritation to the throat</note></gloss>
**White Atractylodes**: Two *liang* <note>original: *baizhu*; a key herb for strengthening the spleen and boosting energy.</note>
**Balloon Flower Root**: Two *liang* <note>original: *jiegeng*; used to clear the chest and direct the effects of other herbs to the upper body.</note>
**Bitter Orange**: Two *liang* <gloss>stir-fry with wheat bran</gloss>
**Largeleaf Gentian**: Three *liang* <gloss>remove the reed heads</gloss>
**Musk**: Two *qian* <gloss>grind separately</gloss> <note>original: *shexiang*; a highly aromatic substance from the musk deer, used in small amounts as a powerful stimulant to the senses and circulation. One *qian* is approximately 3.7 grams.</note>

**Prepared Rehmannia**: Four *liang* <note>original: *shudi huang*; unlike the raw juice used previously, this is Rehmannia root that has been steamed and dried, making it a powerful tonic for the kidneys and "essence."</note>

The ingredient above should be ground into a fine powder and incorporated into the medicinal paste prepared previously. Place the mixture into a mortar and pound it between two thousand and three thousand times <note>intensive pounding was a standard pharmaceutical practice to ensure the ingredients were perfectly homogenized and the pills would be shelf-stable.</note>. Form the mixture into pills the size of a paulownia seed <note>approximately 6 to 9 millimeters; a standard measurement in historical Chinese pharmacy.</note>. For each dose, take twenty pills with a decoction of licorice root. Administer after meals, three times per day. Cease the medication once the patient has reached a state of comfort and recovery.

### Central Decoction for Rising Vapor
<note>"Rising Vapor" or "Steaming" usually refers to "Steaming Bone Disorder," a traditional description of the cyclic, internal fever associated with chronic wasting diseases like tuberculosis.</note>

The ingredient above should be coarsely ground <term>original: *fuju*; a pharmaceutical term for chopping herbs into small, bean-sized pieces to facilitate extraction during boiling.</term> and simmered with two *dou* <note>approximately 4 liters</note> of pure wine until it thickens into a medicinal paste. Every night, take a portion the size of a marble <note>original: *danzi da*; literally "the size of a crossbow pellet."</note>, dissolve it in good wine, and swallow. Continue until a sweat is produced <note>sweating was viewed as a sign that the internal heat or pathogen was being released through the skin.</note>. Following this, the patient should still take the "Tonic Pill for Shooting the Navel."

### Tonic Pill for Shooting the Navel

## Spirit Aconite Pill
### To treat all cold-type illnesses, pain, numbness, and Wind-Qi <note>In traditional medicine, "Wind-Qi" refers to external pathogens that invade the body's channels, often causing sudden onset symptoms like spasms or wandering pain.</note>.

## The Realized Man Zuo Ci’s <gloss>also known as Shanyang [Good Cultivation]</gloss> Thousand-Gold Rehmannia Decoction
<note>Zuo Ci was a legendary Daoist "Realized Man" or "Immortal" from the late Han dynasty, famous for his magical feats. "Thousand-Gold" is a common term in historical Chinese medicine to denote a formula of immense value.</note>

**Fresh Rehmannia** <term>Sheng Dihuang</term> <note>original: *Sheng Dihuang*; the raw root of the Chinese foxglove, known for its ability to "cool the blood" and nourish the body's internal fluids.</note>: One *cheng* <term>cheng</term> <note>A unit of weight roughly equivalent to 15 pounds or 7.5 kilograms in this period.</note>. <gloss>Extract the juice and simmer it in a stone vessel until it thickens into a paste. Incorporate dried Rehmannia powder into the mixture—judging the amount needed based on the firmness of the paste—and pound it thoroughly.</gloss>

For the above: form into pills the size of a **tung tree seed**. Take twenty pills per dose on an empty stomach. Long-term consumption severs worldly desires and allows one to become a divine immortal who never dies.

## Formula for Removing Accumulations and Clusters
<note>"Accumulations and clusters" (*jiju*) is a traditional diagnostic category referring to abdominal masses, tumors, or severe digestive stagnations.</note>

**Calomel** <term>Qingfen</term> <note>original: *qingfen*; mercurous chloride, used historically as a powerful purgative.</note>, **Powdered Frost** <term>Fenshuang</term> <note>original: *fenshuang*; a highly refined mercuric sublimate.</note>, and **Cinnabar** <term>Zhusha</term> <note>original: *zhusha*; mercuric sulfide, a heavy mineral used in Daoist alchemy and medicine.</note>: <gloss>Half an ounce of each.</gloss>
**Croton Seed Frost** <term>Badou Shuang</term> <note>original: *badou shuang*; the pressed, defatted powder of the *Croton tiglium* seed, an extremely potent and toxic purgative used to "break up" internal blockages.</note>: <gloss>Two and a half mace <term>qian</term>.</gloss>

For the above: grind the ingredients together until uniform. Refine honey to make a medicinal paste and roll into pills the size of a **hemp seed**. Take three [pills] with ginger broth...

# Principles for Assessing Deficiency and Excess with Additions and Subtractions
<note>In traditional Chinese medicine, "Deficiency" (xu) refers to a lack of vital energy or fluids, while "Excess" (shi) refers to the presence of a pathogen or stagnation. "Addition and Subtraction" refers to the clinical practice of modifying a base formula to suit a patient's specific symptoms.</note>

## Formula for Treating Chronic Abdominal Masses
<note>The term "zheng jia" refers to internal masses; "zheng" are fixed, hard masses often associated with blood stasis, while "jia" are movable or intermittent masses associated with qi stagnation.</note>

**Rhubarb** <term>Dahuang</term> <note>original: *Dahuang*; a powerful medicinal root used to purge heat and break up blood stasis.</note> <gloss>wrapped in wet paper and roasted in embers</gloss>, **Bur-reed** <term>Sanleng</term> <note>original: *Sanleng*; used to vigorously break up accumulations and move the qi.</note> <gloss>wrapped in wet paper, roasted, and sliced while hot</gloss>, **Borax** <term>Pengsha</term> <gloss>ground into a fine powder</gloss>
<column-break/>
**Dried Lacquer** <term>Ganqi</term> <note>original: *Ganqi*; processed sap of the lacquer tree, used to break up stubborn internal "clots" or "clumps."</note> <gloss>stir-fry until the smoke is completely dissipated</gloss>, **Croton Seed** <term>Badou</term> <note>original: *Badou*; an extremely potent and toxic purgative; removing the oil reduces its intensity.</note> <gloss>remove the skin and press out the oil</gloss>
<column-break/>
Take one **tael** <term>liang</term> <note>approximately 40 grams</note> of each ingredient listed above and grind them into a fine powder. Use one **liter** <term>sheng</term> <note>approximately 200 milliliters</note> of vinegar to simmer the mixture into a thick paste, then incorporate the following medicines:
<column-break/>
**Costus Root** <term>Muxiang</term>, **Clove** <term>Dingxiang</term>, **Bitter Orange** <term>Zhishi</term> <gloss>stir-fry with wheat bran and remove the inner pith</gloss>, **Cinnamon Heart** <term>Guixin</term> <note>the inner, most aromatic part of the cinnamon bark.</note> <gloss>one tael of each</gloss>
<column-break/>
For the ingredients listed above: grind them into a fine powder and combine with the previously prepared vinegar paste to form a dough. Pound the mixture one thousand times with a mortar and pestle, then form into pills the size of a **radish seed**.
<column-break/>
Take three to five pills per dose with a large drink after meals.
<column-break/>
**Asafoetida Pills for Opening the Flow of Qi** <note>Asafoetida (*Awei*) is a pungent resin used to move stagnant energy and treat digestive blockages.</note>: This formula primarily treats all types of blocked **qi** <term>qi</term> <note>the body's vital energy or functional force</note>, as well as stomach and back pain, internal knots and obstructions, and feelings of oppressive agitation or mental confusion.

## Formula for Treating Corpse-like Syncope and Sudden Pain
<note>"Corpse-like syncope" (*shi jue*) refers to a sudden loss of consciousness where the patient appears dead but retains a faint pulse. "Sudden pain" (*zu tong*) refers to acute, agonizing spasms in the torso.</note>

**Corpse-like syncope** is a condition where a person suddenly appears as if in a drunken stupor, their limbs become cold, and they are no longer aware of human affairs. **Sudden pain** refers to pain between the heart and the abdomen, or beneath the left and right flanks, that is so intense it is unbearable. This is what the common folk call "**ghost arrows**" <note>original: *guijian*; a traditional folk term for sudden, stabbing pains, which were historically attributed to invisible spiritual strikes.</note>.

**Realgar** <term>Xionghuang</term> <note>original: *Xionghuang*; an arsenic-based mineral used in ancient medicine to "expel evil" and treat sudden, violent illnesses.</note> <gloss>two taels, ground into a fine powder</gloss>

**Cinnabar** <term>Zhusha</term> <gloss>two taels, ground into a fine powder</gloss>

<summary>This page concludes the recipe for an Asafoetida pill for internal masses and introduces a diagnostic description and emergency formula for "corpse-like syncope" and the sudden, sharp pains known as "ghost arrows."</summary>
<keywords>Asafoetida, Agarwood, Cinnabar, Corpse-like syncope (shi jue), Ghost arrows (guijian), Realgar, emergency medicine, folk terminology</keywords>

<margin>For treating heart pain that is unbearable</margin>

Grind the two ingredients mentioned above <note>Continuing from the previous page: likely referring to specific resins or herbs meant to be combined with the garlic base</note> together until they are evenly mixed. Take one head of **garlic** <term>Dasuan</term>, wrap it in wet paper, and roast it in hot ashes. Remove the paper and pound the roasted garlic into a paste. Form into pills the size of a **cherry**. For each dose, dissolve one pill in **warm wine** and swallow.

**The Ghost-Crying Pill** <term>Guiku Dan</term> <note>A dramatic name suggesting a formula so powerful that even "ghosts"—metaphors for stubborn or hidden ailments—would be driven out in tears.</note> primarily treats various pains within the abdomen, the stagnation of **vital energy** <term>Qi</term> and **Blood**, and cases where food and drink remain undigested. It is indicated for blockages between the **Yin and Yang** <note>The complementary cosmic forces within the body</note> and instances where **Cold and Heat** <note>Opposing pathogenic factors</note> clash, causing pain. Use this prescription to master these conditions.

**Monkshood** <term>Chuanwu</term> <note>original: *Chuanwu*; the root of *Aconitum carmichaelii*, a potent warming herb used here in its raw form to pierce through heavy stagnation.</note> <gloss>fourteen pieces, raw</gloss>
**Cinnabar** <term>Zhusha</term> <note>original: *Zhusha*; mercury sulfide, used to settle the spirit and as a preservative coating.</note> <gloss>one tael</gloss> <note>approximately 37.5 grams</note>
**Frankincense** <term>Ruxiang</term> <note>original: *Ruxiang*; the resin of the *Boswellia* tree, used to move blood and alleviate pain.</note> <gloss>one fen</gloss> <note>approximately 0.3 grams</note>

Grind the ingredients above into a fine powder. Take one cup of **vinegar** and one tael of **Flying Squirrel Droppings** <term>Wulingzhi</term> <note>original: *Wulingzhi*; literally "Five Spirits' Fat," the feces of the complex-toothed flying squirrel, used to disperse blood stasis.</note> in powdered form. Boil these together into a medicinal paste. Mix this paste with the herbal powders and form into pills the size of a **tung tree seed**. Use **Cinnabar** as a coating. Take seven pills per dose. Men should take them with **warm wine**, and women should take them with **vinegar soup**.

<note>This text emphasizes the ritual and gendered nature of administration (wine for men, vinegar for women), common in medieval medicine to harmonize with the perceived differences in "hot" or "cold" constitutions.</note>

### Formula for Expelling Long Worms and Treating Heart Pain
<note>In ancient Chinese medicine, "Heart Pain" often refers to severe pain in the epigastric region (upper stomach) as well as the chest. "Long Worms" typically refers to roundworms (ascaris).</note>

**Jujubes** <term>Dazao</term> <gloss>twenty-one pieces, pitted</gloss>
**Green Vitriol** <term>Lufan</term> <note>original: *Lufan*; ferrous sulfate. Historically used in potent doses to kill parasites, though it is quite harsh on the stomach.</note> <gloss>one tael, divided into twenty-one pieces</gloss>
Heat the vitriol until it glows red, then place one piece inside each jujube.

**Thunder Balls** <term>Leiwan</term> <note>original: *Leiwan*; a medicinal fungus (*Omphalia lapidescens*) renowned for its ability to dissolve parasites.</note> <gloss>seven pieces</gloss>
**Calomel** <term>Qingfen</term> <note>original: *Qingfen*; a mercury-based compound used as a purgative and parasiticide.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>
**Aucklandia** <term>Muxiang</term> <gloss>one mace</gloss>
**Cloves** <term>Dingxiang</term> <gloss>one mace</gloss>
**Mercury** <term>Shuiyin</term> <gloss>half a tael</gloss> combined with **Lead** <term>Qian</term> <gloss>half a tael</gloss>, melted together to form an amalgam of granules.

Grind the dry ingredients above into a powder. Take two taels of **beef** and one tael of **axle grease** <note>original: *Chezhi*; the heavy grease from a cart's axle. In ancient toxicology, heavy oils or fats were believed to help "trap" or "smother" parasites while protecting the stomach lining from the harsh mineral ingredients.</note>. Mince the beef and grease together until they form a fine pulp.

Pour one **liter of rice vinegar** into a pot and boil the meat until it reduces into a thick medicinal paste. Incorporate the powdered medicines and simmer the mixture together until it reaches the correct consistency—neither too hard nor too soft. Place the mixture into a **mortar** and pound it between two thousand and three thousand times with a **pestle**.

Form the mixture into pills the size of a **wild jujube seed**. When shaping each pill, first take a single **scarlet thread**... <note>The text ends here; the thread was likely used to string the pills together or for a specific ritualistic application mentioned on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page details two prescriptions: a simple powder for immediate pain relief using Aucklandia and Zedoary, and a complex, highly potent alchemical pill containing mercury, lead, and green vitriol designed to expel intestinal parasites and treat severe abdominal pain.</summary>
<keywords>Aucklandia, Zedoary, parasites, mercury, lead, jujubes, green vitriol, heart pain, Master Hua's Central Treasury</keywords>

Within the medicine, leave approximately two feet of **silk string**. In cases of **Long Worms** <term>Changchong</term> <note>original: *changchong*; these are generally identified as roundworms (*Ascaris lumbricoides*), which were a common focus of medieval medicine.</note>, at the start of the **fifth watch** <note>The pre-dawn period, roughly 3:00 AM to 5:00 AM, often considered the most effective time for expelling parasites.</note>, swallow one pill with **oily rice water**. Keep the end of the string outside the mouth; do not allow it to be swallowed completely. Wait a short while; when pain is felt in the heart <note>The "heart" here refers to the epigastrium or upper stomach area.</note> and the string moves, immediately pull the string so that the medicine and the worm are extracted together. 

If the pain from **Heart Qi** <term>Xinqi</term> is unbearable, dissolve one pill in **hot vinegar soup** and take it; the pain will stop immediately.
